## M: market umbrella–moatic

### market umbrella
 
 <i>n.</i> a type of sun umbrella (square/octagonal) doubtless originally used to shade produce at street markets but in NZ more commonly used on patios, decks, etc. [Jim writes: The term is not in any dictionary I have seen and is not self-defining. It is likely to be international English in the extended as well as the original sense, but may be worth checking.]

### marketroid
 
 <i>n.</i> a New Right ideologist

>  This no more validates the peculiar fundamentalist cult developed around the theory in Chicago in the 1960s and imported to New Zealand by Roger Douglas and his marketroids, than Mohammed's authorship of the Koran validates the Taliban. <i>(Dec 30, 2001,Sunday Star Times,A6</i>)

### marking
 
 <i>n.</i> castration and ?docking of stock, possibly f. <i>earmarking</i> (See [also lamb-marking](l-lay.md#lamb-marking))

>  <b>PERRY</b> <i>Lamb Marking</i>. - The castration and docking of lambs is best carried out when the lambs are from four to six weeks old; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 94 <i>Lamb Marking.</i> ... [95] Once the operation of earmarking, tailing, and castrating is commenced, ... <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,79</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The third man would do the `marking'. <i>(1949,High Country Days,99</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Few men lamb marking or branding calves were his equal, ... <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,112</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Calf marking is another major job that is done is in Bush Gully yards. ... I was interested to find that all the calves - marked in March and fairly sizable - are done in the bail ... . The marking cradle was tried but did not find favour. <i>(1973,Big Country,65,1977 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) docking of calves' tails, ear-marking and castration.

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> It was straight into calf marking for him. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,57</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [orig. S I.] docking of lambs' tails, ear-marking and castration.

>  <b>WALLACE</b> Lamb-tailing and marking were on at this time - end of June. <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,ii 43</i>)

>  Lamb marking consists of three different operations: the placing of a distinguishing mark in one or both ears, the amputation of tails, and the castration of male lambs. <i>(Sep 1, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,197</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> Docking is Northspeak for marking, but whatever it is you have to muster the sheep, yard them and do unspeakable things like dagging, drenching, earmarking, tail-cutting and a considerable amount of castration. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,169</i>)

### marking count
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a tally or total number of lambs on a property at docking time [considered as a lambing percentage.]

>  <b>MOWAT</b> We are about 30 lambs short of the marking count. <i>(Jan 31, 1917,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

### marking cradle
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Calf marking is another major job that is done is in Bush Gully yards. ... I was interested to find that all the calves - marked in March and fairly sizable - are done in the bail ... . The marking cradle was tried but did not find favour. <i>(1973,Big Country,65,1977 ed.</i>)

### marking muster
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a muster to earmark and castrate calves.

>  <i>(Oct 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,349/3</i>) [NOTES: also Oct 356/3, May 461/1]

>  <b>MACNICOL</b> A few calves miss the marking muster, grow up and have calves too. <i>(1965,Beyond the Skippers Road,27</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [S. I.] a lamb docking muster.

>  After the marking muster musterers proceed to the high country and muster wethers. <i>(Oct 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,348</i>)

### marking time
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [S.I.] docking or tailing time.

>  <b>KERR</b> A Taylor once rode into the Burwood yards at marking time on a young pacer, barely broken, wild dogs everywhere, no socks under his boots. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,69</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> The 5000 breeding ewes had only produced 2500 lambs by marking time and 1300 at weaning. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,52</i>)

### marking yard
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a docking yard.

>  <b>KERR</b> In case you think this is nothing, they had to shear them on a drop sheet near the marking yards with blades. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,90</i>)

### Marlborough
 
 <i>adj.</i> (See [harebell](h-hea.md#harebell),[lilac](../L#lilac))

### Marlborough daisy
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Pachystegia</i> genus

>  In its stony native habitat the Marlborough Daisy is often straggly and less prepossessing, which may explain why it has been notably lacking from private gardens despite their eye appeal and the fact that they were discovered way back in 1853. Its discoverer first classed it with the Olearia genus but it was later given its own genus, <i>Pachystegia</i>, a family which occurs naturally only in New Zealand. <i>(Jun 1, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,9</i>)

### Marlborough green gecko
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a small gecko of uniform olive to bright green colouring, found in Marlborough and nearby islands. [<i>Naultinus manukanus</i> aka Manuka gecko] (See [Manuka gecko](../M#manuka-gecko))

>  [Caption] A Marlborough green gecko <i>(Jul 2004,Conservation Action,10</i>)

>  Shrublands are important habitats for many lizards. The Marlborough green gecko (<i>Naultinus manukanus</i>) is found in regenerating manuka shrublands and coastal scrub around the Marlborough Sounds. <i>(Mar 1, 2009,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.teara.govt.nz/en/shrublands/6/2]

>  The project is targeting the local species like the Forest and the Marlborough Green Gecko with potential for species like the giant Snail and the giant Weta. <i>(2009,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.conservationmarlborough.co.nz/conservation_projects.html]

### Marlborough lilac
 
 <i>n.</i> Hebe hulkeana, a hebe with lilac-coloured flowers [also Awatere lilac, ca1953 (R. Mason) i.e. from the Awatere district, Marlborough, where it is abundant (see LBM 12/1953)]

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>HEBE HULKEANA </i>. The `Marlborough lilac' grows on limestone bluffs from the Conway River northwards as a bush half to one metre tall ... and flowers range from pinkish lavender to white. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,174</i>)

### Marlborough mini gecko
 
 <i>n.</i> New Zealand's smallest gecko, resident in the mountains of the Rangitata Gorge. [<i>Hoplodactylus "Marlborough mini"</i> aka pygmy gecko.]

>  A variety of insects and three species of lizard (spotted and common skink and Marlborough mini gecko), were found in a range of sites. <i>(Feb 2008,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.marlborough.govt.nz/content/docs/environmental/Newsletter-SNA-February2008-NEa.pdf]

>  The common skink, Marlborough mini gecko and Southern Alps gecko have all been recorded within the licence area. <i>(Nov 2008,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.linz.govt.nz/docs/crownproperty/high-country-leases/leaselist/mt-gladstone-prelim-prop.pdf]

>  Marlborough mini (Hoplodactylus "Marlborough mini") - only recently identified, and found between Houghton Bay and Sinclair Head. <i>(2009,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.wellington.govt.nz/services/gardens/pdfs/boautumn09.pdf]

### Marlborough rock daisy
 
 <i>n.</i> a native plant, <i>Pachystegia insignis</i> which grows naturally in dry and coastal areas

>  Pink tree broom, Marlborough rock daisy and weeping broom are native to the park and endemic to south Marlborough. <i>(Aug 2008,FMC - (Federated Mountain Clubs of NZ) Bulletin,30</i>)

>  [Caption] A distinctive shrub, the Marlborough rock daisy, Pachystegia insignis, sports grey drumstick buds for months ands then opens its shaggy white flowers in early summer. For years I've been promising myself some Marlborough rock daisies and finally I've done it and have two babies of this distinctive and unusual native shrub growing happily in pots in the sun. <i>(Apr 27, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,9</i>)

>  Where it's dry year-round, Watson has plugged the gaps.. with clumps of grey tussock, broom and Marlborough rock daises (Pachystegia insignis). "The Marlborough rock daisies aren't that easy to grow here.. because they need perfect drainage." <i>(Oct 20, 2002,Sunday Star Times,F7</i>)

>  The pick of the native flowering plants seen in November were the Pachystegias, or Marlborough Rock Daisies. <i>(Feb 1990,NZ Gardener,55</i>)

>  They advise visitors to look out for the native pink broom, rare native lichen or the giant Marlborough daisy clinging to rumpled rock. <i>(Nov 1999,North & South,32</i>)

>  I was delighted to find a good example of a Marlborough rock daisy during my visit. <i>(Feb 21, 1999,Sunday Star Times,D8</i>)

>  The Marlborough Rock Daisy, Pachystegia insignis, is a striking small shrub which deserves a much wider following among gardeners than it has at present <i>(Jan 1990,NZ Gardener,19</i>)

>  A low-growing spreading shrub between 30 centimetres and 90 cm tall, Marlborough rock daisies are great for the home garden in those more exposed, dry areas with good drainage ... <i>(Aug 18, 2007,Dominion Post Indulgence,17</i>)

### Marlborough Sounds shag
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment)

>  <i>King Shag Leucocarbo carunculatus<i> (GMELIN, 1789) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Carunculated shag, Marlborough Sounds shag, rough-faced shag, Cook Strait cormorant</i>. ... The only shag with pink feet in the Cook Strait and Marlborough Sounds area. Prominent caruncles at base of bill. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,118</i>)

### Marlburian
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McINTOSH</b> Those words, `giving a hand', and `doing a turn', recurred so frequently that they became almost Marlburian. <i>(1940,Marlborough,5</i>)

### marlin
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [species name] marlin .. [scientific name] -- [other name(s)] Unspecified - see also black marlin, blue marlin, shortbill spearfish, striped marlin, sailfish <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

### marlin-spike
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> STRIPED MARLIN <i>Makaira mitsukurii</i> (Jordan and Snyder). The striped marlin or marlin-spike has a wide distribution throughout the warm waters of the Pacific. It visits northern coasts of New Zealand from December to April, ... <i>(1940,Fishes NZ,73</i>)

### marmageddon
 
 <i>n.</i> (Cuisine, humorous) the Marmite famine following earthquake damage to the Sanitarium manufacturing building in Christchurch in 2011, mirroring a state of national panic and foreboding. [ORIGIN: blend]

>  Ms Martin said the first thing she did when Marmageddon dawned on Monday was jump in the car and drive to four neighbourhood supermarkets, all of which were out of stock. <i>(Mar 22, 2012,NZ Herald,A3</i>)

>  It's been dubbed Marmageddon and the Marmapocalypse ... but Marmite-maker Sanitarium insists it didn't plan the shortage crisis. <i>(Mar 21, 2012,NZ Herald,A3</i>)

>  Marmageddon, it turns out, hasn't hit the Chatham Islands yet. <i>(Dec 4, 2012,Dominion Post,A17</i>)

### marmalade
 
 <i>n.</i> mishaps on a production line

>  They [sc. workers on a toothpaste production line] also joked about "marmalades" - mishaps where the odd tube would burst its contents all over the machinery. <i>(Dec 12, 2002,Dominion Post,A5</i>)

### marnice
 
 <i>n.</i> embarrassment

>  <i>what a marnice (?marnus ?maa-ness)</i>: embarrassment <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

### maro
 
 <i>n.</i> a traditional Maori dress, a sort of kilt or apron worn by males and females. [Maori] a sort of <i>kilt</i> or <i>apron</i> worn by males and females. (so Williams, 183) Maori, <i>maro</i>

>  <b>STEVENSON</b> While I believe it to be a fragment of a maro, I admit that the identification is well open to question; indeed, without the knowledge of the Moriori maro it would have been rash to speculate on its original form. <i>(1947,Maori and Pakeha in North Otago,144</i>)

>  <i>maro</i>, finely-plaited apron: a fathom is ma[a]ro[o], but that word has never come into common use. (Definition in O.E.D. incomplete). <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/145</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>HULME</b> `Just as well I didn't make you fellas get into maro and piupiu,' I joke. <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,92</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>maro</i>: kind of kilt or apron worn by males or females. Can be made of various materials. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,192</i>)

### marooning party
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FORSTER</b> It may be curious to know the nature of our marooning parties, as the seamen called them. 1824 Southey 1856 Olmstead Slave States <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,165</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> We landed about 2, inorder [sic] to dress our dinner: ... We left the beach at five & went up this Arm, till it was quite dark: & then took up our Quarters for the Night, but as it is highly [271] improbable, for people that have not been at Sea & especially that have not frequented uncultivated Countries to form an Idea of the manner of living on a <i>marooning</i> party, to form an adquate Idea of these parties, we intend to give one here in this place. The first thing is to look out for a good place with a gentle beach with wood & a brook close to it. After landing everybody is busy to get dry wood, a necessary article in so wet a Country as New-Zeeland. [There follows a full description: roast food, grog, jollity and storytelling, etc] <i>(May 7, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/270</i>)

### maroro
 
 <i>n.</i> a flying fish

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Of the Fam. Esocidoe may be noticed the <i>Maroro</i>, or flying fish (<i>exocetus exulans</i>). This singular fish in New Zealand attains the length of eighteen inches. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,624</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> <i>Exocaetus speculiga</i> C. and V. Flying-fish, or Maroro <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,305</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Evolantia microptera</i> (Cuv. & Val.). FLYING-FISH; MARORO. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,21,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> FLYINGFISH ... <i>Other names: Cypselurus lineatus; </i>maroro (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,207</i>)

### marowai
 
 <i>n.</i> protective leg-covering

>  We planned to make a pair of marowai (<i>Maro</i> = covering, <i>wai</i> = leg) - leggings for protection against the spines of matagouri and wild Spaniard. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/40</i>)

### marriage celebrant
 
 <i>n.</i> a person authorised by law to solemnise a marriage. [Jim Cameron writes: Before 1976 these persons were called <i>officiating ministers</i>. (Marriage Act 1955 as amended). The use of the word "<i>solemnise</i>" is perhaps of interest; the legislation uses it also in relation to marriages by Registrars. It is not the usual secular term.]

### married
 
 <i>n.</i> a type of bread-loaf

>  <b>TATE</b> ... two thousand steaming loaves ... condensed and marrieds, twist and vienna, poppy and plait. <i>(1933,Doughman, The,12</i>)

### married couple
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sp. use; a rural labour unit] married employees [where both wife and husband are involved in either shepherding, tractor-driving, milking, cooking, housekeeping or gardening. ]

>  What we had for dramatic interest were a series of Married Couples and the Men on the Station. <i>(Jan 13, 1950,Listener (New Zealand Listener),4</i>)

>  A married couple manages the dairy farm with some outside help. <i>(Apr 7, 1987,NZ Journal of Agriculture</i>)

>  Herd Manager/Married Couple wanted. [couple] <i>(Feb 9, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,23</i>)

>  Wanted, by J D Canning, at Oakbourne, Wallingford, a Married Couple; the Woman to cook, the Man to milk and make himself generally useful. <i>(Feb 27, 1872,Hawke's Bay Herald,1</i>)

>  Wanted, a Married Couple, the man will be required to plough, the woman to milk and wash. Apply to H W P Smith, Aorangi. <i>(Apr 22, 1872,Hawke's Bay Herald,1</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> All told they keep a permanent staff of about ten including a married couple in the cookhouse at the homestead; another married man is stationed at an outstation over by the Rangitikei bridge on the Moawhango-Taihape road. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,145</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> married couple: man and wife, both of whom are employees. At one time they may have been cook and groom, or cook and gardener, or cook and cowman, or even perhaps a house domestic married to a shepherd. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,52</i>)

>  <b>HARGREAVES</b> There was a job going for a married couple on a sheep station. Mum did the cooking, washing and general home duties. Dad did the milking of the cows, making butter, cutting wood, killing sheep and helping with the washing. <i>(2000,To the Back and Beyond,24</i>)

>  .. they are supported in the day-to-day running of the station by four married couples and three single shepherds. <i>(Apr 12, 2001,NZ Farmer,38</i>)

### married man
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sp. use; a rural labour unit] a farm or station employee with married accommodation [ not requiring catering. ]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> All told they keep a permanent staff of about ten including a married couple in the cookhouse at the homestead; another married man is stationed at an outstation over by the Rangitikei bridge on the Moawhango-Taihape road. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,145</i>)

>  He employs a contract milker who in turn employs a married man. <i>(Jan 1977,NZ Dairy Exporter,09</i>)

>  Stuart employs a full-time married man.. <i>(1988,NZ Journal of Agriculture,Feb/Mar 20</i>)

>  He has had some part-time assistance from his married man during the peak of lambing ... <i>(Sep 8, 1961,NZ Farmer,18</i>)

>  How do Ted and the married man he employs manage to fit into sheepfarming and town supply dairying and still find life worth living? <i>(Dec 10, 1964,NZ Dairy Exporter,59</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> I'd fallen out with the married man on the place.. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,133</i>)

>  Mr Walsh at present employs a single man, but plans to get a married man soon. <i>(Jan 28, 1965,NZ Farmer,5</i>)

>  Excellent fattening and breeding property .. good married man's house. <i>(Sep 24, 1970,NZ Farmer,106</i>)

>  Mr McCallum employs three married men. <i>(Oct 1974,NZ Dairy Exporter,13</i>)

>  The farm is run by him and a married man. <i>(May 1981,NZ Dairy Exporter,5</i>)

>  He and his married man crutched and bellied the hoggets themselves in July ... <i>(Sep 1986,NZ Journal of Agriculture,08</i>)

### married shepherd
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shepherd provided with married accommodation and not requiring catering.

>  Married shepherd - Applications are invited for the above position on Waitangi Terrace Station. <i>(Feb 3, 2001,Dominion,63</i>)

### marrow
 
 <i>n.</i> penis [ca1935 Havelock Schoolboys. (? connection Maori mongamonga =(1) marrow (2) penis)]

### marrowbone country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering] rocky high altitude grazing land, too steep for horses.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> marrowbone country: differs from pigskin country as it is too steep for horses and has to be walked. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,52</i>)

>  It was time to take the maggot taxis up to the marrowbone country <i>(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion,C6</i>)

### Marseille tile
 
 <i>n.</i> a burnt-clay roofing tile, commonly used esp. on bungalows and State House. [Jim writes: Introduced to NZ about 1901 and imported until 1924, after which they were made locally. See Salmond <i>Old NZ Houses</i> p 122 etc. An account of the development of the Marseilles tile roof in Australia is in Robin Boyd <i>Australia's Home</i> p. 151-153. Despite its European name and the original provenance of the tile, this term is not in British dictionaries I have seen. NSOD has only <i>Marseille ware</i> - a type of pottery produced in Marseilles during the 17th and 18th centuries. DEH adds: researching family geneology, I encountered a ship from Marseilles via Genoa to Sydney or NZ in 1909 from Marseille with a cargo of tiles. Many Kelburn houses have Marseilles tiles]

>  <b>F12</b> The tower is topped off with a belvedere ("beautiful sight"), roofed in Australian-made Marseille tiles, which match the bungalow's roof. <i>(Feb 1, 2003,Dominion Post</i>)

>  <i>Tile, marseilles</i> - Clay tile similar in form to those originally imported from Marseilles in the early part of the century. Burnt clay tiles are not often used in New Zealand construction. <i>(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,116</i>)

>  Mrs Gibson had fallen in love with the cottage which, with its lavendar, Marseille tiles and bay window seemed reminiscent of writer Katherine Mansfield who spent summers in a bungalow a stone's throw away at the turn of the century. <i>(Feb 15, 2003,Dominion Post,F16</i>)

>  <b>SALMOND</b> [Caption] The orange Marseille tiles are peculiar to the bungalows of Australasia... <i>Ibid.</i> 122 When Marseille tiles were introduced to New Zealand in about 1901, roofs took on a new colour and texture. The vivid terracotta-orange contrasted strongly with the drab red-oxide paint of iron roofs and the grey of slate. <i>(1986,Old New Zealand,46</i>)

### marsh
 
 <i>adj.</i> as a distinguishing epithet in names of plants and birds see bedstraw, buttercup, cress, crake, cudweed; fern, (buckler); grass, foxtail; haloragis, mallow, marigold, pennywort, ragwort, rail, reed, ribbonwood, rush, samphire, speedwell, stachys, thistle

### marsh crake
 
 <i>n.</i> a small secretive swamp bird, Porzana pusilla affinis (also called koitareke). (See [also marsh rail](mar-moa.md#marsh-rail))

>  They include the endangered crested grebe,.. black swan and marsh crake... <i>(Aug 2002,Forest & Bird,305/30</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Present local authority plans ... could destroy the breeding grounds for ... the rarer bittern, godwit, white-faced heron, shag, pukeko, crake, knot, golden plover and greenshank. <i>(1985,G'day Country,77</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kotoreke</i>, n. <i>Porzana affinis, marsh-rail. = kareke. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,175</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kotoreke, ... Porzana pusilla affinis, marsh crake. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,150</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kotoreke, ... Porzana pusilla affinis, marsh crake. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,150</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> MARSH CRAKE <i>Porzana pusilla Other name</i>: Koitareke. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,106</i>)

>  <i>135. Porzana pusilla affinis</i> (Gray, 1846) <i>Marsh Crake (Koitareke) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,42,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Marsh Crake Porzana pusilla affinis</i> (GRAY, 1846) ... OTHER NAME: <i>Koitareke</i>. ... The marsh crake, a secretive swampland bird, is heard rather than seen. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,166</i>)

>  <b>Moon</b> Both the spotless and marsh crakes possess strong homing instincts... <i>(2002,Birds of New Zealand,63</i>)

### marsh fern
 
 <i>n.</i> Dryopteris thelypteris var.<i>squamulosa</i>. [JD notes: Now <i>Thelypteris confluens</i>] [Brownsey 1989 gives swamp fern for this.]

>  <b>POTTS</b> (And) <i>(1882,Out in the Open</i>)

### marsh marigold
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Caltha</i> sp. spg also <i>marygold </i>

>  <i>Caltha novae-zelandiae</i> New Zealand marsh-marigold <i>(1909,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Cockayne Report on a Botanical Survey of Stewart Island (And)]

### marsh rail
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. spotless crake](spa-sys.md#spotless-crake))

>  He forwarded it through Mr Lane, chemist, to Dr de Lautour, who pronounced it to be a specimen of the marsh rail, "ortygometra affinis" (Buller); native name, Koitarike. <i>(Jun 16, 1896,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ORTYGOMETRA AFFINIS. (MARSH-RAIL/) ... <i>Native name.</i>- Koitareke. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/103</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kotoreke, n. Porzana affinis, marsh-rail. = kareke. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,175</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> In the wet, undrained lands round about the lake are to be found, the Swamp Rail and the Marsh Rail, both species quite rare, ... <i>(1927,Birds of New Zealand,77</i>)

>  MARSH-RAIL (Porzana affinis). - A shy little bird which frequents swampy situations, being very seldom seen. <i>(1928,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,9/188</i>) [NOTES: (Hope)]

### marsh ribbonwood
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Plagianthus divaricatus</i> [aka saltmarsh ribbonwood, swamp ribbonwood. <i>Plagianthus divaricatus</i>; incl 1x shore ribbonwood]

### maru
 
 <i>n.</i> a kanuka

Easily distinguished from [manuka] by its greater size, ... wood durable; much used for piles, house-blocks, posts and rails, &c. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,161</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a rush-like marsh plant common in the North Island. [Maori], also called Maori burr weed

>  <b>chee</b> <i>S[parganium] antipodum, Graebner ... Maru.</i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,744</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> ... mention should be made of the MAORI BURR REED or MARU (<i>Sparganium</i>), a common rush-like marsh plant in parts of the North Island with flat, strongly-keeled leaves and erect stems ... <i>(1961,Flora NZ,141</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a New Zealand-bred cultivar of phalaris Phalaris aquatica [ Harding grass.]

>  Pasture species are Grasslands Wana cocksfoot and Maru phalaris, and clover. <i>(Feb 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,20</i>)

>  Over summer, autumn and winter, maru was 34% more productive than ryegrass .. Kara cocksfoot may have an advantage sown as a mixture with either maru or roa. <i>(Sep 1985,NZ Dairy Exporter,30</i>)

>  Cows on the grassgrub-tolerant species, roa tall fescue and maru phalaris milked on for a further fortnight. <i>(1985,NZ Dairy Exporter,70</i>)

>  Maru - resistant to pests such as grass grub and black beetle. <i>(1995,Grasslands Range of Forage and Conservation Plants,22</i>)

### Mascot of HMS New Zealand, the
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>FRASER & McGIBBON</b> <i>MASCOT OF H.M.S. NEW ZEALAND, THE</i>: The Maori `Piu-piu,' or cloak of black and white strips of flax and feathers, and the carved greenstone amulet figure, ("Tiki") worn round the neck (similar to the old battle insignia of Maori chiefs) presented to the battleship <i>New Zealand</i>. They were given by an old Maori Chief in 1913, with the injunction that they must always be worn when the ship was in action, the chief prophesying that they would keep the ship from harm. Both "Piu-piu' and `Tiki' were worn at the Battle of Jutland by the Captain - to the great satisfaction of the crew, who, it is told, before the battle began took special measures to find out that the Captain was actually wearing the ship's mascot. On another occasion, when an action seemed imminent, the <i>New Zealand</i> mascot was also worn. On that occasion, word was specially passed round the decks among the men - `It's all right, he's got 'em on!' The <i>New Zealand</i> at Jutland escaped with only one shell hit, and had no casualties. Writing home after Jutland an officer said: "This ship bore a charmed life and suffered to the smallest extent though we were right in it the whole time.' <i>(1925,Soldier and Sailor Words,152</i>)

### masher
 
 <i>n.</i> a lady-kill(er), seems to come into fashion ca1906 (cf Truth No. 41), remain for a few years, and disappear.

>  <b>INGLIS</b> Mount Eden is of course the lion of Auckland ... nursemaids, babies, mashers, and maidens, and all the modern medley of tourists munch their apples, ... <i>(1887,Our NZ Cousins,17</i>)

>  At the same time the practice rendered it unsafe for any girl to wait abroad for her legitimate lover or any wife for her husband. She was almost certain to be accosted by some prowling `masher"- a word that was in much more common use then than it is now - [20 years before] and cases of condign punishment for such audacity, at the hands of lover or husband, were so frequent as hardly to excite comment. <i>(Jul 14, 1906,NZ Truth,56/5/4</i>)

>  In respect of his clothing he was an unmistakeable masher from his high-heeled boots to his stick-up collar. <i>(Jan 2, 1890,Otago Witness,4</i>)

>  K has found a masher at last. <i>(Jul 1, 1893,NZ Observer,22</i>)

>  K.C... says she went back to soothe some of the soft-headed Chelsea mashers. <i>(Jul 8, 1893,NZ Observer,21</i>)

>  Every other girl had her print skirt torn to ribbons by the spurs of the military mashers. <i>(Sep 17, 1904,NZ Observer,17</i>)

>  Our local masher seems very sweet on the little girl from town. <i>(Oct 8, 1904,NZ Observer,22</i>)

>  "Masher" Skeates' 'hundred yards' forced handicap against a stray heifer in Lower Queen-street called forth great applause from the spectators. <i>(Nov 12, 1904,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  In Cambridge, the "masher" of the party, the only one who could sport a white shirt and a collar, met some friends. <i>(Feb 12, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  At Dunedin recently they gave' a "masher" concert for a charitable purpose and.. a net balance of GBP21 8s 6d was handed over. The balance is supposed to have been spent in mashing. <i>(Dec 10, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  Tommy Packer was playing tip cat on the pavement, and he hit the cat with his stick, and tipped it, all muddy, straight into the masher's face. <i>(Jun 13, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  He was a new chum just out from England, and we took a deal of notice of him at once, because he was the first masher we ever saw. He wore a curly brimmed hat and had curly brown hair, and dark-brown eyebrows that looked as if they were painted.. and he always carried a crutched stick in one hand and a pair of yellow gloves in the other, and he never came out without a flower in his buttonhole... All down our street, they called him "the beautiful mashah," and whenever he went by, we always used to say "Here comes the mashah, the beautifool mashah. Who wouldn' t be mashah? '' <i>(Jun 13, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  As for those who belong to the noble army of the masher and the dude, everyone knows that they like barmaids. <i>(Jun 11, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  <b>FROBISHER</b> The first appeared like one self-pleased, / And somewhat of a flash-air, / With bushy whiskers, black-avised, / A dapper little masher. <i>(1893,Sketches of Gossipton,78</i>)

### Massey's
 
 <i>n.</i> [noun and attrib, re army life in PM Massey's era: slang for anything that was derided]

>  The once derided camp stew, soldier boots, uniforms, food, and everything else liable to criticism is known as "Massey's." <i>(Oct 2, 1918,Poverty Bay Herald,9</i>)

### Massey's Cossacks
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) [(usu. pl., occas. ellipt. Cossack) a name given by their opponents to the mounted special constables recruited in 1913 to control strikers (see special n. 2 a); also (quot. 1993) occas. misapplied (or transferred) to the special constables recruited to control unemployed protesters in the Depression of the 1930s (see special n. 2 b).]

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> The Farmers' Union organized its effort on military lines and enrolled mounted farmers as `specials' and others to form `arbitrationist' unions to work the wharves and even to man ships. ... In Wellington there was some fighting between the strikers and `Massey's Cossacks'. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,206</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND & TAYLOR</b> But it [1931] was not a happy time for Auckland. The mounted men were farmers come to the city to help get produce exported overseas. It was unpleasant to see women ... spitting on them and calling them scabs, Massey's cossacks, and worse names than that. <i>(1981,Sunrise,89</i>)

>  <b>PUGSLEY</b> ... Massey started enrolling 2000 special constables from the country districts [to counter wharf strikes in 1913]. ... Massey's "Specials' were largely Territorial Force. ... They rode in as `Massey's Cossacks'. <i>(1984,Gallipoli: the New Zealand Story,43</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> The Government [of 1913], the employers and the farmers retaliated by organizing special constables and vigilante groups, known to unionists as `Massey's Cossacks,' to work the ports and break the strike. <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),62</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> The Government [of 1913], the employers and the farmers retaliated by organizing special constables and vigilante groups, known to unionists as `Massey's Cossacks,' to work the ports and break the strike. <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),62</i>)

>  Like that "right bastard" Prime Minister Massey who, in 1913, got farmers to break the Wellington waterfront strike. "His Cossacks went storming through the streets of Wellington"... <i>(Dec 17, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

>  The Prime Minister, William Massey, employed many farmers as special constables - they became known as "Massey's Cossacks" for their brutal baton charges. <i>(Dec 5, 1999,Sunday Star Times,C7</i>)

>  Massey Cossacks, as they became known, were recruited and commanded by Army territorials, men who went on to become the officers leading New Zealand troops in the war. <i>(Apr 5, 2000,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  First, in the great maritime strike of 1890, then the 1913 strike which saw Massey's Cossacks ride into our history and then in the great 1951 lockout, the watersiders were defeated, leaving a bitter legacy that divided the union movement and lingers to this day. <i>(Feb 10, 2001,Evening Post,27</i>)

>  [The fence] was still being put up in 1913 when a general strike broke out, [prompting dozens of volunteers, including farmers on horseback (nicknamed Massey's Cossacks), to stand in front of it to guard the wharves. <i>(Sep 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 65/67</i>)

>  Over 1000 police and 'special' mounted constables were enrolled from the farming community by the Massey Government. Nicknamed 'Massey's Cossacks' by the strikers, the local farmers eagerly responded to the call to quell the 'Red Feds'. <i>(Jun 2002,NZ Memories,No., 36/55</i>)

>  The dispute became notorious for the violence against and by the "specials" or Massey's Cossacks" -- temporary police recruited from country areas to control striking workers. <i>(May 24, 2006,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> 137 <i>(1980,Southern Gentry</i>) [NOTES: In 1913 paramilitary cavalry units called 'Massey's Cossacks' were formed by young men from the middle classes to put down another strike.]

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> [Caption] Strikers scattering as mounted 'specials' ('Massey's Cossacks') gallop up Featherston Street during the General Strike of 1913. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,102</i>)

>  There was then the sound of hooves on ashphalt as the territorials from the Waikato Mounted Rifles worked ther way towards where Mulgan stood on duty. Known as 'Massey's Cossacks', after the former hard-line Prime Minister during the first World War, most of the mounted volunteers were (like Massey himself) farmers, out to whip the proletariat into line. <i>(2003,Long Journey to the Border,64</i>)

### master
 
 <i>n.</i> a freezing works departmental boss

>  The boss, that is, the department boss, is the <i>Master. </i> <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: Feilding Freezing Works]



 
 <i>n.</i> an officer of the High Court

>  Master Anne Gambrill was told yesterday that the creditor that petitioned for bankruptcy last year.. had withdrawn its opposition to reversing the order... The master reserved her decision after hearing from lawyers for the bank and for receivers Michael Stiasny and Grant Graham, who say the money belongs to the family's liquidated cropping company. <i>(Jul 12, 2000,Dominion,8</i>)

>  Masters hear the bread and butter of the commercial life of New Zealand, such as bankruptcies, insolvencies, and summary judgment applications, Solicitor-General Terence Arnold, QC, told the final sitting. <i>(Apr 17, 2002,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  I know that the Minister and his officials had considerable difficulty in drafting the Bill, and that there were many dissenting voices about whether there should be masters in the legal structure... The issue of masters being part of the judiciary is a major change. It introduces a new tier in the judicial structure between the district court judges and high court judges, although they would sit in the High Court and would have the authority to deal with interlocutory proceedings in that court. <i>(1986,NZ Parliamentary Debates,475 : 5161</i>)

### masting
 
 <i>n.</i> heavy flowering [for mast (year) see SOED mast noun hom.2]

>  Masting - the synchonised [sic] fluctuation in flowering and seeding among plant populations - is more common in New Zealand than in other countries... A fortnight ago Southern Beeches .. were masting so enthusiastically, the air was filled with vast quantities of pollen. <i>(Jan 4, 2006,Wairarapa News,5</i>)

>  That the humble beech seed is ultimately responsible for all this carnage and chaos reflects a feature of New Zealand flora called "masting"... These seasons of heavy flowering are commonly known as mast years. <i>(Apr 2000,NZ Geographic,No. 46/12</i>)

### mat

Atkinson)]

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> `One day I think all Maoris will wear the mat again.' <i>(1938,Promenade,117</i>)

>  [The Maori men to the making] of their prized dog-skin, or Kiwi-feather, clothing mats ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/8</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Maori races)]

>  From [flax] the Chief's elegant and ornamented silky Paipairoa, and the shaggy bee-butt looking Pake and Ngeri, - with their many intermediate kinds of clothing mats, - were alone manufactured. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/33</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> Wonderfully strange is the effect that this at last produces when the speaker has worked himself up to the highest stage of intense action, when he rushes forward, his mat flying wildly around him, brandishing with a peculiar quivering motion a <i>taioha</i> - wooden broadsword - or a tomahawk, rushing forward with high-toned vouce and hastily-spoken words, ... <i>(1881,Poenamo,3/5/177</i>)

>  The high class women ... stayed at home and made mats - piupiu from the fibre of flax; pokeka, rain mats from dried flax; ... <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,252,1988 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> When clad in a shoulder mat, Native fashion, or when stripped to the waist with the haft of a tomahawk in his flax-cord belt, which supported his kilt-like<i> piupiu ... </i> <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,37</i>)

>  <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,128</i>) [NOTES: Meikle]

>  <b>CRAIK</b> First, the chief threw off the mat which he wore as a cloak, and, brandishing a tomahawk in his hand, began a war-song ... <i>Ibid.</i> 177 ... we mean the plant from which the natives fabricate ... the cloaks, or mats, as they have been somewhat improperly called, which form their clothing. <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,92</i>)

>  How they paraded the streets and wharves in their best mats and finery. <i>(Oct 8, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  The Maoris came "dressed to kill," in mats and feathers and bare-footed. <i>(Mar 30, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,18</i>)

>  The Maoris, clad in mats and "beaucoup" war paint, performed a blood-curdling haka. <i>(Aug 30, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,V/51 : 58</i>)

>  I do believe that I'd be back to the mat myself if I hadn't been to a Pakeha high school. <i>(Jun 5, 1947,NZ Woman's Weekly: The First 60 Years,3</i>)

>  It is a kiwi mat, with a lovely border of the feathers of the native pigeons. <i>(Jan 21, 1937,King Country Chronicle,5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori cloak often of woven flax (cf flax mat, maori mat) [Williams (313) 2. A garment made from undressed flax leaves]; an old word given by the Europeans to various Maori outer garments, or other coverings, that usually were, or appeared to be, flat pieces of woven material, mainly of New Zealand flax.

>  The third sort of Ahou [cloak] seemed to be made of the Cloth Plant coarsely beat out or of a kind of narrow Sedge or course [sic] grass- ... the ends of the stuff of about a foot long were left out so as to form a thick covering layer over layer, and very much resembled what Seamen call thrum-Matts. <i>(Oct 11, 1769,Cook's Journals,1/572,1955 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1769 Oct 11 W.B. Monkhouse in 1955 Cook Journal I-572]

>  Their common clothing are very much like square thrum'd matts [sic] that are made of rope yarns &c. to lay at the doors or passages into houses to clean one[sic] shoes upon, these they tye [sic] round their necks the thrum'd side out and are generally large enough to cover the body as low as the knee; they are made with very little preparation of the broad glass plant before meantioned. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/740 Their Cloathing (both Men [and] Women) is a kind of matt or cloth made of silk grass or fine flax, curiously wove and tied about the shoulder that reaches to their knees ... [they] call it an <i>Ahow</i>, [fn <i>ahu</i>] and over it they have one of a coarser sort made of Grass well thrumbed with the same to keep out the wet which they call a Bugy Bugy. [fn] <i>pakepake</i> often made of undressed flax. <i>(Mar 31, 1770,Cook's Journals,1/279</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> The oldest man ...was of a brownish Complexion, and had a redbrown Matt on his back; The [sic] rest ... had likewise Matts, but of a whiter hue, than the old Mans . <i>(Mar 28, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/242</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> They appeared to be dressed in mats, and had broad paddles with which they managed their canoe .... <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/131</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> Most of them [Chatham Islanders] were cover'd with Matts or Seal Skins hung loosely over their Shoulders, which reach'd down to about the Hip <i>(Nov 1791,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1791 Nov E.Bell Journal of Voyage in H.M.S. `Chatham' (Vancouver expedition) (MS ATB) 61]

>  <b>SAVAGE</b> The common covering is a mat, composed of strongbladed grass, and so thickly woven ... it is in effect a complete thatch ... when they are seated, or squatted down, their figure very much resembles a large bee-hive, super-mounted with the head of a New Zealander. <i>Ibid.</i> 50 The dress of the natives consists in a mat finely woven of the native flax, and its glossy appearance, with a fringe all round, renders it by no means an inelegant sort of mantle. <i>Ibid.</i> [p?] Kakahow ... A mat, clothing <i>(1807,New Zealand (various authors/titles),48</i>)

>  <b>KENDALL</b> The women employ part of their time in making kakkahows (kakahu), <i>mats, moka</i> (muka), <i>etc</i>., ... <i>(Jul 25, 1814,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1814 July 25 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 67]

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> You will send the <i>Active</i> full of moca (muka), potatoes, limes, mats, fish, and nets. <i>(Mar 9, 1814,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1814 Mar 9 Marsden in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 58]

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> The dress he [Duaterra] put on consisted of a large mat made of flax, which descended below his waist, and was fastened round it by a belt of the same material; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 1/95 Like the natives [of a North Cape tribe], he [a Tahitian who had been `civilised' in Port Jackson] wore the mat thrown over his shoulders, but the one he had on was bordered with a deep vandyke of different colours, and fairly bedizened with the feathers of parrots and other birds, ... <i>(Dec 13, 1814,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/75,1817 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1814 Dec 13 Nicholas NZ (1817) 1/75]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Kai t|aka; A mat so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,157</i>)

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> They were clothed with their best mats, their heads ornamented with feathers, and had prepared an immense quantity of common and sweet potatoes and some tarra (<i>taro</i>), a root which they are very fond of, about the size of a small turnip. <i>(Aug 1820,Letters & Journals,283,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> They were clothed with their best mats, their heads ornamented with feathers, and had prepared an immense quantity of common and sweet potatoes and some tarra (<i>taro</i>), a root which they are very fond of, about the size of a small turnip. <i>(Aug 1820,Letters & Journals,283,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McCRAE</b> ... we were received with the greatest hospitality by his people who waving their mats called out to us - `Haromai, Haromi[sic],' their welcome to strangers which means literally `Come to us' `Come to us'. <i>(1820,Journal,20,1928 ed.</i>)

>  <b>KING</b> `August 15th. - When an husband dies the widow laments and lives in a small house called the aretaua (<i>Whare-taua</i>), making mats to wrap the corpse in when taken up again. She must not wet her fingers with her spittle while making the mats, called watu-tangi-knitting - crying (<i>Whatu-tangi</i>); the mat is called kakaho-wroimate, mat (of) tears, or bed-wet-with-tears-while-making (<i>Kakahu-roimata</i>), a mat of sorrow; a widow of Rangeehoo (Rangihoua) made two for her departed husband and one for herself. <i>(Aug 15, 1822,Letter,254,1934 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1822 Aug 15 John King in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 254]

>  <b>CRUISE</b> (Remarks) Their dress consists of a mat made of the native flax, which is very fine and silky ...; it is thrown over their shoulders; another mat ... is fastened round their waist by a belt or girdle. <i>(1823,Journal,182,1957 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SHEPHERD</b> The sailors here have a temporary dwelling by dragging their boats up ... and turned them keel uppermost laid a quantity dry fern ... rolls themselves up in a blanket or New Zealand cackakoo or Matt and sleeps upon the Fern. <i>(Mar 25, 1823,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Mar 25 Shepherd Journal in 1940 Howard Rakiura 365]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> On their landing, many of them were stripped and furnished with native mats. <i>(Jan 7, 1823,Journal,36,1961 ed.</i>)

>  Mats, and indeed native manufactures in general, are fast disappearing in this end of the Island ... <i>(Aug 9, 1834,NZ Independent,31,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> Moyterra ... sent back two large Pigs, and a Cacahow [<i>kakahu</i>] mat, [fn below] in return to Col Arthur, and said he should report this to his People and Friends at the time he gave his Sharkatty or Harkatty [<i>hakari</i>], meaning a Feast, ... he covered himself with his Cacarhow or Native Cloak ... [1963 fn] The word `mat' is a marginal interpolation; below Markham gives the more accurate rendering, `cloak'. <i>Ibid.</i> 40 Cacahows are now getting very scarce and a Ki tuck-er [<i>kaitaka</i>] or Mat Cacahow, [with] ornamented borders, is rarely to be seen in the present day... [Natives] have no other Costume [than English blankets] except a mat which goes round the Middle nearly down to the Knees. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),30,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... the native mat when new and clean is very becoming being worn like the roman toga one shoulder being bare ... <i>(Apr 8, 1839,Journal,2/92</i>)

>  <b>DEAN</b> <i>(1840,Letters</i>)

>  He gave me a dogskin mat by the Ngaticahounas [Ngatikahungunu]. <i>(Apr 3, 1849,Torlesse Papers,72,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McKILLOP</b> The natives make very handsome mats, by beating the flax out with a piece of wood, and plaiting the border very ingeniously by hand. <i>(1849,Reminiscences,162</i>)

>  I bought a kaitaka mat of E'ou's wife. <i>(Jan 16, 1850,Torlesse Papers,126,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> [Tamihana] rose with the sun, shook the fleas out of his mat, and joined the consistent Christians in prayer and praise. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,2/249</i>)

>  <b>CARBERRY</b> ... some of the old chiefs were in mats & blankets ... <i>(1859,Journal,2/249</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Before the introduction of blankets, the clothing of the New Zealanders consisted entirely of garments manufactured from the fibres of the native flax. These garments, or `mats,' as they are generally termed, display great ingenuity and taste in their fabrication; the threads are interwoven longitudinally, with others placed crosswise, and every thread is carefully fastened at intervals of about half an inch. <i>(1866,Polynesia,155</i>)

>  <b>MAIR</b> The Maungapohatu Natives are a wild, restless set, with large shaggy heads of hair, and clad in mats made from the coarse fibres of the <i>Toii</i> (Cordyline Indivisa) <i>(1871,AJHR,F-1/43</i>) [NOTES: 1871 AJHR F-I, 43 Gilbert Mair in 1979 Webster Rua 82]

>  There were still traces [of the Maori] - one a mako stick cut, and the others some hairs of a mat caught in a stick. <i>(Mar 15, 1864,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/96</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>JOHNSTONE</b> The rough pureki .... when seen upon the men in the canoes which boarded the first vessels that visited the Island, was not inappropriately called a `mat', and the ugly name came to be applied to any description of garment worn by the Maoris. [<i>puureke</i> (i) <i>(1874,Maoria,16</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Some extra mats for tent-roofs against rain;/ <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,2/4</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Their own native mats were warm and waterproof. ... But formerly they had not only the kaitaka [i-circumflex], made of the finest flax and ornamented by a handsome border, ... <i>(1884,Our Maoris,84</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,141</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> Being very tall, [the Maoris] presented a dignified appearance clad only in their silky white <i>Kaitakas</i> with huia feathers in their hair. These finest of Maori mats they wore tied on the right shoulder, leaving that hand ... free to grasp the greenstone `mere'. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,37</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> <i>(1970,Not Here, Not Now,197</i>)

>  <i>mat ... NZ </i>cloak or cape worn by Maoris, Maori way of life <i>(she had been to a High School and then returned to the mat). </i> <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,463</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  [Hdg] Preparing pillars and Maori mats The entrance to Government House, Wellington, will soon have a Taranaki flavour - carved pillars and woven mats made in the Te Atiawa (Taranaki) style Petone Technical Institute students carved pou out of a 100-year-old totara pile ... Te Atiawa women are still plaiting the mat to be laid at the main door. <i>(Oct 31, 1986,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  <b>A.H. ADAMS</b> Then the impatient chiefs / Of Awhita, flinging their mats aside / And grasping <i>meres</i>, to the war-dance sprang <i>(1913,Collected Verses,30</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep with a matted fleece.

>  <b>BRABAZON</b> Commenced to shear this morning at 5.30 a.m. Fine day. Not bad fleeces. No mats. <i>(Nov 1897,Brabazon Diaries,15</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a Maori sleeping mat

>  Beneath the carven counterparts of their ancestors and the fierce <i>teko teko</i> on guard high above ... the Maoris lament on their mats. <i>(Aug 1953,Here & Now,21</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> `Perhaps it's better,' said Tiffany with youth's cruelty towards an unwanted lover. `Hemi is always asking me to come under his mat.' <i>(1938,Promenade,235</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> The Chief ... gave me a small mat to sleep in and cut fresh Palm leaves, and a clean Mat for me to sleep on ... <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),41,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> He addressed his speech to a lone white man who sat before him on a mat in the meeting house. <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,109</i>)

### mat sugar
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Sugar] came in mats, the big ones weighing 180 lbs, and was often called `mat sugar'. This mat was native made, being a pleated plant, and was pretty well square. Outside this mat, and covering it, was a coarse bag called a soojee (or suji?) bag. A familiar saying of the early days was, `As rough as a soojee bag', and this saying was applied to both people and things. Up to 1863 I never saw sugar except in mats ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,97</i>)

### mat time
 
 <i>n.</i> "show and tell" time at kindergarten?

>  His voice has strengthened - he can speak up at mat time at kindy. <i>(Nov 5, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

### mat-making
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BUICK</b> Its [sc Moa] feathers were used for mat-making <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,236</i>)

### mat-pin
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The more common things to be found in the neighbourhood of [112] old settlements are bone mat-pins, bone barbs of fish-hooks ... <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/111</i>)

### mata
 
 <i>n.</i> = pink maomao [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  SCORPIS FAIRCHILDI. sp. nov. Native name - Mata. <i>(1875,TrNZI,7/241</i>) [NOTES: (Hector)]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Caprodon longimanus</i> (Guenther). LONGFIN; MATA. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,33,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> <i>Maomao, pink .. mata</i> .. Caprodon longimanus <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> PINK MAOMAO [<i>Caprodon longimanus</i>] ... Longfin and mata (Maori) are alternative names. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,44</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> PINK MAOMAO or LONGFIN ... <i>Other names: Caprodon longimanus</i>; mata (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,227</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>PINK MAOMAO</i> (Mata) <i>Caprodon longimanus</i> ... The pink maomao is a relatively large sea perch ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,207,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Ma[a]ta[a]</i> (iii), <i>ma[a]ta[a]ta[a],</i> ...2. <i>Torpedo fairchildi, electric ray</i>, and <i>Caprodon longimanus, longfin;</i> fish. <i>(1975,Dictionary of the Maori Language,185</i>)

>  pink maomao, longfin, mata (maataa) <i>(1875,TrNZI,7/241</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a beetle

>  <b>BULLER</b> [The morepork castings in the Canterbury Museum] are hard pellets, of an oval form, and of the size of a Sparrow's egg, composed chiefly of the hard elytra and heads of various coleopterous insects, among which I noticed particularly the shiny covering of the mata (<i>Feronia antarctica</i>), a handsome ground-beetle which is found on the Canterbury Plains, but does not occur in the North Island. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,20,1888 2nd edn 1/195 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a water fern

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Histiopteris incisa </i>WATER FERN ... Maataa ... <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,432</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a blubber knife

>  <b>BULLER</b> SPHENOEACUS PUNCTATUS. (COMMON UTICK \{FERN-BIRD\}.) ... <i>Maori names</i>. Mata, Matata, Kotata, Nako, and Koroatito. ... It frequents the dense fern (<i>Pteris aquilina</i>) of the open country, ... <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,128,1888 2nd edn 1/59 ed.</i>)

>  Mata, or blubber knives, are another important link. <i>(Mar 18, 1924,Otago Witness,44</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> = fernbird [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [matata (1842)](mar-moa.md#matata)))

>  SPHENOEACUS PUNCTATUS, Quoy. and Gaim. Mata. Grass-bird, Grass-pheasant, Utick. ... [58] From its call, it is in some places named the Utick. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/57</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]



 
 <i>n.</i> [Brunners' spelling,<i> mutta</i> white-bait; a variety of INANGA]

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> In October and November commences the fishing season here [in Paroa, West Coast] - the mutta [fn.mata, a variety of the inanga], or white-bait entering the rivers ...in great quantity. <i>(1857,Great Journey,55,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,213, 503</i>) [NOTES: [OEDS check ? sense]]

>  <b>BUICK</b> The first would come the whitebait - the <i>mata</i> of the South Island Maori - now largely eaten out by trout ... [fn refers to Brunner's Journal of 1847 `In October and November commences the fishing season here, the mutta [mata], or whitebait, entering the rivers with the tide in great quantities.] <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,174</i>)

### matagauri
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [matagouri](mar-moa.md#matagouri))

### matagory
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [matagouri](mar-moa.md#matagouri))

### matagouri
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) Discaria toumatou or wild Irishman (from Maori tumatakuru)

>  ... etu matukuru .... thorn plant. <i>(1839,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Oct (1839) E.J. Wakefield MS Diary]

>  <b>GARVIE</b> In some places a few patches of black birch are found in the gullies, and manuka scrub on the ranges; but towards the Manuherikia only a few patches of matakaru, and a dark small-leaved shrub are to be met with. <i>(1857,Report</i>) [NOTES: c1857 Alex Garvie [Report on Otago] in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding transitional spg <i>matakuru </i>19 spg also <i>matakuru </i]

>  ...Much of it is encumbered with matakura scrub. <i>(Sep 22, 1859,Otago Gazette,280</i>) [NOTES: (A. Garvie)]

>  <b>DUNCAN</b> . ... tomatagorra <i>(1888,Wakatipians,82</i>)

>  The tea generally tastes of birch or Matagouri <i>(May 7, 1896,Otago Witness,48</i>)

>  <b>morr</b> <i>Matagory, n</i>.a prickly shrub of New Zealand, <i>Discaria toumatou</i>, Raoul.; also called <i>Wild Irishman</i> (q.v.). The Maori name is <i>Tumatakuru</i>, of which <i>Matagory</i>, with various spellings, is a corruption, much used by rabbiters and swagmen. The termination <i>gory</i> evidently arises by the law of Hobson-Jobson from the fact that the spikes draw blood. <i>(1898,Austral English,289</i>)

>  Now I've been around some stations way out back upon the hills, /.../ By mountain creeks that murmur where the matagauri grows,/ <i>(1950,Farm & Station Verse,44</i>) [NOTES: c1890-1900 Thompson `Another Station Ballads' in 1950 Farm and Station Verse 44]

>  <i>Mat&#769;.a-go&#769;.ry</i> (m&abreve.t&#769;.&adot.-g&odot.&#769;.r&ybreve.)<i>, n.</i> [Corruption of Maori <i>tumatukuru.] (Bot.) </i>See TUMATUKURU. <i>[Local, N. Z.] </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2026</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> By the mountain creeks that murmur where the matagauri grows,/ <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,43</i>)

>  Continuing our walk we find it impeded by a tangle of tussock, bracken, flax, matakouri, spear grass, ... all of which has vanished today. <i>(1914,History of North Otago,42,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> On the rather loose soil of the gentle slopes [of the Upper Awatere Valley] where there are thickets of wild Irishman or matagouri (<i>Discaria toumatou</i>) are many rabbit warrens, ... <i>(1969,Molesworth,154</i>) [NOTES: c1919 Cockayne Journal of Agriculture in 1969 McCaskill Molesworth 154]

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> ... I saw that the supposed bushranger was a compact bush of matakoura or wild Irishman. <i>(1925,Random Recollections,27</i>)

>  <i>Matagouri.</i>- Discaria toumatu. The <i>Wild Irishman</i> or <i>Irishman</i> scrub. (q.v.). ([1898 Morris Austral-English]) <i>(Nov 4, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>BURDON</b> Under their shelter the ... Spaniard or spear-grass lay in ambush, while ... lawyer bushes, and matagauri filled in spaces untenanted by other plants. <i>(1938,High Country,54</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> [Speargrass] was of no use as a rule, but when dead its stalks were useful to boil the billy. The great standby in this woodless country ... was tumatakauru [sic: ?indicating pron mata-gauri] or matagouri, that prickly pest ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,120</i>)

>  The steppe was originally burned to get rid of the matagowrie and spear grass. <i>(1954,NZ Geographer,154</i>) [NOTES: (Cumberland)]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Matagouri flourished on poor or rocky soil. <i>(1947,Pioneer Recollections,5,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> ... shrubs common in the tussock zone: matagouri, native brooms, dwarf koromikos, monoao, sarmentose tutu, the grasslike spiky-leaved Spaniard, the harsh patotara, golden cottonwood, cushion-like pimeleas, and the low, matted, carpet-like growth of the scabweeds are characteristic of the low tussock country, ... <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,35,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BLAKISTON</b> On the northern side, where the only cover was tussock and matagouri, there were never many pigs, ... <i>(1952,My Yesteryears,17</i>) [NOTES: [f. c1869]]

>  <b>STRONACH</b> There was scarcely any grass. The knobs were dotted with grey matagouris, and in the gullies a few tussocks grew. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,33 [pl]</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Here was a large flat delta near the river's mouth covered in manuka scrub, dotted with matagouri and clumps of flax. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,69</i>)

>  The river forced us to do much sidling through the tauhinau and matagouri and across a limestone face ... <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,76</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Discaria ... Toumatu</i> [sic] ... Wild Irishman. MATAGOWRI, TUMATA-KURI [sic] ... <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,54</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... the O.C. said when the Japs land we'll chase them into the donga, we'll chase them into the matagouri ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,225</i>)

>  <b>MALING</b> Matagouri or Wild Irishman, and speargrass or Spaniard. <i>(1958,Torlesse Papers,74 fn</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> The whole [Waikaka] valley and the ... ridges were covered [in 1876] with luxurious white tussocks (Poa caespitosa) diversified with a few snow grass tussocks (Danthonia raoulii), trees and shrubs comprising Maukoko, or native broom (Carmichaelia australia[?lis]), Koromiko (Veronica salicifo), daisy tree (Aleria rirgata [?virgata]), cottonwood (Cassinia fulvida), ... native thorns or matagouri and mingimingi (Coprosma propinqua). [check] <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,239</i>)

>  ~ See <i>Irishman, wild. </i> <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),252</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> There were a few scrubby stony flats covered with Irishman and speargrass (Irishman is the unpleasant thorny scrub which I saw going over the hill from Lyttelton to ChristChurch) on either side of the stream; ... [ed fn to 1964 edn ed Brassington and Maling : Matagowry (<i>Discaria Toumatou</i>) and Spaniard (<i>Aciphylla Colensoi</i>). <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,IV/54</i>) [NOTES: 1964 ed. fn in 1863 Butler First Year iv/54]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Many native plants have Maori names more or less mutilated such as <i>matagowri, goai</i> (kowhai), and <i>biddy bid</i> (piri piri). <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/680</i>) [NOTES: Wall NZ Speech]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Matagouri .. .. tumatakuru, tumatu-kuru .. .. <i>Discaria toumatou </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>matagouri n</i>. N.Z. prickly shrub, known coll. as Wild Irishman [corr. of Maori <i>tumatakaru</i>]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> <i>Matagouri</i>: Corruption of<i> tau matakuru</i> [sic], a spiky shrub. <i>(1972,Mountain World,310</i>)

>  <i>matagouri n</i>. a New Zealand thorny bush which forms thickets in open country. Also called: <i>Wild Irishman</i>. [< Maori <i>Tumatakuru</i>] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,695</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 463; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 302]]

>  <b>BRASCH</b> Only the thorn/ Alone on the parched rise, in human matagowry/ Dry-green and fibrous, ... <i>(1939,Collins Concise English Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: `Waianakura']

>  <i>matagoury</i>: corruption of tumatakuru. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>BRASCH</b> Only the thorn/ Alone on the parched rise, inhuman matakauri/ Dry-green and fibrous, sorrowing,/ <i>(1984,Collected Poems,5</i>) [NOTES: Waianakarua]

>  ... the apparent spread ... has resulted from the suppression of matagouri (<i>Discaria toumatou</i>), a native shrub. <i>(1987,Triennial Report Botany Division,1982-1984/59</i>)

>  <b>MIKAERE</b> ... there was the little thing ... playing about quite happily amongst the matagouri and the taramea. <i>(1988,Te Maiharoa,78</i>) [NOTES: [used by Maori speaker/writer]]

>  <b>DAWSON</b> With the exception of matagouri (<i>Discaria toumatou</i>) New Zealand divaricates are not spiny ... <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,144</i>)

>  A few stunted matakuri (or Wild Irishman) ... <i>(1890,Zealandia,I-613</i>)

>  They're [the hinds] not really happy without the gullies and a bit of matagouri. <i>(Dec 14, 2000,NZ Farmer,27</i>)

>  About 30m up, a clump of grass stood between low-growing matagouri. <i>(Nov 15, 1999,Evening Post,17</i>)

>  <b>McHUTCHESON</b> The matagouri scrub and tussock. <i>(1892,Camp-Life in Fiordland</i>)

>  That matagauri ridge beyond was called the camping ground. <i>(1897,Tom Bracken's Annual,No. 237</i>)

>  Much over-run with the scrub called 'tomata -guru.' <i>(Sep 22, 1859,Otago Gazette,264</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Matagory, a prickly shrub of New Zealand. <i>(1898,Austral English,289</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. the Esk facings are a series of great shingle guts and bluffs, steep as a hen's face and interspersed with tracts of heavy tangle matagouri. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,76</i>)

>  The track wound around the hillside, and soon we began the descent, and were soon winding our way through matagouri at the side of a gentle stream that flowed through the tight valley into the Awatere. <i>(May 8, 2000,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  He's tanned from the outdoor life and his hands are full of infected cuts from matagouri thorns that come out of the wool. <i>(Jul 22, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),45</i>)

>  The pair agreed to settle the matter by racing their horses to the nearest matagouri bush. <i>(May 2001,NZ Geographic,51/46</i>)

>  Matagouri is a weed, say farmers, who treat the native thorn bush like gorse... Matagouri is a hard, twisted plant with belligerent spikes, but it gives a distinct purply-grey tint to the southern high country. <i>(Aug 11, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  But the wethers, creatures of habit and suspicion, are reluctant to cross even this small creek, bubbling across shingle between grassy banks smudged with matagouri. <i>(Feb 20, 2010,Your Weekend,8</i>)

>  ...he went back into the thick matagouri scrub in the steep gully of the creek. <i>(May 1994,NZ Historic Places,47/37</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The Poulter and Riversdale country is practically all bushbound and, with those tracts of matagouri as well, it is difficult to get clean musters. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,120</i>)

>  The thief had disappeared - lost among the spear-pointed matakowri. <i>(Nov 11, 1924,Otago Daily Times,66</i>)

### matagouri country
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) land supporting a vigorous growth of tumatakuru.

>  <b>SHARPE</b> [I] observed a big area of clear matagouri country. <i>(1966,Fiordland Muster,15</i>)

>  Sheep grazed on matagouri country prior to summer shearing can get considerable matagouri debris caught in the fleece, which may lead to a reduced wool price. <i>(Mar 1986,Proceedings 1985 Hill & High Country Seminar,112</i>) [NOTES: Proceedings 1985 Hill & High Country Seminar]

### matagouri flat
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) an extended level area of land, often a river flat, which grows the indigenous tumatakuru (matagouri)

>  It [the track] ambles over matagouri flats, then through stately red beech forest ... <i>(Sep 2008,NZ Geographic,28</i>)

>  <b>Newton</b> The Riverdale flats, opposite Grasmere, include some extensive areas of swamp and sweet, healthy, matagouri flats and although they lie on the shady side of the river, are wonderful lambing and tupping country. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,138</i>)

>  Crossing the matagouri flats of Lakes Station, the route turns right, down the long grass cattle flats. <i>(2007,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www.tramper.co.nz]

>  Cross the bridge and take the 4WD track to the forest edge on the far side of the matagouri flats. <i>(2007,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www.doc.govt.nz/templates/trackandwalk.aspx?id=36391]

### matagouri mermaid
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep, back-country shepherd or land girl.

>  Matagouri mermaid: 1. sheep. 2. lone shepherd. 3 land girl. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

>  <b>ORSMAN</b> matagouri mermaid, a back-country man who has kept to himself. <i>(2000,Personal Communication</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [matagouri](mar-moa.md#matagouri))
