### matai
 
 <i>n.</i> a native forest tree with hard timber and a tall straight trunk; [Maori matai] <i>Podocarpus spicatus</i> a timber tree or its timber, black pine, mai

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Kauri</i> and <i>Totara, Rimu</i>, and <i>Matai</i> and <i>Maire,/</i> Red-as-blood <i>Rata</i>, and bright-as-blood <i>Pohutukawa,/ Manuka</i> dark-ey'd, Convolvulus star-ey'd - / The glittering of you that morning! [f@n2 attached to the italicised names] 2. <i>Kauri</i>, etc. (<i>Kow-ree</i>, To[acute]-ta-ra, Ree[e acute]-moo, <i>Mah[a acute]-ti</i>, Mi-reh, Rah[a acute]-tah, Po-hoo-too-kah[a acute]-wah, <i>Mah[a acute]-noo-kah</i>): New Zealand Bush trees. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,54</i>)

>  matai : black pine. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Podocarpus spicata</i>, Br. Matai, Mai, Black-pine of Otago.--A large tree, 80 feet high; trunk 2-4 feet in diameter. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,124</i>)

>  [Native name] Matai .. [Settlers' name] Black-pine .. [Family] Coniferae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,120</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 120]

>  <b>KNOX</b> Then there is the Matai, which is much like the English yew, and is used for making furniture; the Miro, which has a beautiful red fruit on which pigeons grow fat; and the Kawaka, which has a remarkable leaf and a durable wood. <i>(1888,Boy Travellers,201</i>)

>  Matai (black pine) .. Podocarpus spicata .. A useful wood, heavy, close grained, easily worked, shrinking little; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,12</i>)

>  Matai (black pine) .. Podocarpus spicata .. A useful wood, heavy, close grained, easily worked, shrinking little; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,12</i>)

>  The Committee recommends the disuse of the terms `red-pine', `white-pine', and `black-pine', in favour of rimu, kahikatea and matai, in order to prevent their being confused with timbers having similar names in Europe. <i>(Jul 17, 1896,AJHR,H.24/</i>) [NOTES: 1896 (July 17) Interim Report No. 3 (i) of Timber Conference (A to J's, H. 24)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Matai, n</i>. often abridged to <i>Mai</i>, Maori name for a New Zealand tree, <i>Podocarpus spicata</i>, R. Br., N.O. <i>Coniferae.</i> Black-pine of Otago. <i>(1898,Austral English,289</i>)

>  The timber on the western side consists of red- and white-pine, matai (or black-pine), totara, kawhaka (or cedar), rata, and occasional silver-pine, besides black- and red-birch (<i>Fagus fusca</i>). <i>Ibid.</i> 30 The timbers of commercial value are totara, rimu, miro, matai, rata, and kamahi, in mixed bushes; but <i>Fagus fusca</i> and other beeches predominate on the high lands between the lakes and the fiords on the west coast. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,19</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>P[odocarpus] spicatus, R.Br.</i> ... bark black or bluish-black. ... Fruit black or nearly so ... [651] matai The Maori name of the black pine of New Zealand. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,650</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>P[odocarpus] spicatus, R.Br.</i> ... bark black or bluish-black. ... Fruit black or nearly so ... [651] matai The Maori name of the black pine of New Zealand. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,650</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> It took all Sean's strength to cock it, but it could drive a reinforcing steel bolt through a piece of four-centimetre matai. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,126</i>)

>  <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),252</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Matai .. .. matai .. .. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> In addition the boys have cut down rimu, totara, matai, and pinus growing on the property and built a 4-stand woolshed without help from outside. <i>(Dec 22, 1954,Shepherd's Calendar,1961</i>)

>  <i>matai n</i>. a New Zealand tree, the black pine, the wood of which is used as building timber. [< Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,695</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 463; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 302]]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> matai <i>species of forest tree </i> <i>(1982,Selected Poems,188,1 ed.</i>)

>  [Lithograph] of matai (black pine) foliage. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,168</i>)

>  In an economic point of view, the chief trees of the Middle Island, are the Red and White Pine, respectively called by the Maoris: the former Mai or Matai (<i>Podicarpus spicata</i>),[fn] the latter the Kahikatea (Podocarpus dacrydioides).<i> [ed. fn] Black Rue of Otago, Hook., f.; Miro, Black Rue or Black Pine in Otago, Hector; Miro, often confounded with Black Pine, Balfour; Matai, Colenso, - Mai or Matai (Taylor). </i> <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/12</i>) [NOTES: (Monro)]

>  <b>HAY</b> The Matai, or `Red Pine' (<i>Podocarpus spicata</i>), needs special mention. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/194</i>)

>  Black rue Pine, or Matai, (<i>Podocarpus spicata</i>), a tree similar, in size and form, to the last [Miro]. Wood white, tough, not so hard or durable in wet places, as Miro. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/31</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <i>matai</i>, a tree (Podocarpus spicatus), black-pine: often shortened to ma[a]i. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/145</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  Mataii. Podocarpus spicata, Br. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/49</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  Puriri Posts, house blocks and firewood (puriri, tea-tree, matai and tarariri). <i>(Sep 1907,Wairoa Bell & Northern Advertiser,[13]</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> Matai (<i>Taxus Matai) - a plant with a small yew-tree leaf, a strong smell, and a rough bark. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),50</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ...matai <i>Ibid.</i> 2/399 The <i>Matia</i> [sic, a1-acute] is a durable wood ...; it has similar properties to red pine or Totara [a's-acute], but grows less bulky. <i>(1838,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/396</i>)

>  ...mataihi <i>(1843,NZ Journal,96/217</i>) [NOTES: (C.H. Kettle, a sawyer)]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> We passed a large number of Matai trees and the ground was literally covered with their berries ... and several miro trees whose beautiful red berries have a rich flavor not unlike that of mace <i>(Apr 1847,Journal,5/36</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> 2. Mai, or Matai, (<i>Dacrydium matai</i>, or <i>taxifolium.</i>) This tree very much resembles the preceding one [kahikatea] in all its qualities. The leaves are smaller, and the wood of rather superior quality, being also generally of a yellowish colour. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,141</i>)

>  <b>ALLOM</b> Allom spg <i>mataki</i> poss. misprint for <i>matahi</i> or confusion with the variant <i>ma[a]taki</i> for <i>ma[a]tai `to watch' </i>... the fine timber it produces [in Wairarapa], particularly the <i>Kahikatea, Rimu, Mai</i> or <i>Mataki</i>, and <i>Totara</i>, which woods are most useful ... <i>(1853,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Allom letter in 1853 Earp NZ 119 1853]

>  <b>ALLOM</b> <i>Maiberry</i>, or <i>Mataki</i>, is a small round fruit of the same colour as the tawa berry, ... <i>(1853,Letter,130</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Allom letter in 1853 Earp NZ 130]

>  <b>EARP</b> Of the [Wairarapa] plain, about 100,000 acres is covered with fine timber, chiefly totara, matai, hiora, kahikatea, manuka &c., all highly valuable for ... cabinet work and carpentry. <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),113</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> <i>Podocarpus spicatus </i>, Br. ... Nat. names, `Mai', <i>Cunn.</i>, and `Mataii', <i>Col. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 232</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> <i>Valuable ... furniture planks are sawn from the Rimu, Kakikatae, or white pine (Dacrydium excelsum)</i>, Matai, Mairi, and Manuka (<i>Leptospermum scoparium</i>). <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/20,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Mataii, <i>Col[enso]. Podocarpus spicata</i>. <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  I have in this paper adhered to the popular [Canterbury, Otago] name of black-pine for this timber, but the native name matai is always used in the North. <i>(1876,TrNZI,9/157</i>) [NOTES: (Blair)]



 
 <i>n.</i> a high-ranking chief or elder in Samoan society [ORIGIN: Samoan]

>  It is not fitting for a matai to plead, or to perform household chores. <i>(Apr 4, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),65</i>)

>  "That's complete rubbish," a high-ranking matai or chief said, "and it's so out of date. This is completely unacceptable in Samoa." <i>(Sep 26, 2005,Dominion Post,A5</i>)

>  <b>Maepu</b> Dad was the oldest in his family and he was a matai so he had certain duties and obligations. <i>(2008,Six Pack 3, The,27</i>)

>  But Clark's important last duty is to lay a wreath at the tomb of Tupua Tamasese Lealofi III, the high-ranking matai gunned down with several others on "Black Syunday", December 29, 1929. <i>(Jun 22, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  The Samoan community bestowed on David Lange the chiefly matai title of tangaloa in recognition of his service to the Pacific communities of New Zealand. <i>(Aug 16, 2005,NZ Herald,A10</i>)

>  The matai responsibilities of Samoan village life, though transported to an ACC park in Grey Lynn, continue to weigh heavily. <i>(May 1987,Metro,112</i>)

>  Despite the decrepit charm of Apia, village life is still the basis of Samoan life, the <i>matai</i>, or chief, holding sway over s strict system of <i>aiga</i>-(extended family)based justice, social protocol, housing and a descending hierarchy of village title-holders. <i>(Jan 31, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  At home the top is to work and become a matai but here the top is to be a lawyer or something. <i>(Jun 12, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)</i>

### matai beer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sappy liquid exuded by matai trees, possibly fermented. [ca1900 Marlborough bushmen.]

>  I have frequently drunk the matai beer which gushes out of the centre of the tree. <i>(Feb 10, 1921,Quick March,13</i>)

>  I dimly remember as a youngster in the days when the Forty-Mile Bush was still standing, such a drink as 'matai beer' was sometimes spoken of amongst bushmen. <i>(Dec 10, 1920,Quick March,11</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> In New Zealand, Maori men knew that cracks in the heart-wood of old matai trees often exude a liquid, matai beer, a stimulating drink that weary white bushmen later discovered. <i>(1976,From the Beginning: chronicles of a county,294</i>)

>  <b>HUTT</b> Matai beer was described by a Taihape newspaper in 1912 as tasting "like used machine oil... When a deep swig is absorbed, one has all the sensations of having swallowed a lighted kerosene stove." <i>(1999,Maori & Alcohol,14</i>) [NOTES: see McKay <i>Puborama</i> 1961, p. 95]

>  ... I dimly remember as a youngster in the days when the Forty-Mile Bush was still standing, such a drink as `matai beer' was sometimes spoken of amongst bushmen. Apparently it was made from the sap of the matai tree, extracted by the simple process of boring an auger-hole in the trunk. ... Can any old-timer axeman give any information concerning this liquor. <i>(Dec 10, 1920,Quick March,13</i>)

>  When I was a boy I lived in Carterton, and that was in the 'sixties, when there was little else than bush there ... I have frequently drunk the matai beer which gushes out of the centre of the tree when the axemen get to the heart. The flavour varies, but always has something of the tang of resin about it, though it is not at all a bad drink. ... I never heard of this matai beer being intoxicating; ... <i>(Feb 10, 1921,Quick March,13</i>)

>  <b>MACKAY</b> In the rip-roaring times of the King Country, Matai beer was a favourite with those who were strong enough and tough enough to drink it. It went out of favour when `cruisers' for the various timber mills complained that much good yellow pine was being ruined through the number of deep axe cuts and even auger holes in the standing trees, made to let the potent juice run into waiting pannikins or buckets. The favourite recipe for this form of wood alcohol represented the addition of three or four cupsful of the sap to each gallon of `ordinary' liquid refreshment. That the resultant mixture had a pretty hefty kick to it may be judged from this description handed in to the Taihape newspaper (1912): `Matai beer is the term used to describe a certain brand or grade of alcoholic brew. It may be identified easily. It smells like an ancient bar room the morning after. It tastes like used machine oil, only a very low grade of machine oil. <i>(1961,Puborama,95</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> But [bushmen] used to make concoctions of their own, and the most popular ... was matai beer. ... When you felled a big matai, a really mature black pine ... you would see the main artery in the heart of the tree gushing out the life liquid. ... It wasn't the sap; this was not related to that. It was a pure clear liquid, and it gushed out like the main artery gushing blood. But that used to be prized, and it would be caught in a billy. That was really something. It was beautiful and cool and sweet to drink as it was, but some of the blokes used to bung in maybe a handful of raisins and sugar and cork her up and let her ferment, and whether that fluid had any other properties in it or not I don't know, but you could get quite a potent drink from it. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,65</i>) [NOTES: (Bob Edwards, bushman)]

>  <b>FULLER</b> MATAI BEER [title] I have been told by old bushmen and Maoris about making beer from the sap of the matai tree ... Apparently when the tree was being cut down the watery sap was collected, more water was added and also some ordinary beer, or yeast. It was left to ferment and was reputed to be quite strong though I have not been able to find any written evidence of this. <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,83</i>)

### mataitai
 
 <i>n.</i> a reserve that recognises and provides for customary food-gathering by Maori in areas special to tangata whenua [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  A mataitai reserve is an identified traditional fishing ground established in fisheries waters for the purpose of customary food gathering. <i>(Oct 8, 2008,Wairarapa News,33</i>)

>  Proposed mahinga mataitai (sea-food gathering) reserves will be set aside for exclusive use by particular Maori tribes... The mataitai move split the Government and will likely split New Zealanders. <i>(Dec 12, 1992,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  The new rules will allow local Maori groups to set up self-managed fishing reserves (mataitais) around traditional fishing spots or reefs. <i>(Dec 17, 1998,Dominion,8</i>)

>  The Government says it wants to protect 10 per cent of New Zealand's marine environment by 2010 in a representative network of "marine protected areas"; either in the sanctity of a marine reserve or some form of fisheries management regime such as mataitai or taiapure (fishing reserves). <i>(Feb 7, 2002,Dominion,11</i>)

>  Last month Parliament declared the coastline from Whirinaki to 28km north a "mataitai" - a reserve that recognises and provides for customary food-gathering by Maori in areas special to tangata whenua. ... The mataitai off Whirinaki is known as "Moremore". ... Mataitai were introduced as part of the Treaty of Waitangi (Fisheries Claims) Settlement Act 1992 to provide a tool for Maori to manage all non-commercial fishing grounds. ... Rock Lobster Council chief Dary Sykes said Moremore was the first mataitai to affect commercial fishing. ... Between 78 tonnes and 105 tonnes of seafood a season came from the Kirakau area and mataitai could result in $2 million lost income. <i>(Sep 16, 2005,NZ Herald,A11</i>)

>  At present iwis manage customary fisheries and mataitai Maori fishing reserves in their regions, giving out licences to take fish or shellfish above recreational catch limits. <i>(Mar 5, 2001,Dominion,3</i>)

>  ..the Kairakau Lands Trust, being tangata whenua who hold manawhenua moana over the area, applied to the Minister of Fisheries for mataitai reserve ... <i>(Nov 4, 2006,Dominion Post,C15</i>)

### Matak
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) the Matakitaki River [ORIGIN: abbreviation, in the Nelson Lakes National Park]

>  Almost a park 'n' play section, the 'middle Matak', as it's known, is spoken of not in terms of its fantastic beginner teaching spots and lovely little gorge, but instead, most discuss flow regimes and which of the two or three excellent play spots are working - or not. <i>(1996,NZ Whitewater,pp 168</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [matagouri](mar-moa.md#matagouri))

 <i>n.</i> <i>Pogonornis cincta<i>, hihi

### matakite
 
 <i>n.</i> a seer

>  Ngati Kahungungu chairman Ngahiwi Tomoana said last week the matakite found the graves just by walking around. The matakite report was "met with scepticism" at first, but they have been proven right, Mr Tomoana said. <i>(Sep 11, 2008,Dominion Post,A8</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> In the first place, his father goes to consult the <i>matakite</i>, or seer of the family, to learn the cause of the illness. <i>(1856,Traditions & Superstitions,125</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> The <i>Matakite</i>, or seers, were analogous to the clairvoyant. <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,202</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> In our party was an old <i>tohunga</i> ...; he was gifted with the power of <i>matakite</i>, or second sight. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/294</i>)

>  <i>matakite</i>, a seer; to practise divination. <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/153</i>) [NOTES: Andersen]

>  Eria, and the narratives which placed him as a matakite were local, and I was on the wrong side of the mountains. <i>(1987,NZ Journal of History,24</i>) [NOTES: Binney]

### matamoe
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>8/30</b> `Matamoe' is the term used by Wellington Maoris to refer to the adult <i>Anguilla australis;</i> but the three stages of growth are also known. The first stage, called `hao,' or `tuna heke,' is a small silver-bellied eel ... It is common in swamps and is sometimes called `swamp-eel' by settlers. The second stage is called `tahu' ... <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a conger eel

>  LEPTOCEPHALUS CONGER (Linnaeus). <i>Conger-eel; Koiro.</i> Called `matamoe' by the Maoris of Arahura. Commonly taken by the trawlers around the North Island, but rarer farther south. Distribution: New Zealand coasts. <i>(1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/119</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

### Matangi
 
 <i>n.</i> the train type introduced to Wellington regional rail services in 2010. [= wind or breeze in Maori]

>  Shouts of acclamation, mixed with sighs of relief, rang out in the crisp air as the train was lowered onto the track. "Seeing the train on the Metlink network signalled that the Matangi had truly arrived in Wellington," says Wayne Hastie, Greater Wellington's General Manager, Public Transport. <i>(Sep 15, 2010,Wairarapa News,23</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Matangoa, <i>Huegel. Cardamine stylosa. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

### Matapia gecko
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a small brown and buff striped gecko from the top of the North Island; very agile with relatively prehensile tail. [<i>Hoplodactylus "Matapia Island"</i>]

>  New lizards found off the Northland coast include the Matapia gecko, from islets and mainland sites near Cape Reinga... <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,No. 304/18</i>)

>  Ornate skink (Gradual Decline), North Cape Pacific gecko (Gradual Decline), Matapia gecko (Gradual Decline), North Cape green gecko (Sparse), Cyclodina levidensa (regionally significant), shore skink, and common gecko. <i>(1997,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.conservation.govt.nz/upload/documents/conservation/land-and-freshwater/land/te-paki-ecological-district/te-paki-shrublands-n02-003.pdf]

### matapouri
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> FULIGULA NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. (NEW-ZEALAND SCAUP.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Papango, Tetepango, Matapouri, Titiporangi, and Raipo; `Black Teal' and `Widgeon' of the Colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,259,1888 2nd edn 2/273 ed.</i>)

### Matariki
 
 <i>n.</i> (Te Matariki) the Pleiades, the heliacal rising of which in early winter marks the Maori New Year. [Jim writes: now an occasional celebration by urban as well as rural Maori. Known to me from posters etc. Not in DNZE.]

>  One whakatauki, or proverb, describes this time as Matariki ahunga nui: Matariki, provider of plentiful food... Matariki marked a turning point in the gardening year... The appearance of Matariki is auspicious for predicting the coming year's harvests. <i>(Jun 5, 2004,Dominion,E17</i>)

>  The Maori new year started on June 23, after the first new moon following since the yearly reappearance above the horizon before dawn of the Pleiades star cluster (Maori : Matariki). <i>(Dec 17, 2001,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  My apologies.. if I did not make it sufficiently clear in describing Matariki that the celebration of a New Year marked by the winter solstice is the "tradition" that links it with Hogmanay and the Saturnalia. It did not occur to me that this might be misconstrued as a suggestion that Maori had been celebrating Matariki here for millennia. <i>(Jul 12, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  Matariki is a time for harvesting and Thornley says it is a good time to gather herbs from the bush... "It's a good time to pick pikopiko. Matariki is a time for putting the past to rest and looking forward to the future." <i>(Jun 25, 2003,Capital Times,18</i>)

>  Winter might not be the easiest time of the year to rise before dawn, but it's the only chance to see Matariki, a cluster of stars that heralds the Maori New Year... Matariki is celebrated differently by each iwi,. but the New Year begins with the first new moon after the appearance of Matariki. <i>(May 27, 2004,Wellington Contact,1</i>)

>  Towards the end of May each year the constellation Matariki, also known as the Pleiades, rises on the north-east horizon around half an hour before dawn. <i>(Aug 2001,Absolutely Positively Wellington,10</i>)

>  [Hdg] Matariki celebrations continue at Te Papa... In the last week of May, the star cluster Matariki, or Pleiades, appears in our dawn sky. For most iwi, the Maori New year begins with the first new moon after the appearance of Matariki. <i>(Jun 14, 2001,City Voice,4</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> At Te Kuiti I never saw the Pleiades because of the hills. Years afterwards, when I lived on the broad plains, I saw that heliacal rising, a handful of sparks on the horizon, until the sun blew them out. Mr Tawhai called them Te Matariki. <i>(1992,Fence Around the Cuckoo,70</i>)

>  But what is Matariki and why has this ancient tradition, which hasn't been much practised by Maori for the past 150 years, suddenly taken off again? Matariki is the Maori name for the beautiful little cluster of stars that is commonly known as the Pleiades or the Seven Sisters, Roughly translated, it means "little-eyes".... At the end of May, Matariki reappears in the morning sky, rising just before dawn... So Matariki is linked to the universal celebration of midwinter. It's the antipodean equivalent of the Scots' hogmanay or the Chinese New Year - in fact it has traditions that go back further than the Roman Saturnalia. <i>(Jun 7, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

 <i>n.</i> the ribbon-fish

### matata
 
 <i>n.</i> a shrub

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Matata, <i>Geolog. Surv. Rhabdothamnus Solandri. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  Sphenoeacus, sp. Matata. Kaitoke swamp. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>RHABDOTHAMNUS SOLANDRI, matata</i>. A slender branching shrub up to 2m tall, ... <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,158</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Matata</i> - A small dusky-coloured bird, with a white and brown spotted breast; ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),60</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a small bird associated with bracken-fern and raupo swamp. [Maori matata[a's-macron]]; also called fernbird, swamp-sparrow; <i>Bowdleria punctata</i>, fern-bird,, grass bird (also Chatham, North, Snares, South Island fernbird) fern-bird. = ko[o]ta[a]ta[a].:

>  <b>POLACK</b> The <i>matata</i> [a's-acute] is a kind of thrush; plumage brown, with feathers intermixed of a reddish tinge. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/300</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 15. <i>Sphenaeacus ? punctatus</i>. G.R.Gray, ... Mata of the natives of Tasman Bay. <i>Quoy et Gaim</i>. Matata of <i>Yate, Polack,</i> and <i>Dieffenbach</i>. Lives in the Typha swamps and amongst fern. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/189</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/189]

>  A Mauri brought me two specimens of the Matata a small bird which lives in swamps. [ed fn 1966] Matata or fern bird (<i>Bowdleia punctata</i>), a small bird of limited flight, brownish red above and white beneath, speckled with black. It is now rare as many of the swamps, its home, have been drained. <i>(Mar 15, 1842,Journal of Ensign Best,335,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The Malala [from Marlarla] is a small brown bird [fern bird] investing reedy swamps, its tail is pretty of forked feathers. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,114,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The family <i>Luscinidae </i>has five species. The Matata, a small low-flying bird with a shrill cry, and the Riro or wren, the smallest bird in New Zealand, are the only species worthy of notice. The Matata has four long and four short tail feathers, similar in texture to those of the Kiwi. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/25</i>)

>  The karimakos in full chorus in the bush and the larks and [64] matatas in the open land about us. <i>(Sep 29, 1863,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/63,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 22. SPHENOEACUS PUNCTATUS. Quoy. and Gaim. Grass Bird. Matata. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,9</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> SPHENOEACUS PUNCTATUS. (COMMON UTICK \{FERN-BIRD\}.) ... <i>Maori names.</i> Mata, Matata, Kotata, Nako, and Koroatito. ... It frequents the dense fern (<i>Pteris aquilina</i>) of the open country, ... <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,128,1888 2nd edn 1/59 ed.</i>)

>  20. <i>Sphenoecus punctatus, Quoy et Gaim</i>. Utick, Grass-bird, Matata. - At one time very commonly met with about the swamps and round the edges of lagoons where the coarse water grasses grew rankly; ... [284] Thirty years ago it was one of the most common species that affected the open country about Rockwood in the Malvern district; the lads called it `utick' from its note. <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,283</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> Some little swamp-birds (<i>matata</i>) were caught and and torn to pieces. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,329</i>)

>  <i>matata</i>: fern-bird. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> FERN-BIRD (<i>Bowdleria punctata</i>). A small brownish striped and speckled bird with curious tail feathers,...This bird has a curious double-note cry which sounds like `U-tick.' It is the Matata of the Maoris. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,86</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Spheneacus ... rufescens</i> ... Chatham Island Fern Bird. MATATA. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,72</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Spheneacus ... rufescens</i> ... Chatham Island Fern Bird. MATATA. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,72</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Ma[a]ta[a]</i> (iii) <i>Ma[a]ta[a]ta[a]</i> ... fern-bird. = ko[o]ta[a]ta[a]. <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,185</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> In North Island forests the MATATA is a common shrub distinguished at once by its handsome red-striped, pendulous, orange blossoms. It is ... known to botanists as (<i>Rhabdothamnus Solandri</i>). <i>(1961,Flora NZ,228,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> FERNBIRD <i>Bowdleria punctata Other names</i>: Matata, Tataki Thrush (Foveaux St.). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,205</i>)

>  <i>250.b. Bowdleria punctata punctata</i> (Quoy and Gaimard, 1830) <i>South Island Fernbird (Matata) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,66,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Fernbird Bowdleria punctata </i> (QUOY & GAIMARD, 1830) ...<i> North Island Fernbird B.p. vealeae </i> KEMP, 1912. <i> South Island Fernbird B.p. punctata </i> (QUOY & GAIMARD, 1830) ... <i> Codfish Island Fernbird B.p. wilsoni </i> STEAD, 1936. <i> Stewart Island fernbird B.p. stewartiana </i> OLIVER, 1930. <i> Snares fernbird B.p. caudata </i>(BULLER, 1894). EXTINCT <i> Chatham Island Fernbird B.p. rufescens </i> (BULLER, 1869). OTHER NAMES: <i> Swamp thrush, grass bird, matata. <i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,274</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> M|ata, <i>s</i>. Name of a certain bird; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,177</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a fern

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> HISTIOPTERIS ... incisa ... Water Fern. MATATA. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,13</i>)
