### match
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>she wouldn't have blown out a match </i> horse racing c/p indicating the filly finished the race without distress, breathing easily, hence fit. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,99</i>)

### match-box farming
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the annual burning of tussock to encourage fresh new growth.

>  <b>O'BRIEN</b> The days of 'match-box' farming are gone. There are 6,000 acres of good grass well fenced. <i>(1971,Te Paki Coastal Park,3</i>)

>  <b>RENNIE</b> They practised 'match-box' farming - burning the tussock annually to encourage the new, fresh growth the sheep liked eating. This led to rapid exhaustion of the nutrients in the soil, and it removed the plant cover which held the soil in place. [match-farming ] <i>(1988,Agriculture in NZ,19</i>)

### match-farming
 
 <i>n.</i> the habitual clearing land by burning-off [also MATCH-FARMER, as a derogatory term]

>  The burning this year is threefold in origin: "match-farming", to take advantage of the occasional season when fern will fire; fire from "over the hill"..; and fire due to carelessness. <i>(May 8, 1950,Evening Post,8</i>)

### match-happy
 
 <i>adj.</i> a farmer or farm-hand keen on habitualburning-off as a farming technique cf MATCH-FARMING

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> On Molesworth itself burning had ceased to be a major management practice but there was still the `match-happy' musterer. <i>(1969,Molesworth,201</i>)

### match-lined
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  The store is match-lined throughout. <i>(Jan 16, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

### match-lining
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>RENEE</b> She'd bet the kitchen'd be the only one left in New Zealand with the original rimu match-lining on the walls, although it's fairly up with the latest trends with the old wooden table and chairs in the middle of the room. <i>(1997,Snowball Waltz, The,124</i>)

>  <b>GASKELL</b> <i>(1947,Big Game</i>) [NOTES: [OEDS]]

>  <b>SALMOND</b> <i>Matchlining (Matched Lining</i>). thin tongued and grooved boarding with edges angled to form a V at joints; widely used to cover interior walls and ceilings, especially in kitchens and bathrooms. <i>(1986,Glossary of Building Terminology,232</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> You can imagine that two layers of match-lining plonked on bits of four by two as studs really don't perform any soundproofing function at all. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,191</i>)

### matcher
 
 <i>n.</i> a matching grant

>  such as chucking multinational EDS $1.5 million, and baiting super-rich America's Cup sponsors with a $30 million matcher. <i>(May 24, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

### matches
 
 <i>n.</i> a bar-room game: also matchsticks [1984 16 F E30 Pakuranga Coll. 21 MATCHES [game out of school] --]

>  At one stage during the party she was Mr Reid sitting ... playing `matchsticks' ... The game involved trying to throw a match into the other person's drink. If this happened, the loser had to drink a full glass of whisky. They called the game `skull'. <i>(Dec 12, 1979,Dominion,1</i>)

>  Other well-known [drinking] games are Hokonui Swindle, Bottles, Fluffy Ducks, Bunnies are Hovering, Cardinal Huff, All Blacks, Matches, and The Amazing Grimaldi Brothers. All these games involve either a complicated intellectual exercise, tongue twisters, mathematical calculations or aerobatics in a confined space; High Candelabras for example, involves intoxicated individuals attempting to traverse the four walls of a room without touching the floor. <i>(Mar 13, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,16</i>)

### matchhead
 
 <i>n.</i> [Kolstad says there was an entry of "matchhead moss" <i> Cladonia cristatella</i>) written but not included in Vol.3 of Dictionary of American Regional English. Kerry Carman (Sep. 2000) writes : This is a very localised Southland/Otago colloquialism for the Grape Hyacinth <i>Muscari armeniacum</i>. The only literary reference I can give you, apart from Frame, is from Invercargill writer Olive Dunn's <i>Delights of Colour in a New Zealand Garden </i>p. 16. Other Southern writers such as Essie Summers also use the term I am told but I can not supply a reference. The origin remains a mystery but it may stem from the old blue topped wax Vesta matches. Dianne Bardsley says she knew the term well from the Wairarapa, but it is not known in Taranaki.]

>  <b>SUMMERS</b> Matchheads formed a solid blue edge to the border. <i>(1976,Gold of Noon, The,142</i>)

>  Matchheads could stem from the old blue-topped wax Vesta matches - which were once widely known in the area. <i>(Dec 9, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),45</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> "...masses of spring flowers - daffodils, matchheads, crocuses, along the banks of the creek that flowed through the property." <i>(1989,Complete Autobiography, The,177</i>)

>  <b>DUNN</b> What a selection to choose from: crocus, matchheads, chionodoxa.. and nemesia. <i>(1990,Delights of Colour in a NZ Garden,16</i>)

### matchsticks
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [matches](mar-moa.md#matches))

### mate
 
 <i>n.</i> a) a habitual companion or fellow worker: `my mate and I worked the shearing sheds around here for almost 30 years'. b) hence a common form of address (= friend). -->perhaps from early whalers and sailors, adopted by goldminers and early colonial labourers `partner and friend', developing into `friend'; and also, through a form of address, to `fellow; JOKER; SPORT'. (cf MATING vbl n.) [AND 1. a. equal partner in an enterprise 1838; b. go mates 1876 2. acquaintance, person engaged in the same activity 1841 3 ... a sworn friend 1891 4. a mode of address, often to a casual acquaintance 1843

>  He [McKenzie] stated that he had no mates with him, although traces of other men could be seen... After talking with him ... the dogs began to ko-hi. Upon being questioned what he [McKenzie] was kohi-'ng for, he stated that `they were his mates,' but witness was not to be afraid, they would not hurt him. <i>(Apr 18, 1855,Lyttelton Times,6</i>)

>  ... we are now satisfied that we have a good paying claim; my mates think that now we have not less than 48 ounces which we have got in these six days. <i>(Aug 5, 1857,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

>  He asked if I or my mates intended going back to Mangawatiri. <i>(1861,McLean Papers,19/12</i>) [NOTES: (Moffitt.)]

>  <b>HARPER</b> Me and my mate here.... <i>(1861,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,64,1914 ed.</i>)

>  ... myself and mates were ahead of the others, and on coming to this place I took my `swag' and laid it on the bank. <i>(1863,AJHR,1862 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Michael Grogan, prospector.) [1862] 1863 AJ D/6/5 Pyke, Report on Goldfields]

>  <b>WALKER</b> He [the miner] was very civil, told us that two of his mates had followed a man to see if they could find a new rush, ... <i>(Jan 12, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker)</i>) [NOTES: (Central Otago)]

>  <b>HODDER</b> My mates, or `chums', [among the diggers] were a queer set. <i>(1862,Memories of NZ Life,F1</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... he was as good a mate as any man could wish for in the bush ..., and we got on very well together during the seven or eight months that we were mates. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,73</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> The pride and delight of the [shearer's] <i>mate</i> was greater than mine. <i>(1874,Station Life in NZ,104</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> These men [i.e. the floating population] usually have `mates' and are hired in pairs. [timber-mill] <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,188</i>)

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> At the time ... there were three chaps, mates, who'd been digging somewhere about the head of the Arahura, came down here timber getting. <i>(1875,Cobb's Box,5</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> [I] hunted up a mate ... [and] `wired into' the work. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,147</i>)

>  [We] fell in with an encampment ... mostly occupied by one man, the only representative of a prospecting party, which had left one of their mates behind to look after ... the horses during their absence. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,100</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> Harry and his mate had now been working in the bush districts of the North Island [on contract bushfalling] for two or three seasons. <i>Ibid.</i> 85 [He] had tried his luck for a time on the gold-fields of the West Coast, where he had picked up Big George for a mate. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,14</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> I selected as my partner a very decent young farmer who lives near ... and is at work branding our sheep all shearing-time. The pride and delight of his <i>mate</i> was much greater than my partner's; he stood near his friend prompting him through the mazes of the most extraordinary quadrille ... <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,104,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> The out-station that I went to was about seventeen miles from the head-station. ... I was particularly fortunate in having a very decent companion for a `mate'. <i>(1881,Chequered Career,36</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> `I like your mate,' I said, meaning Marion. <i>(1964,Walk on the Beach,212</i>) [NOTES: (Not for a Seagull) of a woman]

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> Uncle Bryce ... went to his best mate's house ... and got shickered on beer. <i>(1987,Redemption of Elsdon Bird, The,16,1988 ed.</i>)

>  ... thinking his mate would look after him. <i>(Aug 12, 1905,NZ Truth,8/5/6</i>)

>  Maynes had passed himself off.. as an old mate of McLennan's. <i>(Apr 26, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,6</i>)

>  He went up to him with a bottle, saying, "Mate have a drink." <i>(Oct 8, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,13</i>)

>  My best mate was sixteen. <i>(Oct 1924,Otago Witness,72</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> dead [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>FORSTER</b> A Comparative Table of the Various Languages in the Isles of the South-Sea ... [English] <i>Dead</i>, die, kill ... [New-Zeeland] matte [a-macron grave] <i>(1778,Observations Made During a Voyage,284</i>)

>  <b>BAYLY</b> [The New Zealanders] cry'd out he was Mattied, viz. kill'd; which made him & his other men rise up & attack our People. <i>(Feb 15, 1777,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb 15 Bayly's Journal in 1908 McNab Historical Records 2/220]



 
 <i>n.</i> ill health or death coming to one who has broken the law of tapu; also called Maori sickness [ORIGIN: Maori, (cf WF `matie' n. (1) + (2))]

>  <b>COLLINS</b> E-mat-tee, <i>means also</i> death <i>Sick. </i> <i>(1804,English Colony in NSW,559</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> Ill A-mattee <i>Ibid.</i> 336 Illness Mattee <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/328</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> M|ate, ...<i> s</i>. Sickness. <i>a.</i> Sick; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,178</i>)

>  My `mate' detained me twelve days at one place. [=wound] <i>(1851,McLean Papers,9/196</i>) [NOTES: (W. Colenso)]

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> <i>(1928,Where the White Man Treads</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> one whose loyalty one can depend on often used instead of PAL, etc.; often refers to a faithful friendship between two men, and the term then implies loyalty of the most constant kind; a term essential to the Australian and New Zealand vocabulary - [1951 16+ F 23 Marlborough C. 29 MATE [friendship term] -->1951 15 M 3 Marlborough C. 29 MATE -->1960 18C M B11 Nelson Boys C. 29 MATE -->1960 18C F B12 Horowhenua C. 29 MATE -->1951 15 M 23 Wellington H.S. 29 MATE (7) -->1951 16+ M 10 St Mary's, Wgton 29 MATE (12) -->1951 14-16+ M 32 St Bede's, Chch 29 MATE (22) -->1951 15 F 3 Marlborough C. 29 MATE (4) -->1951 16+ M 10 St Mary's, Wgton 29 MATE (5) -->1951 14-16+ M 26 Wellington H.S. 29 MATE (20) -->1953 14-17 M A12 Thames DHS 29 MATE [M10] -->1984 16-17F E4 Pakuranga Coll. 29 MATE [F12 M21] -->1984 14 F E122 Wgton Girls C. 29 MATE [F6]]

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> New Zealanders make the most reliable and dauntless companions there are, and in dangerous situations ... will never leave a mate, as they call one another, for a moment. <i>(1939,Present Without Leave,189</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> I never asked him any more questions either then or any other time. ... He was a great mate. <i>(1978,Twilight Hour,16</i>)

>  <i>mate</i>: pal or friend - usually closer than a mere friend; e.g., `That's my mate you just met.' <i>(1981,NZ Police National Headquarters</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Sam, a single man and now likely to remain that way, lived with a Maori [58] mate in an old cottage ... <i>(1984,Razorback,57</i>)

>  At a party with some mates a week ago, after a `fair amount of booze' [schoolboy] James was dared to `skol' half a bottle of whisky. <i>(Sep 21, 1986,Sunday Star (Auckland),A3</i>)

>  <b>FARJEON</b> `Look 'ere, mate, we've bin and let you in this 'ere scheme, and we arn't agoin' to 'ave it blown upon.' <i>(1866,Grif: a Story of Colonial Life,9</i>) [NOTES: [a criminal speaks]]

>  ...match-farming <i>(May 8, 1950,Evening Post</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i> for acquaintance or strangers in a passing exchange, esp. LISTEN (etc) MATE (cf CHIEF, SPORT) rarely MATEY [-->1951 14 M 10 Wellington H.S. 11 MATE [addressing an equal] -->1953 14-15 M A11 Thames DHS 11 MATE [4] -->1984 17 F E Pakuranga Coll. 11 MATE [F1 M9]]

>  <b>WILSON</b> Harry Prince and another betting man [are on board] ... and a young fellow who ... does not speak, and Joint is my mate [in a two-berth cabin] from Christchurch. <i>(1874,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1874 J.G.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 197]

>  One man exclaimed So help me there lies the finest horse that ever crossed the Terahmacow and all echoed him. Another said Matey never own a good horse, another said, Justly you might as well tell an overlander to drown himself at once as ride a bad horse ... Half the township would have shouted for me if I would have drank ... and I was asked all down the river if I was the man that lost a fine horse saying Well mate, I am sorry for you. Fate. <i>(Apr 30, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,179,1950 ed.</i>)

>  `See here, mate, I'll tell you what I conclude to do.' <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,3/i/77,1889, 1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> Mr George Munroe - referred to in Mr. Gillies' letter - is still residing in the Tuapeka District, and in response to my inquiries he has favoured me with an interesting account of Gabriel Read's first appearance on the scene:- ... `It was in June, 1861, that Read came, and I met him at the foot of what is now called Munroe's Gully, when he accosted me, saying - `Can you tell me, mate, where Black Peter was working?' I gave him the information required, and asked him to come and stay with me for the night, which he did. ... He found it there the next day. I came upon him working in the creek, when he sang out: `Hullo, mate! - I have got him.' And so he had. Both of us worked hard for a week or so, and got a few ounces with a tin dish. Read stayed with me during the time. He wanted me go `mates' with him and find him in `tucker', and we would keep it quiet for a few months; <i>(1887,History of the Early Gold Discoveries  in Otago,40,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> Sings out, `Are you ready to go, Jack?' `You can cast her adrift, mate,' I said./ <i>(1897,Station Ballads,53,1945 ed.</i>) [NOTES: `The New Chum's Ride']

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> We sat and talked for half an hour .../ For he [the swagger] was fairly well informed, although he called me `Mate',/ <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,62</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> In a waiting-shed he saw a blurred and shapeless figure sitting, an old tramp. ... Easy to get into conversation by offering a fill of tobacco, ... but he was disappointed when the tramp called him `Sir,' instead of `Digger' or `Mate'. <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,123,1970 ed.</i>)

>  `Mate, it's a fact: I've often thought it would be better to ... turn the flywheel by hand, ...' ... `And lummy, mate, if we get stuck we might be here for long enough. ...' <i>(1948,Our Own Country,16</i>)

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> This big joker say to me, `Listen, mate, that horse has got no show.' <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,24</i>)

>  <b>BACON</b> `But surely it'd take some time before this block was ready for settlement?' I asked. `Yeah, mate, but there's blokes got to come in here to get it ready. They [Lands and Survey Department] usually send in a block manager with half a dozen other jokers, shepherds, drivers and so on, to clear it up and get the place a bit straight for the permanent settlers. <i>(1963,In the Sticks,87</i>)

>  `I said, your ticket <i>mate</i>.' He doesnt [sic] mean friend. And my god ticket ... [sic] ... `Eighty-one cents', ... <i>(1979,Islands,325/240</i>) [NOTES: Stations on the Way ...]

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> `I suppose so. Oh yes. Too right it was.' ... `Are you thinking of Maria?' `Too bloody right I am, mate.' <i>(1980,Photo-Finish,201</i>)

### mate Maori
 
 <i>n.</i> Maori sickness

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> The sign most often taken to indicate this sort of sickness is that the sickness does not appear to be yielding readily to ordinary Maori or to pakeha methods of treatment. It is then considered to be a Maori sickness - a `mate Maori' as the phrase is commonly used - and for sure, safe, and quick results, treatment should be referred to a Maori expert, a tohunga, either male or female, who will use a combination of religious prayer, seance-possession, and magic to restore the sick person to health. <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,219</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> To break the law of<i> tapu</i> was to die of <i>mate Maori</i>, the sickness caused by an uneasy Maori conscience or the influx of demons. <i>(1963,Ratana,9,1972 ed.</i>)

>  If a Maori is genuinely disturbed, in Maori terms, the answer again is to restore their mauri (life force), as for physical illness. This is particularly true for <i>mate</i> Maori, Maori sickness, which arises from spiritual and social causes, not physical ones (Pakehas would call it psychosomatic). <i>(May 1987,Metro,100</i>)

>  <b>BINNEY et al.</b> Inexplicable sickness, <i>mate Maori</i>, is often attributed to evil forces living in the human body; their extraction, possible only by faith in the healer, is a major part of the role of a tohunga. <i>(1979,Mihaia,25</i>)

>  Her son had refused to go to hospital for treatment of his mental condition, after an earlier visit to a local doctor. It has been revealed the youth was angry over such things as social and political issues in New Zealand and South Africa, as well as the nuclear issue. There is a suggestion that the youth also suffered from a form of spiritual illness, called mate Maori. The youth had been to see a Maori spiritual healer, a tohunga, but his mother said tapu forbids her from discussing his diagnosis. <i>(Apr 13, 1986,NZ Times,3</i>)

>  In 2000, Tariana Turia, who was then associate Maori affairs minister [sic], questioned whether psychologists had the appropriate training to deal with issues such as Maori sickness (mate Maori). Victoria University's head of Maori studies [sic], Peter Adds, said mate Maori was a fairly common condition. <i>(Mar 10, 2007,Dominion Post,A1</i>)

### mate urutaa
 
 <i>n.</i> epidemic, hence Spanish flu [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Where was God when their land had been confiscated, then the mate urutaa, the death cold, ravaged their people? <i>(Jul 31, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

### mate's rates
 
 <i>n.</i> a discounted charge for services rendered, as a favour to a friend etc.

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> 'How much will he charge?' 'Mate's rates.' 'How much is that, exactly?' 'I reckon he'll do it for nothin.' <i>(1995,Mum,185</i>)

>  The spare bedroom is long overdue for a fresh lick of paint and a friend of a friend offers to do the job under-the-table at mate's rates. <i>(Aug 21, 2004,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

>  Many of the hassles arise from "mate's rate" jobs or under the counter deals... <i>(May 17, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>mates rates</i>: If you happen to be a plumber, storekeeper, paperhanger, etc. Mates rates is what you charge your friends, considerably DISCOUNTED from what you charge the general public. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,64</i>)

>  <i>mate's rates</i> pl. <i>n. N.Z. inf</i>. discounted or preferential rates of payment offered to a friend or colleague (esp. in <i>at mate's rates): he got the job done cheaply by a plumber friend at mate's rates. </i> <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,696</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 302]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>mate's rates</i> payment that may not involve tha Inland Revenue department, usually for trade work, not necessarily involving mates; eg `I can work on your boat weekends. Strictly cash. Mate's rates, eh?' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,72</i>)

>  First there are those New Zealand words [in Collins English Dictionary, 2nd ed.] ... for example, ... <i>solo mother, walk socks</i> (but not <i>walk shorts</i>),<i> block, kiwi</i> (as in Golden Kiwi), <i>mate's rates, ... Maori Warden. </i> <i>(Feb 27, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),65</i>) [NOTES: (G. Kennedy)]

### maternity home
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> ... I was born at home. This was normal procedure in those days [1912], when maternity homes were still uncommon. <i>(1983,Memoirs,15</i>)

### maternity paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a paddock put aside for farm stock about to produce young.

>  I have noticed on some farms that cows approaching calving particularly when they are run as a separate mob in a maternity paddock, are not fed as well as the dry mob. <i>(Sep 1973,NZ Dairy Exporter,57</i>)

>  Springers usually are put into a maternity paddock and go on to all grass after calving. <i>(Sep 1976,NZ Dairy Exporter,12</i>)

### maternity ward
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a lambing paddock.

>  <b>KERR</b> I would carry a sharp knife, some two-metre lengths of binder-twine, a bottle of disinfectant and raddle out to the maternity ward in the company of two reliable lambing dogs. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,77</i>)

### mateship
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... when I married and started on my own, forty men came to help me fell the first bush. ... That is the true spirit of clanship and mateship ... <i>(Sep 10, 1921,Quick March,13</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> Mateship among the diggers to some degree mitigated the hardships of gold-digging in a rough country. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,301,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GEBBIE & McGREGOR</b> One small city pub I worked in was living proof that mateship was integral to after-hours imbibing. <i>(1979,Incredible 8-Ounce Dream,46</i>)

>  Men drink with each other for mateship. To drink with the guys from the club is related to bonding and intimacy ... <i>(Oct 19, 1986,NZ Times,3</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> At home I became more of a loner. I longed for the mateship and solidarity I had experienced when working in the bush. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,64</i>)

>  ... Life is at risk, and reliance and mateship are very important ... political mateship ... <i>(Oct 11, 1989,NZ Parliamentary Debates</i>) [NOTES: 1989 Oct 11 R.J.S. Munro MP 10.10 pm]

>  Mateship during a common danger or a common struggle is in most cases mateship of the best kind throughout life. <i>(Jan 25, 1913,NZ Observer,2</i>)

>  New Zealand men were once more put in an all-male environment, and one of great stress. Alcohol and mateship helped them get through it. <i>(Sep 19, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),31</i>)

>  The cumulative effect of these brief stories, though, is revealing - mateship is just another kind of love... Which makes this book the subversive statement that Hopkins seeks; that, whatever the future might hold, male mateship will retain its exclusivity. At least till the wife calls. <i>(May 19, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),60</i>)

>  A near forgotten New Zealand which defined itself through uneasy juxtapositions of long-suffering mateship and a fierce desire to be left the hell alone. <i>(Feb 11, 2004,Capital Times,7</i>)

### matey
 
 <i>n.</i> in address, by diggers (rare in NZ) [AND 1854] [1951 15 M 23 Wellington H.S. 29 MATEY [M3] -->1951 15 F 3 Marlborough C. 29 MATEY]

>  One man exclaimed So help me there lies the finest horse that ever crossed the Terahmacow and all echoed him. Another said Matey never own a good horse, another said, Justly you might as well tell an overlander to drown himself at once as ride a bad horse ... Half the township would have shouted for me if I would have drank ... and I was asked all down the river if I was the man that lost a fine horse saying Well mate, I am sorry for you. Fate. <i>(Apr 30, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,179,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> Some one [sic] whistled, some one sang out, `Are you right there, matey [?= right, there, matey]?' `Matey!' The friendliness of it, the - the - Just to prove how happy she was, just to show the tall fellow at home how she felt, and how she despised stupid conventions, Laura took a big bite of her bread-and-butter ... She felt just like a work-girl. <i>(1922,Stories,489,1984 Alpers ed.</i>)

### Matilda
 
 <i>n.</i> swag

>  ...the poor devil [of a worker] is on the rocks, and has got to give Matilda a roll again, and swing her on to his hungry frame. <i>(Feb 17, 1906,NZ Truth,35/8/6</i>)

>  Therefore, Woollcombe, get thee hence; go home thou wowser on the wallaby; hump bluey, carry Matilda, pad the hoof, thou parsonical howler, thou Wowseristic whaler Woollcombe. <i>(Nov 6, 1909,NZ Truth,228/1/3</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Matilda</i>. See <i>waltz Matilda.</i> Among New Zealanders (-1932), gen. <i>carry Matilda. </i> <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,512</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>carry Matilda</i> to carry one's swag or `matilda'; NZ variant of Australian `waltz Matilda'. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,26</i>)

>  That night he packed up his `Matilda' <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,139</i>)

>  <b>PICARD</b> ... put my bag of tricks in the hall - haden't [sic] formed acquaintance with dear Matilda or Miss Bluey in them days - ... <i>Ibid.</i> 8 [I] may some day have to give Matilda another ride through this spot they call God's own country. Think its [sic] Dick Seddon's mostly. Gets a big hearing with the Maorilanders. <i>(1906,Some Ups and Downs in NZ&A,7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [figurative use]

>  Every [N.Z.] child born owes to the money lords of England 80 [pounds]! That's a nice little `Matilda' for a million people to carry, isn't it? <i>(Jun 9, 1911,Maoriland Worker,14/2/1</i>)

### matinee eye
 
 <i>n.</i> A person suffering from `matinee eye' would be one who had attended a considerable number of matinee film sessions thereby incurring a soreness of eyes due to eye-strain. [tdeverson]

### mating
 
 <i>n.</i> see mate v.1 [cf. KEEP COMPANY at COMPANY]

>  <b>McNAB</b> `Mating', or `la peche[e1-circumflex] par association' was common to the French as well as the Americans, but no mention of it is made among the Australians. <i>(1913,Old Whaling Days,247</i>)

### mating fit
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of ewes] in sound condition for tupping.

>  <b>STEVENS</b> Observations of these flocks suggests that during most of the summer the ewes are maintained in 'mating fit' condition which in practice is unnecessary. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,34</i>)

### matipo
 
 <i>n.</i> red matipo, mapau

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Suttonia <i>australis</i>, A.Rich. ... Nat. names, `Mapau', `Sipau', and `Matipo', Middle Island, <i>Lyall</i>. `Tipau', north of the Thames river, and `Mapua', south of that river, <i>Colenso</i>. ... A small leafy tree, very closely resembling <i>Pittosporum undulatum</i> in appearance. <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 172</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Matipo, Middle Island, <i>Lyall. Myrsine Urvillei. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i> [Suttonia australis (The Red Matipo)</i>] ... Native name <i>Mapau, Mapou,</i> or <i> Matipo</i>. The name Matipo is generally applied by Europeans to <i>Pittosporum tenuifolium,</i> but this is apparently the plant to which the Maoris attached it. This tree bears some resemblance to <i>Pittosporum tenuifolium,</i> but can be readily distinguished by the reddish colour of the leaves. [<i>Rapanea Urvillei</i>, 1907] <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,332,1940 p. 353 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> This species is largely used for the formation of ornamental hedges and is then called the `Matipo.' This is a misomer, as the Maories apparently applied this name to a species of <i>Suttonia. P. Tenuifolium</i> was called by them the <i>Tawhiwhi </i>or <i>Kohuhu.</i> <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,199</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> <i>matipou</i>, a tree (Myrsine Urvillei), commonly mispronounced matipo, the `o' with the non-Maori sound of the English diphthongal `o'; the Maori `o' is the sound of `awe' or matapo: the Maori shortening of he word is mapou. <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/153</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Red matipo .. .. matipo .. .. <i>Myrsine australis </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  Sir, - Your gardening correspondent (September 21) recommends two native trees which he calls `Lemon Matipo' and `Black-stemmed Matipo'.... The only true Matipo has red stems and is also named Mapou (Myrsine australis). Pittosporum eugenioides I have always known as Lemonwood or Tarata; P. Tenuifolium is properly called Kohuhu. <i>(Sep 19, 1975,Evening Post,2/8</i>)

>  <i>matipou n.</i> = MAPAU. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,464</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <i>(1916,NZ Association of Nurserymen, Trade Register</i>) [NOTES: (And)]



 
 <i>n.</i> 1. a usual name for black mapau. (black matipo) 2. red matipo [Maori matipou] (See [also mapau](m-mar.md#mapau))

>  The pittosporums, or matipos, take prominent place among the spring flowering trees. <i>(Oct 12, 1935,Christchurch Star-Sun,14</i>)

>  In the wild, [wekas] are grilled over a green matipo stick. <i>(Jul 3, 2004,Dominion Post,E5</i>)

>  Myrsine Urvillei Matipo <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/122</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  <b>BARKER</b> The varieties of matapo, a beautiful shrub, each leaf a study, with its beautiful tracery of black veins.... <i>(1874,Station Life in NZ,94</i>)

>  The tipau, or matipo (Pittosporum tenuifolium), makes the best ornamental hedge I know of. <i>(1879,TrNZI,21/329</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> I think we should plant a hedge inside [the fence] but the best one will be Pittosporum or as they call it here [?in NZ rather than Aust] the Matipo or the Ngaio which makes I believe a beautiful hedge ... when it does not grow straggly. <i>(May 15, 1874,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1874 May 15 J.G.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 191]

>  11. <i>Prosthemadera Novae Zealandiae</i>, Gml. Parson-bird, Poe-bird, Tui, Koko. - Often builds under the shelter of <i>Rubus</i> twines, which many of us have learnt to fear or respect as bush-lawyer ... More than once I have found it near the top of a matipo (<i>Myrsine</i>) ... <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,276</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Matipo, n</i>. another Maori name for the New Zealand trees called<i> Mapau</i> (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,289</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,94,1886 ed.</i>)

>  Broadleaf [fn <i>Griselinia littoralis</i>.], .. matipo [fn <i>Pittosporum</i>.], red matipo [fn <i>Myrsine urvillei.</i>],.. and the totara pine [fn <i>Podocarpus totara</i>.] jostle one another in the dense dry bush. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,184</i>)

>  About the beginning of 1902 a branch of the so-called matipo (<i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i>) ... was sent to me. <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/305</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> The name Matipo is generally applied by Europeans to P. tenuifolium, but [Suttonia australis] is apparently the plant to which the Maoris attached it. The tree bears some resemblance to P. tenuifolium. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,353</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The Maori went to the bush and cut down a [31] mapou - not matapo, which is neither Maori nor English - a great, heavy green trunk and carried it to Suisted's house ... <i>(1939,First Voyage of the New 'Southern Cross',30</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The under-scrub was very dense, Matipo, Ini-Ini, Konini, Pokaka, Mick-a-Mick, Ohau, Ribbonwood, Ngaio, Lemonwood and others, ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,242,1952 ed.</i>)

>  -->matipo The Maori name of a shrub or small tree which makes excellent hedges. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),253</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> Henry commented on the legs of the girls: `Too thin! Like matipo.' `Too much hino at the top!'. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,152</i>)

>  <i>matipo </i>[s] <i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i> Sol. ex Gaertn. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,47</i>)

>  <b>HASCOMBE</b> ... sweet smelling matipo trees. <i>(1969,Down and Almost Under,148</i>)

>  Let Abner the fencing contract 4 miles, 4 sods, 3 wires 4 x 3 ditch 600 matipo posts per mile, ake ake strainers and furse planted on both sides ... A 7-wire fence works out at [pounds]50.5.0. per mile. <i>(Apr 21, 1883,Chudleigh Diary,321,1950 ed.</i>)

>  Rain drummed on the iron roof of out hut, grew to a crescendo of watery thunder and stopped apart from a steady drip, drip, drip as water made its way down a drooping branch of matipo to land on a plastic fuel can a few metres from my ear. <i>(Feb 21, 2004,Dominion Post,E18</i>)

### matipo-tarata
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SMITH</b> ... on most of the rivers about here [upper Waikato], grows a [383] tree quite new to us; it is something like the matepo, and very graceful. [ed. fn] The manuscript diary adds that this tree grows like the larch. <i>(Feb 12, 1858,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Feb 12 Smith Notes of a Journey in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 382-38]

>  Matipo-tarata, black-mapau (<i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i>) <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,150</i>)

### matipou
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>matipou</i>, a tree (Myrsine Urvillei), commonly mispronounced matipo, the `o' with the non-Maori sound of the English diphthongal `o'; the Maori `o' is the sound of `awe' or matapo: the Maori shortening of the word is mapou. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/153</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

### matiri
 
 <i>n.</i> Griffin's silverfish

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> GRIFFIN'S SILVERFISH ... <i>Other names: Seriolella amplus</i>; blue-jaw, bonito; matiri (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,223</i>)

### matiwhitu
 
 <i>n.</i> a fish

>  <b>HECTOR</b> It may be useful to give a list of the different fishes the Maoris distinguish in the Taupo district, ... Matiwhitu. - A red Kokopu. <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,129</i>)

### matook
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [matuku](mar-moa.md#matuku))

### matric
 
 <i>n.</i> short for matriculation

>  The incentive to obtain licensed interpreters' certificates and Maori being made a subject for the Civil Service Entrance Exams formed the first outside step. Then followed the Matric. <i>(Mar 6, 1929,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/182,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Buck)]

>  His [Burchfield's] 15 nieces and nephews here were talking about taking `U.E.' - it was `matric' when he left [in 1949] <i>(1954,Evening Post</i>)

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> [He] was in the lower fifth form [in the 1930s-1940s] which entitled him to have a tilt at the University Entrance examination known generally and erroneously in those days as 'Matric'. <i>(1974,Predicament,18,1981 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GLOVER</b> <i>(1962,Hot Water Sailor,45,1981 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [1920's]]

>  <b>CHISHOLME</b> In 5A his results were ... unimpressive. He did ... pass his `matric' ...Mandatory military cadet parades were a necessary ... part of college life. <i>(1987,From the Heart,10</i>)

>  <b>CRANE</b> He found life very full ... two hours' teaching a 'matric' (University Entrance) Evening Class in English <i>(1986,I Can Do No Other,212</i>)

>  I was to be the first orphanage child to sit and pass the university matriculation examination, commonly known as "matric". <i>(Feb 5, 1977,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Then , again, I've heard young men cursing the day they left the city to come to the back-country, wondering why they didn't work harder at school in their matric. year, or why they didn't take up a trade. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,21</i>)

>  Once in every one and a-half years. on an average, there was a row about one or more "Matric" papers. <i>(Dec 3, 1947,NZ Observer,21</i>)

>  His nieces and nephews here were talking about taking "U.E." - it was "matric" when he left. <i>(Mar 3, 1963,Evening Post,17</i>)

### matricary
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> SCENTLESS CHAMOMILE (<i>Matricaria inodora</i>) is often called dog daisy, and also matricary and mayweed. It is about as common as the former species [oxeye daisy], and grows in similar places. It is inodorous, and the flowers are about 2 inches across. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,176,1967, ed.</i>)

### matriculate
 
 <i>v.</i> a) to be accepted as qualified to enter a university. b) to pass an examination designed as a university entrance qualification.

>  <i>Peter</i>, survey chainman, matriculated, rep-footballer: ... <i>(Jun 1953,Here & Now,29</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  When he matriculated, he hadn't done as everyone expected and gone to the city to enrol at university. <i>(Dec 1956,Landfall,10(4), No. 40 : 286</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> My sister Dororthy went to Nelson College for Girls and matriculated and came home, and she passed what was called a 'D' certificate, the first of the teaching certificates. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,176</i>)

### matriculated
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  If the matriculation sufficeth, why go to the expense and trouble of having a `D' examination at all? Or again, why does the Matriculated student receive his partial `D', while the candidate who has passed ... is denied Matriculation privileges. <i>(1901,NZ Journal of Education,3/89</i>)

### matriculation
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There is hereby established a University Entrance Board.. whose duty shall be to consider the curricula and courses of study at secondary schools in relation to the educational and other requirements for matriculation at any constituent college. <i>(1926,NZ Statutes,17 Geo. V 70/742</i>)

>  Specimen Examination Papers EUCLID <i>For Matriculation.</i> <i>(1901,NZ Journal of Education,3/46</i>)

>  I am at present swatting for Public Entrance and Matriculation examinations. <i>(Oct 28, 1924,Otago Witness,76</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  If the matriculation sufficeth, why go to the expense and trouble of having a `D' examination at all? Or again, why does the Matriculated student receive his partial `D', while the candidate who has passed ... is denied Matriculation privileges. <i>(1901,NZ Journal of Education,3/89</i>)

>  "This will be the business man's matriculation certificate," declared Mr. R. Masters, Minister of Education, when the regulations were published of the School Certificate Examination. <i>(Apr 12, 1934,Feilding Star,4</i>)

### matriculation examination
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The `D' certificate ... can be obtained through two different examinations: the ordinary departmental `D' examination and the Matriculation examination of the New Zealand University, with a few subjects specially concerning teachers to be passed in. <i>(1901,NZ Journal of Education,3/89</i>)

### matriculation paper
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A correspondent sends me the matriculation paper in geography. <i>(Dec 18, 1890,Otago Witness,21</i>)

### matrimonial property
 
 <i>n.</i> That part of the property of husband and wife which is subject to division between them on the breakdown of the marriage, and may be divided on the insolvency of either, in contrast to <i>separate property</i>.

### matrix gold
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> Regulations for the working of matrix Gold combined with Quartz or any other rock, remaining in its original place of deposit, will be published as soon as conveniently may be, and as occasion may require. [160] ... All alluvial or matrix Gold procured without due authority ... will be liable to be seized, ... <i>(1853,Auckland,159</i>) [NOTES: 1852 Coromandel Gold Field. Provisional Regulations in 1853 Swainson Auckland 159]

>  On the resumed diggings, the parties ... washed [7 1/4oz of dust] ... the quantity set forth, and are continuing their work with every prospect of finding eventually the matrix Gold, which the nuggets now obtained indicate to be in the vicinity of the workings. <i>(Jan 12, 1853,NZ Government Gazette,6/1/2</i>) [NOTES: (Heaphy's Report from Coromandel)]

>  This would point to the mammillated appearance being caused by a certain amount of attrition on the rough, pointed, matrix gold. <i>(1890,TrNZI,22/403</i>)

### matrix of dysfunctionalism
 
 <i>phr.</i> a phrase used by Minister Parekura Horomia

>  Just how serious this "matrix of dysfunctionalism" - to coin a favoured Horomia phrase - is, depends on who you talk to. <i>(Jun 27, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

### matron
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sl.] a very old cow.

>  a very old cow - matron. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a breeding ewe.

>  <b>GORDON</b> This is the first time since I've been on Glenburn that we've been free of wethers in September and it's a great way to go. We're selling on the top of the market and it frees up the country for the matrons. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,72</i>)

### Matter of National Importance
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  The amendments [to the resources Management Act] elevate historic heritage to a Matter of National Importance, giving it the same recognition afforded such treasures as the coastal environment and areas of significant native flora and fauna. Anyone operating under the resources Management Act must recognise and provide for the protection of historic heritage from inappropriate subdivision, use and development. <i>(Sep 2003,NZ Heritage,No. 90/9</i>)

### mattery-eye
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a disease of potatoes caused by Phytomonas solanaceara contamination.

>  In that district [Pukekohe] great damage has recently been occasioned by a bacterial disease popularly known as 'gravy-eye' or mattery-eye.' <i>(May 21, 1917,NZ Journal of Agriculture,357</i>)

>  The diseases of the second group, usually termed "wilts" are due to the following organisms .. mattery-eye (a bacterial disease due to Phytomonas Solanaceara). Mattery-eye is expressed in an aggravated form, wilt of the whole plant being rapidly followed by its death. <i>(Jan 21, 1929,NZ Journal of Agriculture,6</i>)

### mattifying
 
 <i>adj.</i> [On hair lotion bottle : contains mattifying component]

### mattress wrapping
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He was seeking a judicial review of last week's decision.. not to include mattress wrapping in its recommendations for preventing cot death... "Currently available evidence does not support mattress wrapping as a preventative strategy for SIDS," Dr Durham said. <i>(Aug 27, 1997,Dominion,9</i>)

### matty
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sticky sheep with matted wool difficult to shear.

>  a sticky sheep (shearing) - a matty. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### matua
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand-bred prairie grass cultivar. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  He has 13 hectares of Matua prairie grass and practically had tears in his eyes when telling us how good it is in a dry summer. His Matua Tarua red clover mixture is good for extending the lactation period in the autumn. <i>(Sep 1985,NZ Journal of Agriculture,17</i>)

>  Some examples [of adaptive cultivars] are matua prairie grass, nui perennial ryegrass, pawera red clover, pitau and tahora white clover, oranga lucerne, kara and wana cocksfoot. <i>(Sep 1985,NZ Dairy Exporter,66</i>)

>  Twenty-four hectares of rape and turnips which would have been sown in wheat in August will instead go into matua prairie grass and pawera red clover. <i>(Sep 1986,NZ Journal of Agriculture,8</i>)

>  The aim of including matua with nui, pawera and piau in a 4-species mix is to grow more grass in autumn-winter ... <i>(Nov 1987,NZ Dairy Exporter,18</i>)

>  It may be possible to maintain the feeding value of Matua prairie grass pastures at higher herbage masses than with rye-grass pastures. <i>(Feb 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,35</i>)

>  Tolerates low-fertility soils better than Matua prairie grass. <i>(1995,Grasslands Range of Forage and Conservation Plants,23</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> parent; father; elder [ORIGIN: Maori, compare Samoan cognate matua]

>  Hohepa added, `Let them have a king. Let us have a matua (father).' <i>(Jun 3, 1858,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1858 June 3 in 1860 Buddle Maori King Movement 14]

>  Pouroto Ngaropo was our matua kaiako... <i>(Oct 2006,Pu Kaea,4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a Samoan or other Pacific elder (= kaumatua) [ORIGIN: Samoan]

>  Health issues of special concern to older Pacific people are discussed at fono held twice a year in Christchurch. The last fono...attracted over 50 people, including 33 older Pacific people (matua). ... Matua heard that the population of Pacific peoples is different from other ethnic groups in New Zealand because more than 50 per cent is aged less than 25 years. <i>(Feb 2008,Keeping On,57:18</i>)

### matua whangai
 
 <i>n.</i> a foster-parent, adoptive parent [ORIGIN: Maori, http://www.learningmedia.co.nz/nz/online/ngata/app2.htm]

>  Maori welfare workers were also critical of the judge. They believe Wells would be better served by matua whangai rather than another prison term. Matua whangai is a Government-sponsored scheme designed to assist young Maori offenders and keep them out of penal institutions. <i>(Jun 15, 1986,NZ Times,3</i>)

>  Suddenly, on December 17, 2005. Matua whangai of Zallen. <i>(Dec 19, 2005,NZ Herald,C7</i>)

### matuakumara
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Matuakumara, <i>Col[enso]. Geranium dissectum.</i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> <i>G[eranium] dissectum</i>, L., A. var. <i>Carolinianum</i>, Fl. N.Z. ... The `Matuakumera' of the North Island Maori: a term, however, more generally applied to the tuberous edible root. Colenso also assigns the Maori name `Pinakitere'; but it does not clearly appear whether to the type, or to which of its New Zealand forms. <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,62</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> GERANIUM DISSECTUM (<i>Linn.</i>) ... The `MATUAKUMARA'.- This plant is abundant throughout both Islands, ... [72] The native name of the plant ... means ... `the father or parent of the Kumara'; and, as it grows prolifically in their plantations, the Maoris may in some manner believe that its presence augurs well for ... the Kumara. <i>Ibid.</i> 178 Matuakumara [Maori name] .. Geranium [Settlers' name] .. Geranium dissectum .. Herb <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,71 ed.</i>)

### matuawhapuku
 
 <i>n.</i> red rock cod, red scorpionfish, Jock Stewart

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>SEA PERCH (Jock Stewart, Matua-whapuku)</i> Helicolenus <i>percoides (Helicolenus papilosus)</i> ... Sea perch are similar in shape and appearance to the scorpion fish but have smoother heads ... It is a deep water species ... more common to the south of Cook Strait. ... In deep southern waters sea perch are a fairly uniform pale yellow-brown with a white belly and further work may show this to be a separate species. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,196,1984 ed.</i>)

>  SCORPAENA CARDINALIS Richardson. <i>Red Rock-cod; Matuawhapuku.</i> Very common throughout the year in rocky localities around North Auckland Peninsula, but not greatly utilized as a food fish. Distribution: A northern species; occasionally seen on the northern and western coasts of the South Island. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/121</i>)

>  <i>Scorpaena cruenta</i> Richardson. RED ROCK-COD; MATUAWHAPUKU. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,53,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> This species is known under several names, but the name Red Rock Cod is the one most generally used. Unfortunately this name is not even remotely related to the true Cods of the family <i>Gadidae. </i> <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,164</i>) [NOTES: [check this cit]]

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> RED SCORPIONFISH ... <i>Other names: Scorpaena cardinalis</i>; red rock cod, scarpee, grandfather hapuku, cobbler; red rock cod (Australia); matua-whapuku (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,288</i>)

### matuhi
 
 <i>n.</i> bush wren; a wren-like bird of the New Zealand bush. [Maori matuhi(tuhi)[a-macron]]

>  <b>BULLER</b> XENICUS LONGPIPES. (BUSH-WREN.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Matukituki, Piwauwau, \{Puano,\} and Huru-pounamu. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,115,1888 2nd edn 1/108 ed.</i>)

>  <i>matuhi</i>: bush wren. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> In addition to the rock wren, there is another species, <i>Xenicus longiceps</i>, the bush wren or matuhi. This bird is also very small, of darker green colouring and with a white throat. It is found in damp rimu and beech forests on the West Coast of the South Island, and builds its nest in hollow trees and, like the rock wren, lines it with feathers. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/689 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BUSH WREN <i>Xenicus longpipes Other names</i>: Matuhi, Tom Thumb Bird (Stewart Is.). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,192</i>)

>  <i>Bush Wren Xenicus longipes</i> (GMELIN, 1789) ... This tiny, inconspicuous bird may be extinct; ... <i>South Island Bush Wren X.l. longpipes</i> (GMELIN, 1789) <i>North Island Bush Wren X.l. stokesii </i>GRAY, 1862. <i>Stead's Bush Wren X.l. variabilis</i> STEAD, 1936. OTHER NAMES: <i>Matuhituhi, green wren, Tom Thumb bird</i>. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,265</i>)

### matuka
 
 <i>n.</i> possibly for matuku, bittern; no Maori word <i>matuka.</i>

>  <b>WILSON</b> The fact that large, wet flies tied in a particular way to imitate small fish are known by three different names causes a lot of confusion. In America, England and elsewhere they are called `streamer' flies; in New Zealand they are called `matukas'; in Australia, `longtails'. As patterns from all three countries are used here, the confusion can be understood. If the angler remembers why each name is used, the confusion disappears. ... / The word `matuka', as used for the New Zealand patterns, is the name of a bird (now protected) whose feathers were first used to tie this kind of wet fly. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,83</i>)

### matukituki
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [matuhi(tuhi)](mar-moa.md#matuhi)))

### Matukituki whitebait
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McDOWALL</b> For many years "whitebait" were fished for in Lake Wanaka as "Matukituki whitebait"... "we used to go whitebaiting in the Matukituki River.." <i>(1984,NZ Whitebait Book, The,14</i>)

### matuku
 
 <i>n.</i> the bittern

>  The Maori distinguished these two according to their habitat: 1, matuku-hurepo (swamp matuku) and 2, matuku-moana (sea-matuku). When the Pakeha mentions the matuku he always refers to the bittern; the other he always calls blue-heron. <i>(1945,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/153</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Mat|uku,<i> s</i>. A bird so called. [poss. BLUE HERON, as there is a separate entry for matuku urepo] <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,178</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 52. <i>Botaurus melanotus</i>. ... Blackish brown on back, ... Matuku of the natives. From a specimen found on the Hokianga River. <i>Miss R. Stone. Dieffenbach.</i> Matuku urepo of the natives, or Crane of <i>Yate</i>. Also found on the Murray, South Australia. Mr. Fortnum. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/196</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Three of the <i>Heron</i> family live on the land. The Matuku, or bittern, has the cry of a bull. The Kotuku, or white crane, the noblest bird of this family, is abundant in the Middle Island, but is rarely seen north of Wanganui in the North Island. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/27</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Matuku (<i>Botaurus poiciloptilus</i>) is a bittern, long-legged and billed. ... The Maori are expert at catching them but I cannot say that bittern meat is good. There is a smaller species of bittern, a blue heron, and possibly others of the family, all known under the common name of matuku. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/221</i>)

>  73. <i>Ardea poeciloptera</i>, Wagl. Bittern, Matuku. <i>(Nov 1885,NZ Journal of Science,557</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The most noticeable absentee from the birds recorded [in Maori food lists c1880] is the koreke or quail, once extremely plentiful. ... The matuku, or bittern, once common enough is not mentioned, nor is the koka or crow, nor any of the smaller birds. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,64</i>)

>  <i>matuku,</i> two birds, 1, Botaurus poeciloptilus, bittern, and 2, Demigretta sacra, blue-heron; the Maori distinguished these two according to their habitat: 1, matuku-hurepo (swamp matuku) and 2, matuku-moana (sea-matuku). When the Pakeha mentions the matuku he always refers to the bittern; the other he always calls blue-heron. <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/153</i>) [NOTES: J.C.Andersen]

>  <i>matuku</i>: bittern. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> AUSTRALIAN BROWN BITTERN <i>Botaurus poiciloptilus Other names:</i> Brown Bittern, Black-backed Bittern, Matuku, Matuku-hurepo. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,79</i>)

>  <i>97. Botaurus stellaris poiciloptilus</i> (Wagler, 1827) <i>Austral[as]ian Bittern (Matuku) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,35,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND</b> Listen to the sullen matuku, the bittern that bellows in the swamp. <i>(1906,Life & Work of Seddon,374</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> blue heron, reef heron

>  In the afn. while we [were] sitting on the beach ... a matuku, a small blue heron, came down near us. I have not seen one for several years. <i>(Nov 23, 1862,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/803,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  Ardea Matook. Heron. Matuku. Port Fitzroy. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  matuku, two birds, 1, Botaurus poeciloptilus, bittern, and 2, Demigretta sacra, blue-heron; the Maori distinguished these two according to their habitat: 1, matuku-hurepo (swamp matuku) and 2, matuku-moana (sea-matuku). When the Pakeha mentions the matuku he always refers to the bittern; the other he always calls blue-heron. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/153</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  This he calld <i>Aeheino mouwe</i> a name m[an]y others before had call'd it by. <i>(Jan 31, 1770,Cook's Journals,1/243</i>) [NOTES: [cf Beaglehole's note on this]]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 53. <i>Herodias matook</i>. ... Matook of the natives of Queen Charlotte's Sound. <i>Forst</i>. Matou cou of the natives. <i>Less. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/196</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/196]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The Matukutuku, or Crane, of this there are several kinds in New Zealand; the blue crane or that which inhabits the seashore is common to every one ... <i>(1844,NZ Journal,114,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 72. ARDEA SACRA. Gml. Blue Heron. Matuku <i>A. Matook.</i> GRAY <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,27</i>)

>  71. <i>Ardea sacra</i>, Gml. Blue, Purple, or Reef heron, Matuku. - May be observed alighting on reefs immediately on the ebb of the tide so that the birds stand in the water. <i>(Nov 1885,NZ Journal of Science,557</i>) [NOTES: T.H. Potts]

>  <b>BULLER</b> ARDEA SACRA. (BLUE HERON.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Matuku-tai, Matuku-nuia, and Matukutuku. ... The Blue Heron is not confined to New Zealand, but is found along the whole of the Australian coasts and throughout the Polynesian archipelago; ... <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/129</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> white-faced heron

>  70. <i>Ardea novae-hollandiae</i>, Lath. White-fronted heron, Matuku.It is far from common in this district. <i>(Nov 1885,NZ Journal of Science,557</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> a wet fly (? orig from bittern's feathers)

>  I quickly put on a matuku; the largest wet fly in the box. <i>(1950,Landfall,16/306</i>) [NOTES: (P.J. Wilson)]

### matuku-hurepo
 
 <i>n.</i> bittern (see also matuku: =`swamp-matuku'), <i>Botaurus poiciloptilus, brown bittern</i>

>  Mat|uku Urepo; [A bird so called.] <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,178</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Matuku urepo</i> - This bird is a species of crane; ... The top of the head is covered with slight bristles; and the back of the scull [sic] is of a red colour, perfectly bald. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),67</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/196</i>) [NOTES: see MATUKU 1. 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/196]

>  Botaurus poicilopterus. Matuku-hurepo. Bittern. Kaitoke swamp. Rare, not seen by me. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> BOTAURUS POECILOPTILUS. (BLACK-BACKED BITTERN.) ... <i>Native name.</i>- Matuku-hurepo. ... [142] The Common Bittern is very generally distributed ... in places suited to its habits of life, such as raupo swamps, sedgy lagoons, and those `blind creeks', covered over with a growth of reeds ands tangle, which are so numerous in all the low districts. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/141</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> BROWN BITTERN (<i>Botaurus poiciloptilus</i>), Matuku-hurepo of the Maoris, is a bird of the swamps and lagoons throughout the country, but now less common than formerly owing to the advance of settlement. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,79</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> AUSTRALIAN BROWN BITTERN <i>Botaurus poiciloptilus Other names</i>: Brown Bittern, Black-backed Bittern, Matuku, Matuku-hurepo. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,79</i>)

>  <i>Australasian Bittern Botauris stellaris poiciloptilus</i> (WAGLER, 1827) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Australian brown bittern, brown bittern, matuku-hurepo. ... Also New Caledonia, southeastern and southwestern Australia and Tasmania. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,135</i>)

>  BOTAURUS POICILOPTERUS, Wagl. Matukuhurepo. Bittern. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/69</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 74. ARDEA POICELOPTERA. Wagl. Bittern. Matukuhurepo. <i>Botaurus poicilopterus.</i> GRAY. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,28</i>)

### matweed
 
 <i>n.</i> wireweed

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> WIREWEED (<i>Polygonum aviculare</i>), also called road weed, mat weed, knot grass, [pig weed,] and hog weed, is common in cultivated land all over the country. It is an annual, with stems up to 2ft. in length trailing over the ground. The stems have swollen nodes (`knot grass') and are very tough (`wireweed'). <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,61,1967, p. 61 ed.</i>)

>  <i>matweed [=] wild portulaca: wireweed</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,47</i>)

### Maui
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The winners of today, in common with winners such as Odysseus and Maui in more heroic times, owe their victories as often as not to treachery, cunning and deceit. <i>(Jan 16, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>(1941,NZ Slang</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>(1941,NZ Slang</i>)

>  Maui The legendary hero of Maori tales who pulled the North Island up out of the sea. -- <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),253</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,315</i>)

>  "The kaupapa have been so supportive, we are there to uplift Maui and his stories and epicness as a great ancestor..." <i>(May 20, 2005,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> The first hero was the mythical Maui, who pulled up Te Ika a Maui, the Great Fish of Maui, which was to become the country that Kupe named Aotearoa. <i>(2000,Maori Heroes,14</i>)

>  On the East Coast, it is said that Maui's canoe, turned to stone, can be seen on the summit. <i>(Mar 25, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Geology) Maui gas, the Maui gasfield itself, or pertaining to these

>  [heading] <b>Maui Reserves</b> Immediately after becoming Director, staff of the Petroleum & Basin Studies Section informed me that they, on the information available to them, had identified that the Maui gas reserves were substantially less than published figures indicated... <i>(Mar 1998,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,115/31</i>)

>  District geologist Graham Mansergh is preoccupied with local engineering geological problems, in particular the various hazards awaiting the Maui pipeline in this part of the world. <i>(Oct 1980,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,50/44</i>)

>  The Geological society of New Zealand has considered the Ministry's discussion paper on the future uses of Maui gas and makes the following observations . . . . <i>(Mar 1986,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,71/11</i>)

>  P. Suggate asked if a Society submission on the future uses of Maui gas was prepared and what usage options it supported. <i>(Mar 1986,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,71/16</i>)

>  My office concluded that once it was known how the maximum recoverable gas could be extracted and in what time span, decisions could be made to ensure optimum use of the Maui gas resource and when a transition strategy to other gas fields of other fuels would be needed. <i>(Jun 1989,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,84/59</i>)

>  Our energy demands have been rising dramatically, met to a large part by Maui gas. ... We all know that gas is a non-renewable resource and that Maui is due to run out in about 10 years. <i>(Mar 1994,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,103/1,2</i>) [NOTES: nb use of 'Maui' without 'gas' in latter citation]

>  In the case of Maui and any future economic discovery, the Government is now very heavily involved in all development phases through Offshore Mining. <i>(Oct 1977,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,44/7</i>)

### Maui's dolphin
 
 <i>n.</i> a type of endangered dolphin, formerly known as the North Island Hector's dolphin.

>  Commercial fishermen have reacted angrily to a new ban on set nets in inshore waters around the North Island's upper west coast to protect the endangered Maui's dolphin. .. Since March 2001, six dead Maui's dolphins have been found between Manukau Harbour and the Waikato River... <i>(Jan 25, 2003,Dominion Post,A6</i>)

>  The groups have called for Doc to first develop recovery plans for Maui's and Hector's dolphin. <i>(Feb 22, 2004,Sunday Star Times,A11</i>)

>  Maui's dolphin is the world's smallest dolphin and found only in New Zealand. <i>(Jun 2007,Conservation Action,57</i>)

>  The death of a rare Maui's dolphin off the west coast of Auckland this week had driven the species closer to extinction, Forest and Bird said yesterday. <i>(Jun 7, 2003,Dominion Post,A4</i>)

>  More than 12,000 hector's and maui's dolphins are estimated to have been killed in fishing nets since 1970. Due to their slow breeding rate and low population maui's dolphin are on the critically endangered list. <i>(Jan 10, 2007,NZ Herald,A7</i>) [NOTES: lower case hector and maui]

>  The Maui's dolphin used to be called the North island Hector's dolphin. <i>(Apr 24, 2007,NZ Herald,A3</i>)

### mauku
 
 <i>n.</i> a fern

>  <b>COWAN</b> `It was usual, too,' added the old<i> pakeha-Maori</i>, `to cook some <i>pikopiko,</i> the young curly fronds of the <i>mauku</i> or ground-fern, with the [human] meat; it added to the flavour. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/428 A rather good bush vegetable is the <i>pikopiko</i>, the curled shoots of the <i>mauku</i> fern. The natives have long given up the use of these bush foods; ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/219,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FIRTH</b> The fleshy bracts of the root-climbing <i>tawhara</i>, the <i>perei</i> orchid,the young fronds of the <i>mauku</i> ... also furnished vegetable food. <i>(1929,Primitive Economics of NZ Maori,51</i>)

>  <b>FIRTH</b> The fleshy bracts of the root-climbing <i>tawhara</i>, the <i>perei</i> orchid,the young fronds of the <i>mauku</i> ... also furnished vegetable food. <i>(1929,Primitive Economics of NZ Maori,51</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Asplenium bulbiferum</i> Forst f. Common name: <i>Hen and chickens fern</i> Maori names: <i>Mauku, mouku</i> ... `Mauku' has been identified as <i>Asplenium bulbiferum, Cordyline pumilio, </i>and <i>Hymenophyllum</i> spp. by Williams and as <i>Asplenium bulbiferum</i> by Best. `Mouku' has been identified as <i>Asplenium bulbiferum</i> by Williams and as <i>Marattia salicina</i> by Tregear. <i>Asplenium bulbiferum </i>is a common fern throughout New Zealand forests. <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,25</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Asplenium bulbiferum</i> HEN AND CHICKENS FERN ... Mauku ... most fronds sprouting tiny plants (bulbils) which drop off ... <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,36</i>)

### mauri
 
 <i>n.</i> sacred stones

>  <b>JENKIN</b> In most if not all Maori villages, sacred stones or<i> mauri</i> were deposited. <i>(1970,NZ Mysteries,105</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a plant

>  <b>20</b> <i>Method of Cooking</i>. Kokopu are cooked in small baskets called <i>tapora</i>, formed by plaiting leaves of mauri or kokaha (both Astelia), or some similar leaves. <i>(1940,Fishes NZ</i>) [NOTES: nf Wms]



 
 <i>n.</i> spirit; the life force or principle. [Maori]

>  In a moving ceremony at the marae, it was granted back to the soldiers to carry the mauri (spirit) of their fallen comrades back to Tuahiwi. <i>(Oct 3, 2001,Evening Post,25</i>)

>  No doubt the face presented by the ensemble of marae and camp organisation, ... together with the mauri of the meeting of diverse, scattered tribal elements, might be characterised as excellent ... <i>(Jul 20, 1936,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/227</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  He also held the title of mauri, `life and soul' of those tribes and of the Ringatu Church. <i>(Jul 6, 1983,Dominion,4</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> 374 Mauri/Mauriora = Life principle, thymos of humans; talisman or material symbol of that secret and mysterious principle protecting the mana (power/vitality) of people, birds, land, forests, whatever ... [sic] <i>(1983,Bone People, The,468</i>)

>  <b>HOWE</b> Mauri - ethos, life-force or principle, life-style, belief. This term applies to both animate an inanimate things. Houses, land, trees, forests, buildings, hills etc. can have a life-force of their own. So can the front (atea) of a marae, a speech, the kumara, or the taniwha of a river. Maoris strongly believe in mauri, and just as a life force may wax or wane depending on a person's health or status, so can his or her `mauri'. Lack of mauri can cause a person to die. <i>(1985,Towards Taha Maori,21</i>)

>  If a Maori is genuinely disturbed, in Maori terms, the answer again is to restore their mauri (life force), as for physical illness. This is particularly true for <i>mate</i> Maori, Maori sickness, which arises from spiritual and social causes, not physical ones (Pakehas would call it psychosomatic). <i>(May 1987,Metro,100</i>)

>  The mind of every Maori is well stored with those different `karakia' ... to protect the life principle (Mauri) of man. <i>(Jul 1910,Kai Tiaki,104</i>)

>  The mind of every Maori is well stored with those different `karakia' ... to protect the life principle (Mauri) of man. <i>(Jul 1910,Kai Tiaki,104</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Folk from both Waikato and Auckland were upset at the mixing of mauri for no good reason that they could see. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,96</i>)

>  [The Waitangi Tribunal] stated that a river may be a taonga as a valuable resource, and that its "mauri" or "life force" is another taonga. <i>(Jun 2, 2004,MediaSearch</i>) [NOTES: The Press]

>  The 'Canes caned the Crusaders 37-20 in a superb victory. The shame is, of course, that this was the last game of the season and the 'Canes wind up languishing in eleventh. <i>(2004,Salient,10/49</i>)

>  We've all heard of the Life Force, the mysterious energy or divine light which illuminates and enlivens the soul and gets Luke Skywalker out of trouble... In China it goes under the name Chi, in Japan as Ki, in India as Prana, in French-speaking countries as Elan Vital and among Maori it's called Mauri. <i>(Nov 10, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  The mauri, its spirituality, never left us. <i>(Mar 25, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

>  The target of makutu was the mauri (life force). <i>(Dec 6, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),86</i>)

>  My maunga images come from living in the shadow of the mountain almost all my life. I feel it as a life force. It is not just the cold, it has a mauri that I miss when I am away. <i>(Jan 2004,NZ Geographic,No. 67/53</i>)

### may
 
 <i>n.</i> OED, <i>Crataegus Oxyacantha</i> blossoms of the hawthorn; hence occas the tree itself : so called becouse it blooms in the mouth of May. The word is inappropriate to NZ seasons, so <i>hawthorn</i> is used exclusively. <i>Hawthorn</i> bushes themselves are relatively uncommon, and the term is often (mistakenly) extended to include <i>barberry. </i>

### mayweed
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>hil</b> SCENTLESS CHAMOMILE (<i>Matricaria inodora</i>) is often called dog daisy, and also matricary and mayweed. It is about as common as the former species [oxeye daisy], and grows in similar places. It is inodorous, and the flowers are about 2 inches across. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> SCENTLESS CHAMOMILE (<i>Matricaria inodora</i>) is often called dog daisy, and also matricary and mayweed. It is about as common as the former species [oxeye daisy], and grows in similar places. It is inodorous, and the flowers are about 2 inches across. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,176,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>mayweed [=] corn chamomile: rayless chamomile: scentless chamomile: stinking mayweed: wild chamomile</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,47</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>mayweed, Austrian [s] Anthemis austriaca</i> Jacq. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,47</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> mayweed, chamomile, stinking chamomile, fetid chamomile

>  <i>mayweed, stinking</i>[s]<i> Anthemis cotula</i> L. <i>Ibid.</i> 75 <i> stinking mayweed</i> [s] <i>Anthemis cotula</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,47</i>)

### ME
 
 <i>abbr.</i> myalgic encephalitis (See [tapanui flu](t-ten.md#tapanui-flu))

<b>1</b> <i>abbr.</i> (Rural) metabolic energy per kg of dry matter [ an indication of feed value of stockfood.]

>  For a lamb, an ME of 8.5 is maintenance level. No matter what else you do, if the ME of your pasture is 8.5, lambs won't grow. Even if your ME is at a good level, your stocking rate must be optimum. <i>(Nov 4, 1999,NZ Farmer,15</i>)

>  Although bales now come in all shapes and sizes, the product inside is what counts. Quality is measured in the percentage of ME - metabolisable energy, which the cow can convert to milk solids .. It costs just as much to harvest low quality feed as good, and the cows need to eat more, so the cost is higher with a lower ME crop. <i>(Oct 18, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,23</i>)

To be fully utilised grass has to be eaten at its most nutritious stage and the idea of this 'mechanical cow' is that the herd is grazed over the pasture at its best stage, but when growth begins to outstrip the herd, the 'mechanical cow' - the forage harvester - is brought out and run out over these paddocks. <i>(Apr 17, 1958,NZ Farmer,4</i>)

Mediacow is a social and economic liberal." <i>(Mar 2004,North & South,93</i>)

### medical centre
 
 <i>n.</i> a group practice of two or more doctors, sometimes with specialist, ancillary or para-medical service. [Jim Cameron writes : See Yellow Pages. There are also places called <i>Health Centres</i>. The definition of a health centre in NSOD and Collins, is premises owned by a local authority providing health care, usually housing a group practice, nursing staff, etc. premises. OED defines it as a local centre for a group practice. It seems to be used loosely in NZ, sometimes simply as a variant of medical centre, sometimes in relation to para-medical services eg <i>Chiropractic Health Centre</i>. Medical centre is not in any dictionary. But see use in Ruth Rendell <i>Harm Done</i>. [medical centre is given as illustr. at medical in ODE - TD]]

>  <b>BAKER</b> George, a GP who had lived through the fever and as soon as he could had moved back home from Taupo where he'd been a partner in a medical centre, wouldn't let Sean get up. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,122</i>)
