### meeting house
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori meeting house: the concept is post-European: distinguish from earlier CARVED HOUSE, which often seemed to be a chiefly dwelling or used for a special purpose: check Cook etc. call them CHIEF'S HOUSES

>  <b>TINNE</b> ... enjoying dry ... quarters at the great `whare-puna', or meeting-house, of the tribe. <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,9</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> 8. `the meeting-house'. Meeting houses have often been the focus of community effort and pride. They are long halls used for meetings and for the accommodation of visitors. They have a verandah at the entrance with a carved gabled front and are richly decorated inside, with patterned reed panels, with black and white painted scrolls on the rafters, and with carvings or paintings of ancestors and incidents in the mythological history of the people on whose marae the meeting-house is built. Supporting the ridge-pole is a centre-post on which are figures of ancestors. The floor is often covered with flax matting, and visitors take off their shoes before entering. Another meeting house is described in the first part of `Hemi's Daughter'. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,135</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> "But all the rooms with this ward overlook the Maori meeting house,' the sister said. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,45</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> On the concrete path to the meeting house/ It was the women who cried out,/ <i>(1979,Collected Poems,400,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMAS</b> The meeting house, situated in the centre of the kainga, was the usual type of those days with walls and roof thatched with raupo and nikau, no floor and dividing partitions. <i>(1970,Way Up North,4</i>)

>  <b>JENKIN</b> The building itself was unusual in that all the figures in the meetinghouse had carved skirts, or <i>piu piu. Ibid.</i> They were told ...of the reputation of the <i>wharepuni</i> (meeting house) at Matahiwi. <i>(1970,NZ Mysteries,108</i>)

>  Prime examples [of surviving traditional technology] are the techniques of building and decorating meeting houses and of making piupiu (dance skirts). <i>(1984,Te Maori,30</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> In those days [c1890s] they had what was known as the `Maori wedding', which was not legal to the Pakeha, but was a binding thing among the Maori people themselves. A young couple would decide that they wanted to become man and wife, and they would have to tell their parents they wanted this. Well, at night-time everyone would congregate in the meetinghouse [the matter would be discussed, the boy and girl would be asked whether they wanted to marry, and they would take each other in front of all their people]. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,109</i>) [NOTES: (Florence Harsant, Taupo)]

>  <i>meeting house</i> large,<i> usu</i>. decorated Maori tribal hall (also<i> wharepuni</i>) <i>(1985,Collins Compact Dictionary,305</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; nf. 1986 NZPOD]]

>  Wood used for carving and building carries with it the spirit of the tree, the spirit of place including the name of the place, and the spirit of the carvers who create the powerful images of tribal ancestors which decorate wharenui (meeting houses). ... Anyone who has spent time in a carved house (wharenui) will know the depth of feeling the carvings evoke, for they make the wharenui a living thing. <i>(Nov 22, 1988,Dominion,10</i>)

>  ... those manners and customs of the Maori which show a tendency to persist ... i.e. a political, social and artistic life centred in the Meeting House and Marae,. . . <i>(Mar 1962,NZ Journal of Public Administration,28</i>) [NOTES: (R.A. Kelly)]

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> The building was formerly a Runanga, or meeting-house of the Hau-Hau rebels ... <i>(1876,Colonial Travel,252</i>)

>  <b>WALLACE</b> Maori Whare-Pani [?puni], or Meeting House. <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,218</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> There are still some beautifully carved specimens of tribal meeting-houses to be seen, but almost all have been spoilt by the introduction of European doors and windows ... <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,277</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> That to him [sc the Maori] the monstrosities which decorated his whare runanga (meeting-house) posts ... and the rafters painted with kokowai (iron-ore rust), for a ground colour in red, ... <i> Ibid.</i> 276 ... according to the sensible code of [Maori] justice, every order and tribal regulation had to be decided in meeting-house convened, and proclaimed in the marae (village green) to the whole people there assembled before the penalties could be exacted. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,7</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The [Ngati-Ruanui] tribe built an unusually large meeting-house for the gathering; ... `Taiporohenui', the name given to the assembly- house, was that of a sacred house of instruction in Hawaiki, according to Taranaki tradition. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/147</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Porter rushed to the meeting-house, and his men thrust their rifle-muzzles into the door and window. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/413,1983 ed.</i>)

>  In many places, the tribal meeting-house stands alone or flanked by a solitary cook-house, patiently awaiting until a death or some object of great moment shall for a brief period draw its people together under its sheltering roof. <i>(1924,TrNZI,55/367</i>) [NOTES: (Buck `Passing of the Maori')]

>  <b>COWAN</b> Most of the people appeared to be gathered in the large<i> nikau</i>-thatched meeting-house. <i>Ibid.</i> 182 ... they could see the village square and the large thatched meeting-<i>whare</i> in which Te Kooti and his followers were housed. <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,159</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Everything's Maori up there. Most of them are Christians now, but on a Sunday you can still see some of the old chaps doing their <i>hau-hau</i>, getting down and barking like dogs. ... That meeting-house is worth seeing. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,189</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> He addressed his speech to a lone white man who sat before him on a mat in the meeting house. <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,109</i>)

>  <b>ZIMMERMAN</b> It took me the time spent in walking two squares to figure out that <i>whare runanga</i> probably meant meeting house. <i>(1946,Where the People Sing,93</i>)

>  <i>(1953,Short Stories (various authors),267</i>) [NOTES: 1944 Stewart in 1953 Short Stories 267]

>  Then we start to walk to the meeting-house. <i>(1945,Journal of the Polynesian Society,54/97</i>) [NOTES: (Beaglehole)]

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> Just across the courtyard was the meeting-house with its splendidly carved posts and panels. <i>(1952,Brown Man's Burden,112,1973 ed.</i>)

>  On a large [52] grassed <i>marae</i> behind the store are two meeting-houses. You may be lucky enough to see a hui in progress. Perhaps a welcome home to one of the many Ruatoki boys who are serving with the Maori Battalion. <i>Ibid.</i> 52 ... to collect parcels for Ruatoki boys overseas. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,51</i>)

>  <b>ASHTON-WARNER</b> <i>(1958,Spinster</i>) [NOTES: see RORO]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> In the country proper the Maori has retained his traditional mode of life which is symbolised in the <i>marae</i>. In a literal sense the term means the open courtyard in front of the communal meeting house; today ... it embraces all aspects of community life - community buildings, tribal gatherings, church activities, and recreation. <i>(1959,Descriptive Atlas,72</i>)

>  [See <i>Koruru</i>] <i>(Nov 1979,Islands,7/5/503</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Among the Maoris the marae, a word which elsewhere in Polynesia meant temple, was the plaza which lay before the meeting-house and was the focus of village life. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,24</i>)

### megaherb
 
 <i>n.</i> a large herb

>  Megaherbs, the flamboyant flowering plants for which New Zealand's offshore islands are well known, are represented by just one species on the Snares: the large-leaved punui, <i>Stilbocarpa robusta</i>)... <i>(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,62/62</i>)

>  Evolution of <i>Stilbocarpa</i>. a megaherb from New Zealand's sub-antarctic islands... The humid climate, cool equable temperatures, locally abundant nutrients, and absence of herbivores are ecological features of the sub-antarctic islands that may have contributed to the evolution of the unusual megaherb, <i>Stilbocarpa</i>, from these diminutive apiaceous ancestors. <i>(Jun 1999,NZ Journal of Botany</i>) [NOTES: Paper by Mitchell, Meurk & Wagstaff]

>  Megaherb describes certain plant species that grow on the Sub-antarctic Islands of New Zealand. They are usually quite colourful and have large leaves considering their native environment. These species have adapted very well to growing in a harsh environment but have become so specialised they are dificult to grow elsewhere. <i>Anisotome latifolia</i> is an example of one of these megaherbs. <i>(2003,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: Http://davesgarden.com]

>  <b>FELL</b> The name 'megaherb' was coined by Britain's Sir James Clark Ross during his 1839-43 Antarctic voyage, accoppanied by a botanist from Kew Gardens, Sir Joseph Hooker, whose name is honoured by the Hooker sea lion. <i>(2002,Campbell Island: Land of the Blue Sunflower,54</i>)

>  The large-leafed megaherb punui.. is a feature of the southern islands, including Whenua Hou. <i>(Sep 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 59/58</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### Meggy-monny-legs
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  These [<i>Myriapoda</i>] are mostly of the harmless `thousand-legged' species, whose only protection appears to be to curl themselves into a spiral and emit an evil-smelling fluid; but one occasionally gets one of the `hundred-legs,' or `Meggy-monny-legs,' who, with his well-armed, fierce appearance, has gained the reputation of being poisonous. <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/220</i>)

### megrim
 
 <i>n.</i> cadgers'-fish; mahue, witch (See [mahue](m-mar.md#mahue))

>  Megrim ...Brill, Mahoa, Witch <i>Caulopsetta scapha</i> <i>(1911,Records of the Canterbury Museum,13/197</i>)

>  <i>Caulopsetta scapha</i> Forster. This species referred to as the `brill' in my former list, is a totally different fish. This specific name belongs to the megrim, or witch, a common form, not sold as a food fish on account of its lean, bony character. <i>(1913,TrNZI,45/232</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> CAULOPSETTA SCAPHA (Forster). Megrim. Known as `witch' or `lantern-fish' at Napier. Taken by trawlers in sandy localities as far north as Hauraki Gulf. Not popular as a food fish. ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts (unknown North Auckland Peninsula). <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/121</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Caulopsetta scapha</i> (Forster). MEGRIM. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,27,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> The `commonest and most widely distributed of the flat fishes of New Zealand, and yet, unfortunately, the least valuable,' is the Megrim or Witch (Plate II. fig. 1). It is our only left sided flat fish, and is a very thin species full of long bones. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,201</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 201]

>  17. <i>Caulopsetta scapha</i> Forster. Megrim or Witch. Maori: Mahoa <i>(1929,TrNZI,61/142</i>) [NOTES: (Young)]

>  <i>Caulopsetta scapha</i> (Forster). Witch (megrim). <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/407</i>) [NOTES: 1938 Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>POWELL</b> Thirteen species of flat-fishes are known from New Zealand waters, including a large Turbot, <i>Colistium nudipinnis</i>, which is comparatively rare, and the Megrim, <i>Caulopsetta scapha</i> , which is not popular as a food fish as it is usually very thin. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,67</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Megrim <i>Caulopsetta scaphus</i> (Bloch and Schn.) ... Megrim <i>Caulopsetta boops</i> (Hector) ... Megrim <i>Caulopsetta hectoris</i> (Gunther) ... Megrim <i>Brachypleura novaezeelandiae</i> Gunther <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,404</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 404]

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> All [flatfish] but the megrim (<i>Caulopsetta</i> ... ) have the right (as opposed to the left) side uppermost ... All are excellent food fish-again with the exception of the megrim, once dubbed `cadgers' - fish' because of its suitability for palming off on those who hover round landing fishing-craft in the hope of perks, especially flatfish. <i>Ibid.</i> 218 [Caption] Fig. 66: FLATFISHES. I. <i>Turbot.</i> 2. Brill;(<i>both </i>Colisteum <i>spp</i>). 3. Flounder, <i>Rhombosolea</i>. 4. Sole, <i>Peltorhamphus</i>. 5. Megrim or witch, <i>Caulopsetta</i> (eyes left!). 6. Lemon sole, <i>Pelotretis. </i> <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,217</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> MEGRIM Witch-fish and Fake Flounder are just two names for this skinny, poor-eating, barely flatfish. <i>Ibid.</i> 142 LANTERNFISH ... Sometimes called Jewel-fish. Another name for the Megrim, an almost transparent flatfish. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,74</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>WITCH </i>(Megrim, mahue) <i>Arnoglossus scapha </i>... The witch is a thin-bodied left eye floundr with an oval shape ... However, they have no commercial value as they have thin watery flesh and many fine bones. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,307,1984 ed.</i>)

>  Ironically, the most abundant of all New Zealand flounders, the witch or megrim, is useless for eating because of its multitude of long thin hair-like bones and thin watery flesh. The Maori name for this fish - mahue - says it all. It translates as `to be left behind'. <i>(Dec 8, 1982,Evening Post,37</i>)

>  [species name] witch .. [scientific name] <i>Arnoglossus scapha </i>.. [other name(s)] megrim <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Witch Arnoglossus scapha </i> Also called megrim, both names derived from similar European flatfishes. Found only in New Zealand. ... A related but smaller species, the crested flounder <i>Lophonectes gallus</i>, of northern coastal waters, can be distinguished by a `crest' of long white dorsal fin rays. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,145</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] megrim 2218-22, 2237, 2249 <i>(1987,Bibliography of NZ Fishes</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> NEW ZEALAND MEGRIM or WITCH (MAHUE) <i>Caulopsetta scaphus</i> (Bloch and Schneider) ... In Napier it is known as Lantern-fish, but for what reason I am unable to say, unless it is from the peculiar transparency when it/is held up to a strong light. Another name given to this useless fish by local fishermen, in Port Chalmers at least, was that of Cadger's-fish. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,186</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a plant, <i>Melicope simplex</i>

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>(1906,Plants of NZ</i>) [NOTES: And.]

### melilot
 
 <i>n.</i> a clover, bokhara clover, sweet clover, yellow lucerne (See [also King Island melilot](../A#also-king-island-melilot))

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> MELILOT (<i>Melilotus spp</i>.) is called also sweet clover, Bokhara clover, and yellow lucerne. ... It is annual or biennial, grows 2 or 3 feet high, with stiff erect branching stems. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,109,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> Melilot clover, a grass that is now extensively sown in many parts, first came under notice on King Island [Australia]. <i>(1951,Confessions,15</i>)

>  <i>melilot [=] King Island melilot: sweet clover</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,48</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> KING ISLAND MELILOT (<i>Melilotus indica</i>) ... native to Eurasia, locally common through New Zealand ... In earlier times it came under notice on King Island, north-west of Tasmania. <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,79</i>)

>  <i>King Island melilot [s] Melilotus indica</i> (L.) All. <i>Ibid.</i> 48 melilot, King Island [s] Melilotus indica (L.) All. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,42</i>)

### melon
 
 <i>n.</i> a fool

>  [The traveller to New Zealand] might be ... told ... that he was `a melon' to have come out and `given it a pop';. . . <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Melon</i>. A new cadet: Royal Military Academy: from ca. 1870: ob. Ex his greenness, as is 2, the Australian and New Zealand sense (late C. 19-20), a simpleton, a fool. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,516</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HAY</b> We grow large quantities of melons - water-melons, musk-melons, rock-melons, Spanish melons, pie-melons, and so on. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,1/223</i>)

>  ...one of his 1883 advertisements read "wanting 100 tons of pie melons and able to buy strawberries." <i>(Apr 1997,NZ Geographic,N0. 34/64</i>)

### melonette
 
 <i>n.</i> name originally proposed for Chinese gooseberries

>  Auckland fruit packers Turners and Growers tried the term "melonette" which it quickly dropped because of the potential for rows over the high American tariffs on real melons. <i>(Apr 29, 2004,Dominion Post,C11</i>)

### melt
 
 <i>v.</i> spend (money, a cheque) on drink

>  Shearers ... draw .... a 40 [pound] to 50 [pound] check, and taking it to the nearest Public House, would hand it over to the landlord, saying, just let me know when that's melted. <i>(Apr 22, 1874,Journal of a Visit,56</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to spend on drink

>  <b>LANGTON</b> A shepherd from an up-country station .... who had come to Dunstan ... to `melt' his cheque. <i>(1889,Mark Anderson,31</i>)

### melt off
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [sheep being worked] to behave assertively toward a dog.

>  <b>WHYTE</b> If the sheep are standing a few yards away, before the dog has approached more than a few paces, they start to melt off him in a steady confident way. <i>(1927,Sheep-Dog, The,36</i>)

### melter
 
 <i>n.</i> a juicy crisp biting peach

>  The overflowing, mouth-drooling juiciness is so predominant in peaches it is used in the summer fruit indistry to class them as melting or non-melting. Non-melters are the ones you find in cans. All the others are melters - or, more accurately, dribblers. <i>(Jun 27, 2003,Dominion Post,C5</i>)

### melties
 
 <i>n.</i> equivalent to present-day melting moments -

>  <i>MELTIES </i>... icing sugar ... butter ... flour ... baking powder ... Put teaspoonfuls on a cold slide. Bake ... Put two together with mock cream filling. <i>(1938,Souvenir Book of Cookery Recipes,38</i>)

### melting moment
 
 <i>n.</i> present-day M.M.'s are stuck together with icing or jam

>  <i>MELTING MOMENTS</i> ... flour ... icing sugar .. cornflour ... butter ... spoonfuls .. moderate oven. <i>(1938,Souvenir Book of Cookery Recipes,40</i>)

### Melycitus
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Melicytus ramiflorus</i>

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>(1906,Plants of NZ</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

### memorandum of transfer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The document whose registration confers valid title is not a deed nor is it known as such but a transfer or memorandum of transfer. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,1</i>)

### memorial hall
 
 <i>n.</i> a public hall erected as a WWI or WWII memorial

>  The country dances in the wool-sheds and memorial halls and the firm clasp of country girls in dizzy waltzes and the horse sports at Tinui and the nodding ponies and the country people in friendly gossip all around the paddock. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,171</i>)

### men's hut
 
 <i>n.</i> the bunkhouse-dining place of shearers and other station hands (See [cf station house](spa-sys.md#station-house),[whare](../W#whare))

>  <b>BARKER</b> The manager has a nice house; and the wood-shed, men's huts, dip, &c., are near each other. <i>Ibid.</i> ) 34 We peeped in at the men's huts - a long, low wooden building, with two rows of `bunks' (berths, I should call them) in one compartment, and a table with forms around it in the other, and piles of tin plates and pannikins all about. <i>(1865,Station Life in NZ,32,1974 ed.</i>)

>  Mason finished drain & got clay &c for oven at mens hut. [Sep 15] Five tramps at Men's hut. -->c1875 Meredith Adventuring in Maoriland (1935) 39 So I invited him to come into the men's hut, and have it out, which challenge he gleefully accepted, ... <i>(Sep 8, 1866,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: [Hocken TS]]

>  <b>PASH</b> Farm labourers are generally housed in a tolerably sized building called the men's hut ... <i>(1883,Report on NZ,6</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> [Temporary hands on sheep stations] live in a large building called `the men's hut'. <i>(1889,New Zealand After 50 Years,155</i>)

>  <i>Men's hut</i>: House where the station hands live. On some stations it is called the <i>station house</i>, and now often the <i>whare</i>, which word was, I suppose, brought from the North Island stations. Originally the men cooked, ate, and slept in a one-roomed hut, with a fireplace and colonial oven at one end and bunks in two or three tiers round the walls. Nowadays most <i>m. hs.</i> are reasonably comfortable houses with separate rooms. ([1898 Morris Austral-English], under <i>hut</i>) <i>(Nov 4, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

### men's quarters
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FRANCES</b> The men's quarters in those days differed little from station to station. The roof and walls were corrugated iron, usually unlined ... <i>(1983,Wildlife Ranger,2</i>)

### mend
 
 <i>n.</i> [ca1930 R. Mason, Auckland Girls' Boarding School (Something) is `at the mend'. Used esp. of shoes: eg. They're at the mend.]

### mental
 
 <i>adj.</i> crazy; often as go mental [1984 16-17 F E36 Pakuranga Coll. 2 MENTAL [9 of a mad person] -->1984 14 F E120 Wgton Girls C. 2 MENTAL [7]]

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> Hughes's brother was crushed under a tree and died next day, and Fabian's wife has gone mental. <i>(1933,Tides of Youth,54</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Drive a man mental it would - On the dog-dosing strip at Kuripuni I fell in love with you - <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,162</i>)

>  <i>mental <i> <i>(1981,Auckland Secondary Teachers College</i>)

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> Poor Mrs Nation is mental. That means she isn't well in the head and needs looking after. <i>(1987,Redemption of Elsdon Bird, The,29,1988 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> esp at the, in the mental

>  <b>McADAM</b> <i>(1905,Pagan's Love, A</i>) [NOTES: c1905-10 McAdam Article from `Witness' [in Pagan's Love 1905]]

### mental home
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> Yes, poor devil. His wife is in a mental home. <i>(1933,Tides of Youth,57</i>)

### menuka
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [manuka](m-mar.md#manuka))

### Menzies' beech
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Nothofagus menziesii.</i>

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> Menzies' Beech <i>(1906,Plants of NZ</i>) [NOTES: (And),]

>  <b>MORELAND</b> There are several varieties of this noble tree, which attains in these southern forests to huge dimensions - Menzie's beech and Solander's beech may reach even a hundred feet. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,206</i>)

### MEPS
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Minimum Energy Performance Standards [ORIGIN: acronym, aka E3; Joint NZ-Aust governments' initiative for energy efficiency]

>  Motors from 185 kW up to and including 265 kW will only be accepted into the scheme when the efficiency of the replacement motor meets or exceeds the MEPS 2006 level for 185 kW motors. <i>(2010,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.motorbounty.co.nz/criteria.htm]

>  New Zealand works with Australia on a joint programme called the Equipment Energy Efficiency (E3) Programme. The E3 programme develops energy efficiency measures for a range of residential, commercial, and industrial products which economic and environmental benefits to consumers and businesses. The E3 allows both countries to honour their commitments under the Trans-Tasman Mutual Recognition Arrangement (TTMRA). <i>(2010,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.eeca.govt.nz/standards-and-ratings/minimum-energy-performance-standards-and-labelling]

>  Will I have to replace all my light bulbs once the MEPS come into effect? No. The MEPS is intended to stop the product being sold, but does not affect those already in use. <i>(Jun 17, 2008,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.scoop.co.nz/stories/PA0806/S00228.htm]

>  New Minimum Energy Performance Standards (MEPS) came into effect 1 April 2010. The New Zealand and Australian governments have a joint task force to help us to become more energy efficient. <i>(May 12, 2010,Horowhenua Chronicle,5</i>)

### Mercedes Maori
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Mercedes Maori, who seem to have all the say, have time and again conceded our rights for little, then shared and squandered it between themselves for generally no gain for the majority of Maori. <i>(Jul 22, 2012,Sunday Star Times,A17</i>)

### Mercer grass
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Mercer grass</i> [s] <i>Paspalum distichum</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,48</i>)

### merchantable
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  MERCHANTABLE. The common size or grade of logs, timber or other forest produce... Sound, free from shakes, large, loose or rotten knots or defects that materially impair the strength. Wood suitable for constructional purposes. <i>(Nov 1957,NZ Timber Journal,59</i>)

### Mercury Bay weed
 
 <i>n.</i> a plant used for lawn grass in warm districts.

>  I shall try that Mercury Bay weed for a lawn. <i>(Apr 9, 1947,NZ Observer,19</i>)

### mere
 
 <i>n.</i> a short flat bone, stone or greenstone hand-club. [Maori] ; mere ... n. Maori war-club [Maori]; a short flat weapon of stone for hand-to-hand fighting (Williams) Maori,<i> mere</i> (occas. <i>meremere</i>)

>  Clark would willingly have fallen on a giant mere to the vigorous strains of haka rap in which the word "bitch" featured hugely. <i>(Dec 3, 2000,Sunday Star Times,A7</i>)

>  maree <i>(1819,Missionary Notices,377</i>)

>  <b>CAPT. HENRY WISHART</b> Warepowre upon this laid aside his marie (hatchet of green stone) and went forward to see how matters stood ... <i>(Apr 2, 1836,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1836 Apr 2 Capt Henry Wishart in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 146]

>  <b>POLACK</b> Each person wore a belt of leather, or matted flax, from which was appended the stone meri [e-acute], or more modern tomahawk. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/163</i>)

>  <b>MATHEW</b> [Patamouri] presented the Governor with one of their splendid Green Talc Hatchets or `Mares' expressly for Queen `Wikitoria'. <i>(Feb 6, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Feb 6 F.Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 41]

>  <b>WILKES</b> Around the necks of the chiefs and their wives is hung their `heitiki', made of a stone of a green colour, ... and which, with their `meara' - a short cleaver or club - is handed down from father to son. <i>(1852,U.S. Exploring Expedition,1/303</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Mar in 1852 Wilkes U.S. Exploring Expedition 1/303]

>  <b>MATHEW</b> Green talc Hatchets or `Mares' <i>(1840,Journal,38</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> In the belt which confines the mat is stuck a tomahawk or `meri' which, until very lately, was their only weapon. <i>(1842,New Zealand (various authors/titles),56</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> In the belt which confines the mat is stuck a tomahawk or `meri', which, until very lately, was their only weapon. <i>(1842,Narrative of a Residence in  Various Parts of NZ,56</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The inmates of each house were engaged in making <i>meri poenamu</i> and ear pendants of that material, for `trade' or presents northward. <i>(1846,Notes of an Expedition to Kawatiri and Araura</i>) [NOTES: 1846 Heaphy Exped. to Kawatiri in 1959 Taylor Early Travs. 237]

>  <b>ANGA</b> The <i>meri</i>, or war-club, is a flattened weapon, from one to two feet in length, which is used in single combat: ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/335</i>)

>  This spirit [of submission] was satisfactorily manifested on Monday, when they [the Maoris] came to town, laid their <i>meris</i> and a spear at the feet of his Excellency and afterwards departed to their homes ... <i>(May 17, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6</i>)

>  <b>CARBERRY</b> The Battle of Rangariri (no date) ... he sent his Mere [e-acute] (a greenstone weapon the insignia of a chief) to the General, which is supposed to indicate that he will not fight any more. <i>(1863,Journal</i>)

>  There was not the slightest degree of shilly shallying - the tomahawk and the mere* going to it with true New Zealand zest; ... [fn] (<i>NOTE</i>) A short flat hand club, of stone or whalebone, with edges, a scull-cracker of the most approved shape. <i>(1864,Von Tempsky Memoranda,167</i>)

>  mere, pattoo pattoo and the hideous breast ornament termed hei tiki. <i>(1880,Encyclopedia Britannica,xiii 540/1</i>)

>  <b>McKAY</b> <i>(1889,Spirit of Rangatira,16</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Mere</i>, (pronounced <i>merry</i>), a Maori war-club; a <i>casse-tete</i>, or a war-axe, from a foot to eighteen inches in length, and made of any suitable hard material - stone, hard wood, whale-bone. To many people out of New Zealand the word is only known as the name of a little trinket of <i>greenstone</i> (q.v.) made in imitation of the New Zealand weapon in miniature, mounted in gold or silver, and used as a brooch, locket, ear-ring, or other article of jewelry. <i>(1898,Austral English,290</i>)

>  <b>LANG</b> `Beneath his shaggy flaxen mat/ The dreadful marree hangs concealed.' <i>(1830,Poems (various authors),116,1873 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The most beautiful of all Maori arms was the battledore shaped weapon (<i>mere</i>), somewhat resembling a flat club, but which was not handled in the usual manner of a club. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,310</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,134</i>)

>  <b>FIRTH</b> Among the Maori, considerations of aesthetic interest helped quite materially ... to decide the relative worth of objects made of the<i> pounamu</i>, the nephrite so much prized for pendants, <i>mere</i>, and adzes. <i>(1929,Primitive Economics of NZ Maori,388</i>)

>  <i>~ n</i>. a stone war-club carried as an emblem of power by a Maori chief; a miniature of the worn as an article of jewellery. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,7</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> Being very tall, [the Maoris] presented a dignified appearance clad only in their silky white <i>Kaitakas </i>with huia feathers in their hair. These finest of Maori mats they wore tied on the right shoulder, leaving that hand ... free to grasp the greenstone `mere'. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,37</i>)

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> I had an instance to-day of the great value the natives sometimes set on their ornaments of green stone maries (meri), as the whites call them. <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,93,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <i>mere</i>, battledore-shaped weapon of greenstone; a most valued weapon, both to Maori user and Pakeha collector. A very commonly-used word, and almost invariably mispronounced like Mary; but both vowels have the same sound, as in mere(tricious) or in the proper name Mere(dith). <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/153</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> <i>mere</i> short flat weapon of stone <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,xxii</i>)

>  <i> mere</i>: war club. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,278</i>)

>  <i>(Nov 22, 1957,Listener (New Zealand Listener),4/2</i>)

>  <i>~ </i>A Maori club, made of stone, hard wood or whalebone. (Pronounce <i>merry</i>). <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),256</i>)

>  <i>mere n</i> -s [Maori] <i>1 Austral</i> : a Maori war club <i>2 Austral</i> : a miniature Maori war club fashioned of greenstone and worn as an ornament <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1413</i>)

>  <i>~ n</i>. Maori war-cub; miniature greenstone trinket in shape of a mere [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> [Maori glossary] <i>mere</i> short, flat stone club; a variety of<i> patu</i>, often made of greenstone <i>(1969,History of New Zealand,319</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> mere: traditional hand-weapon of bone or greenstone, used by men of high rank as a ceremonial accompaniment to speech making. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,141</i>)

>  <i>mere n</i>. a short flat Maori striking weapon. [< Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,707</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 470; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 307]]

>  <b>BELICH</b> <i> mere </i>short club, sometimes of greenstone (New Zealand jade) <i>(1986,NZ Wars, The,337</i>)

>  <b>CRAIK</b> [They] struck them on the head with their merys. <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,94</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>mere</i>: short flat weapon made of stone or greenstone. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,192</i>)

>  <b>BONWICK</b> <i>(1887,Romance of the Wool Trade,229</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> <i>(1942,Death and the Dancing Footmen,[Peng 1949] ed.</i>)

>  Down in the Hawke's Bay District the other day someone found a Maori meremere of such a size that one strong man could handle it only with difficulty. <i>(Nov 8, 1913,NZ Observer,20</i>)

>  There is a good collection of Maori mats, carvings, greenstone meres, axes, &c. <i>(Aug 27, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,30</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> M|ere, <i>s</i>. A war-club. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,180</i>)

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> He informed me that when he was at the Thames on a former occasion a chief had given him a maree, one of their war instruments, to sell for him for an axe. <i>(Aug 1820,Letters & Journals,277,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CRUISE</b> The existence [45] of the sufferer is terminated by a blow on the head, struck with a stone club called a mearee. [e3-acute] <i>(Mar 2, 1820,Journal,44,1957 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SHEPHERD</b> Tommy said it was next to impossible to attack a New Zealander with a sword ... as they have a method of pushing such weapons aside ... and generally kill the person with a Maree or axe or spear before he has time to make a second thrust. <i>(Mar 24, 1826,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Mar 24 Shepherd Journal in 1940 Howard Rakiura 364]

>  Most of these men had their stone marees (axes) slung to their wrists ..., & their spears placed in order, ... <i>(1827,Boultbee Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1827 Boultbee Journal in 1979 Begg 200]

>  <b>ATKINS</b> ... and with his <i>maree</i> (a short stone club, with a sharp edge [the chief] split his skull. <i>(Mar 2, 1829,Narrative of Eight Months Sojourn in NZ, A</i>) [NOTES: 1829 Mar 2 Atkins's Narrative in 1908 McNab Historical Records 1/692]

>  <b>SHERIDAN</b> ... in former days when in battle ... [they had] only their own implements of destruction - spears, nipes, and an instrument made out of beautiful blue or green marble stone, which they call a Mary, none but a chief is allowed to take them to battle, they are very scarce and very dear. <i>(1833,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1833 Sheridan Letter from Sydney Monitor in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 48]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Titore gave me a <i>Mere</i> as a sign of peace being established between us. <i>(Feb 4, 1833,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,278,1961 ed.</i>)

>  An instrument made of a beautiful blue or green marble stone, which they call a Mary. <i>(1833,Sydney Monitor</i>) [NOTES: 1833 Sydney Monitor letter (McNab OWD 48)]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> Then the Chief with his Marre [<i>mere</i>] / gives him a Clip on the Pipkin and he is speedily cut up, and so ends the life and begins the feast. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles)</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> Visits 10 a merai <i>(1834,Narrative of Eight Months Sojourn in NZ, A,2</i>)

>  [commentary on sketches] green stone maree/ Long CLUB or Mire/ Baracoota hook <i>Ibid.</i> 82 Most of these men had their stone marees (axes) slung to their wrists, and their spears placed in order ... <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,44,1986 ed.</i>)

### mere pounamu
 
 <i>n.</i> a greenstone club

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The greenstone ornaments belonging to the old chief [Te Heu Heu] are remarkably fine; his <i>meri poonamu </i>is one of the largest I have seen, and is formed of semi-pellucid jade. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/116</i>)

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> The elder men were dressed in their best dogskin and <i>kaitaka</i> mats, some of them had their heads decorated with <i>huia</i> and <i>kotuku</i> feathers, not neglecting their <i>mere</i>[es acute]<i> pounamu</i> (greenstone clubs), and every other ancient emblem of chieftainship ... <i>(May 26, 1848,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1848 May 26 Maclean Diary in 1940 Cowan Sir Donald Maclean 36]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> In the northern island [the rakau-pounamu] is called a `patu-pounamu' or `meri-pounamu'. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,34</i>)

>  <b>MANING</b> The whole upshot of this treaty on political economy ... (which I humbly lay at the feet of the Colonial Treasurer) is this:- I would not give one of your locks, my dear, for all the gold, silver, pearls, diamonds, <i>mere pounamus</i> - stop, let me think, - a good <i>mere pounamu</i> would be a temptation <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,2/26</i>)

>  Two elderly natives ... brought to my house a very handsome mere pounamu (greenstone club) ... <i>(Mar 27, 1899,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/618,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (R. Parris)]

 <i>n.</i> a tree

 <i>n.</i>

>  They commenced a war dance ... armed with guns, spears, <i>taiahas, meri meri,</i> &c., ... <i>(Apr 6, 1861,Otago Witness,6</i>)

### meringue cake
 
 <i>n.</i> precursor to pavlova

Turn upside down ... spread with whipped cream flovoured with passionfruit ... decorate with chopped nuts and cherries. <i>(1934,Marigold Book of Recipes, The,14</i>)

### meringue pudding
 
 <i>n.</i> precursor to pavlova

>  <b>BRANDON</b> <i>MERINGUE PUDDING</i> ... rice ... boiled ... milk ... butter ... yolks of 3 eggs ... lemon ... and mix with the rice. Bake ... Beat the whites ... with ... sugar ... lemon essence ... pour the beaten whites over it, and bake a light brown. <i>(1905,'Ukneadit'. Home for Incurables Bazaar,44</i>)

### merino

 <i>n.</i> (capital) a sheep bred for fine wool 2. a soft yarn or fabric, originally one made from the wool of a merino.

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) high country grazed by merino sheep in large unfenced blocks for the summer months.

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a high country Merino sheep breeder or farmer of established repute.

### mermaid's purse/mermaid's pinbox
 
 <i>n.</i>

Each capsule contains one egg. The shape is like a miniature pillow, with four horns (one at each corner). <i>((9/0/1956),NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,98</i>)

### message
 
 <i>n.</i> `Do you understand?' [ca1950 L. Cleveland eg. I'll make it plain to you. You're not coming. Get the message?]



 
 <i>n.</i> errand, shopping esp. run/go messages

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> "You went for the messages this morning." <i>Ibid.</i> 33 Most Saturday morning after messages had been done and bagzing for the afternoon on Gormie's porch was over, Brin and Smitty and Horsey and Gormie would go along to the park to have some kicks. <i>(1950,Our Street,17</i>) [NOTES: 1975 edition]

>  <b>W.J. JIMPSON</b> The following I have noted since I came to New Zealand ... Messages: to us [British], this always implies carrying information and our term for small shopping is `errands'. <i>(Dec 1, 1957,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: 1957 Dec 1 p.c. W.J. Jimpson (Tolaga Bay)]

>  <b>MACNICOL</b> I ... went on to do the weekly shopping for both families. When I returned to the car after the last message it was in an uproar. <i>(1965,Skippers Road,144</i>)

>  <b>GILDERDALE</b> <i>messages</i> Errands, jobs; as in `do the messages'. B. Sutton-Smith's <i>Our Street</i> (1950) page 24 and in many books thereafter. <i>(1982,Sea Change,241</i>) [NOTES: Note on Language]

>  Aunt Mag had a nasty habit of asking you to go to the grocer's or the butcher's on some message or other, just when you were planning to do something quite different. <i>(Aug 29, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>) [NOTES: (Davin)]

>  <b>KEINZLY</b> Under the shop verandahs young boys and girls scorched golden by the heat paraded for each other, and pakeha and Maori children ambled home with their messages and change, wonderfully cool in the shade of slabs of coloured ice on sticks. <i>(1960,Tangahano,33</i>) [NOTES: Jim Cameron says <i>run/go messages</i> is recorded in DNZE but (as a citation shows) phrase and meaning are slightly wider than the definition there, comprehending the shopping itself and what is obtained... Whatever its initial NZ currency the phrase is general NZE and not special to Southland as sometimes suggested.]

### metal
 
 <i>n.</i> road gravel

>  Go and load yourself up with 11 stone of road metal. <i>(Jun 15, 1912,NZ Free Lance,4</i>)

>  <b>OVERTON</b> ..the road was formed in to the homestead from the end of the metal at Warwick Downs. <i>(1949,Fifty Years Sheep Farming,82</i>)

>  <b>BLYTH</b> <i>Dirt</i>: Paydirt or gravel wash containing metal. <i>(1935,Gold Mining Year Book,11</i>)

>  <b>wink</b> Even communities as large as [143] Tauranga are cut off from the rest of New Zealand by gravel roads. (The word `metal' is never applied to a road in America.) <i>(1954,These New Zealanders,142</i>)

>  <b>SHERWOOD</b> At a warning notice `Seal Ends' the tarmac stopped and gave way to loose chippings the size of potatoes, ... <i>(1985,Botanist at Bay,23</i>)

>  If John or Alex wanted metal To make the roads look firm and good.. I'd tell the clerk to write a letter. <i>(Jun 10, 1919,Pukekohe & Waiuku Times,Suppl. 1</i>)

>  <b>PAYTON</b> Many of these bush roads are what are called "summer" roads, consisting entirely of mud without any metal. <i>(1888,Round about NZ,47</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>IRELAND</b> The road back to Auckland was mostly metalled in those days. <i>(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,47</i>)

>  The contract for metalling the Riccarton Road through Hagley Park has also been let ... <i>(Jul 1, 1857,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

>  <b>MCALLISTER</b> One of the party naively remarked that they got half a mile of road metalled that summer. <i>(1976,Old Taranaki,53</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> small stones

>  I found myself wishing I had heavy clay soil so that I could use this marvellous stuff he's talking about, Waikato pea metal, a substance similar to fish tank gravel, full of tiny round pebbles of pumice, sand, granites and mica... <i>(Apr 15, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),101</i>)

### metal road
 
 <i>n.</i> an unsealed gravel road

>  If you have ever had the stuffing knocked out of you on a gravel road, you may have paused to consider why such byways are known as "metal roads" when they are quite clearly covered with small stones and not metal... Norm explained that "metal" is a Scottish term for broken rock. <i>(Oct 16, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),50</i>)

>  The main metal and shell roads are also getting into a deplorable state. <i>(Jun 15, 1917,Pukekohe & Waiuku Advocate,4</i>)

>  They'd go in his car / to places he'd chosen in the bush, by a waterfall, / places less open / than his metal road where she first stopped / when he waved / & came from the cow-shed to talk to her. <i>(Jul 3, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>) [NOTES: Jan Kemp "A childhood secret"]

### metalled
 
 <i>adj.</i> formed with gravel

>  We trundle up a metalled road... <i>(Aug 28, 2004,Dominion Post,E16</i>)

 <i>n.</i> exponent of metal music

### metallician
 
 <i>n.</i> a bookmaker

>  The Taieri Advocate says: It is seriously stated that there was very little tote betting at Tuapeka, the detective employed not having a single case to report. Those who were present at the meeting and saw the splendid business done by several metallicians know a little different. <i>(Feb 24, 1972,Tapanui Courier</i>) [NOTES: c1897 Taieri Advocate in 1972 Feb 24 Tapanui Courier `75 Years Ago' (Griffiths Collection)]

>  [Hdg] A Tapanui reader has sent me a recent issue of the local Courier, drawing my attention to two `Past Events' items. ... a paragraph about tote betting back in 1896, where it was stated that those attending the Tuapeka races `saw the splendid business done by several metallicians'. It's a new word to me ... [entry from <i>Oxford English Dictionary</i> [John Roberts, Box 962, Dunedin] metallician 1.<i>Racing slang.</i> A bookmaker ... 1874 <i>Notten's Slang Dict. Metallician</i>, a racing bookmaker. Bookmakers use metallic books and pencils. 1887 Daily Tel. 12 Mar. 5/2 In Australia the bookmaker has to pay dearly ... As for the long-suffering Australian public, they are mulcted as heavily as the much-taxed metallician.] <i>(Mar 6, 1972,Tapanui Courier</i>) [NOTES: Old Slang?]

### metha
 
 <i>n.</i> methylated spirits (for drinking) (See [(cf METHO)](../(#(cf-metho)))

>  <b>HYDE</b> The first few nights [at Waikaremoana hydro camp], Starkie was out of tune with his bedmates, for two of them were drinking methylated spirits, which they called `metha' and `Johnny Gee'. ... `I knew a joker went blind drinking that metha. They put stuff in it to keep you off drinking it.' <i>Ibid..</i> 190 They [sc Maoris] drink a lot of metha, don't they. Great names they've got for it - Phar Lap, and Johnny Gee, and White Light. There's one old Maori down here who went blind, drinking the metha. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,152</i>)

### Michael

### Mick
 
 <i>n.</i> a Catholic [ca1941 St Patrick's College, Silverstream [in ODE as chiefly Austral.; in SOED no label - cf. DNZE no entry]]

>  <b>STEWART</b> The next day was Sunday, and Joe went to Mass ... Joe was hardly surprised to see Captain McGinty there, for with such a name he could hardly have failed to be a `mick'. <i>(1981,Hot & Copper Sky,191</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> `I know a girl who did. She was a Mick. Are your family Micks?' `We go to Saint Monica's. ...' `Oh, that's C. of E., same as us. ...' <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,48,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>B. BAINBRIDGE</b> a limerick on the McVicar Timber Company: NZ Forest Service An Anglican priest called McVicar / Said to his bishop, `You stick 'er! / I'm sick of you pricks, I'm joining the Micks, / The pay's bad, but promotion's much quicker <i>(1956,Personal Communication</i>)

>  <b>MUIR</b> `She may be a Catholic.' `A mick? I doubt it.' <i>(1960,Word for Word,206</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> `Hello, you again. I'd no idea you were a mick', she said. <i>(1970,Not Here, Not Now,56</i>)

>  <b>MEIKLE</b> The Roman Catholic church (attended by `Micks' or `Doolans') was outside this ranking ... <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,127</i>)

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> They'd call themselves micks, or doolans or tykes, but if you called them anything like that. they'd get maggoty. <i>(1984,Blackball 08,119</i>)

>  The landing beach.. seems to be a nursery for kingfish and frost-fish. <i>(Mar 9, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,45</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the tail of coin (in two-up)

>  The New Zealanders are very religious men. Their priests lead them out to a quiet spot where they can pray. The priest spreads out a holy mat with marks on it which means something they have great faith in. He kneels down beside the mat, then a row of worshippers kneel all round him, with another row bending over them, and another row standing behind them. The worshippers throw their offerings on to the holy mat, and the priest places two coins on a short piece of polished wood which he calls a kip, and raising his eyes to the sky, he throws up the coins as an offering to Allah. All the worshippers raise their eyes also to the sky, and then bow solemnly over the mat, and say together, `God Almighty', and the priest answers, `A pair of Micks', which means that the offerings are not accepted, or he may say, `Oh Lord, he has done 'em again', and the joyful cries of some of the worshippers show that Allah is pleased, and so they, too, are glad. <i>(1938,With the Cameliers in Palestine,198</i>)

### mick
 
 <i>v.</i> to chaff

>  We generally laughed as he practised his Kiwi or micked me or spoke Cockney <i>(Sep 1983,Landfall,147/277</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> mistress

>  The brothers were getting upset, he says, because their "micks" (mistresses) were being done over in the women's toilets. <i>(May 14, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

### Mick Dooley
 
 <i>n.</i> an Irishman (See [Mick](mar-moa.md#mick); [Micky Doolan](mar-moa.md#micky-doolan))

>  A Mick Dooley asked a Wellington barmaid for `some of that new Frinch drink'. <i>(Sep 9, 1905,NZ Truth,12/1/7</i>)

>  `Mick Dooley, an old time Australian pugilist') <i>(1906,NZ Truth,50/7/8</i>)

### mick-a-mick
 
 <i>n.</i> any of several small-leaved shrubs. [Maori] (See [miki-miki](mar-moa.md#miki-miki))

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The under-scrub was very dense, Matipo, Ini-Ini, Konini, Pokaka, Mick-a-Mick, Ohau, Ribbonwood, Ngaio, Lemonwood and others, ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,242,1954 ed.</i>)

### mick/micky
 
 <i>n.</i> a vagina [ca1935 (Havelock) cp. P. mickey 3. penis: Anglo-Irish.]

### Mickey Dooley
 
 <i>n.</i> a Roman Catholic

>  A former Unemployed Workers' Union employee, a hippie hop-picker from Motueka, a Mickey Dooley from St Patrick's College Silverstream, Devlin hides his left-wing roots well on his national Radio Sport show each morning. <i>(Nov 17, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

### Mickey Finn
 
 <i>n.</i> a sleeping pill

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> MICKEY FINN (n) Sleeping pill. From the sense of drugged alcohol, USA 1906. Possibly derived from one Mickey Finn, a notorious Chicago publican, 1896-1906. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,250</i>)

### mickey mousing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Teachers were sometime frustrated by the lack of immediate action and a lot of "mickey mousing around" by the service. <i>(Nov 3, 1999,Dominion,14</i>)

### Mickey's Broken Doll
 
 <i>phr.</i> otherwise called Aussie's Broken Doll

>  <b>B.F.SHEPHERD</b> You think that I've been miking all this while;/ Why I took a chance,/ Left one ball in France,/ And now I'm Mickey's broken doll. <i>(1939,NZ Truth</i>) [NOTES: 1939 This version heard Trentham, according to B.F. Shepherd [Auckland corresp. April 12, 1976] [Les Cleveland's note: The `Mickey' referred to =Michael Joe Savage. So this is a 1939, N.Z. adaption of a W.W.1. song.]]

### Micky
 
 <i>n.</i> an Irish person (Micky Doolan)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> It would have been a `cold day' [at Hokitika] for the four who removed [the Fenian martyr's] cross could the `Mickies' have discovered their identity ... <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,24</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Mick; Mickey or Micky</i>; (Or with capital initial.) An Irishman: orig. (- 1869), U.S.: anglicised ca. 1890: more gen. in Canada, Australia, and New Zealand than in Britain. Ex <i>Michael</> <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,518</i>)</i>

### micky
 
 <i>n.</i> short for miki-miki

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Mickey, n</i>. In New Zealand, a corruption of <i>Mingi</i> (q.v.). [=Cyathodes <i>sp</i>.] <i>Ibid.</i> 294 <i>Mingi</i>, ... In south New Zealand it [Cyathodes] is often called <i>Micky. </i> <i>(1896,Austral English,292</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Micky.</i> 3. A New Zealand corruption (- 1898) of Maori <i>mingi</i>, orig. <i>mingi-mingi,</i> a shrub or small tree (<i>cyathodes acerosa</i>). Morris. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,519</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [mikimiki](../M#mikimiki))

### Micky Doo
 
 <i>n.</i> a Mickey Doolan (q.v.), an Irish Catholic [ORIGIN: abbreviation, the first citation below is misplaced; the 1984 citation is for Mickey Doolan]

>  Where Mickey Doos have always been. <i>(Sep 23, 1905,NZ Truth,14/1/8</i>)

>  <b>McELDOWNEY</b> When she came in from shopping she found that Michael Hickey had the gym full of `little Micky Doos' - Holy Name children. <i>(1983,Warm South,123</i>) [NOTES: (1965) 1983 McEldowney Warm South 123 [1965 July 21]]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Trouble was she was a Mickey Doo - Buggered for the want of an Irish king - <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,161</i>)

>  And it [boxing] was something Catholic kids (Mickey Doos) were forced to do that gave us another excuse for avoiding them. <i>(Aug 2, 1982,National Business Review,32</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> We'd gang up on kids who chucked off at us - like the boys from the Catholic home. They'd call us [from the Presbyterian Boys' Home in Dunedin] `Pressbuttons'; we'd call them `doolans', `doolies', `Mickey Doolans' - or worse. <i>(1984,Island to Island,87</i>) [NOTES: [c1935]]

>  <b>SOMES</b> [Henry Williams] had not given them [sc. Maoris for land] even a fish-hook or a head of tobacco, except in payment to the boys who carried his things, or to those who became converts to the mihanere (missionary) creed. <i>(Apr 19, 1841,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Apr 19 Joseph Somes to Lord John Russell in GBPP House of Commons 1841 (No.311) 129 [NZ letter of 23 Aug 1840]]

>  <b>RENeE</b> Strange, I thought. But then I thought, well, she's a Micky Doo Tommy Boy. I'm, a Methodist myself. <i>(1998,Yin & Tonic,133</i>)

### Micky Doolan
 
 <i>n.</i> an (Irish) Catholic (cf. MICKY DOO) [ca1930 R. Mason (Sometimes Micky) nf P, W3 ca1941 St Patrick's College, Silverstream]

>  However the fact that Mike only polled 529 votes against 434 for a wog, 409 for a micky doolan and 253 for Professor Liley's stand in shows [etc] <i>(Jul 5, 1972,Salient,5</i>)

>  And this time I promised Dad that I'd never play with that little Micky Doolin bastard again. <i>(Jun 1987,Landfall,162</i>) [NOTES: (Gordon)]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Mickey Doolan</i> Roman Catholic and usually Irish, aka <i>mick, mickey, Mickey Doo;</i> `mick' used elsewhere. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,72</i>)

### micron creep
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a change in records of fleecewool core size [often due to imprecise measurement.]

>  The phenomenon [of wool core becoming coarser on testing] has been noted by farmers using all forms of fleece testing, and has been commonly refered to as 'micron creep' or 'diameter shrinkage.' Micron creep occurs when fleeces which have been objectively tested are lotted into discreet micron bins. The actual cause of micron creep is imprecision of the measurement. <i>(2002,Wool Grower,Spring 26</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (Environment)

Mr Hunt told Dr Gibbs he remembered the weta well, especially its unusual markings of "red and black with possibly some white." <i>(Nov 3, 1993,NewsVUW (Wellington),2(17): 11</i>)

 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [fleece wool] of medium fibre thickness.

### middle beat
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a mustering beat that does not include tops or bottoms. (See [outside beat](ore-ozz.md#outside-beat),[top beat](ten-tot.md#top-beat),[bottom beat](bla-bri.md#bottom-beat))

>  <b>PASCOE</b> The man half way down the slopes has what is called the 'middle beat.' <i>(1958,NZ Sheep Station,22</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> I was coasting along on the middle beat, about halfway up the face, when the man on the bottom struck a big sour Hereford bull ... <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,60</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> A hard beat is one of the middle beats and very often includes bluffs and also much need to run dogs on the blind. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,6</i>)

>  <b>GIBSON</b> I was given the middle beat on the face. <i>(1990,Among the High Hills of Canterbury,73</i>)

### Middle Earth
 
 <i>n.</i> New Zealand, from association with the Tolkien trilogy, Lord of the Rings

>  The Tararua Tramping Club, pitching itself as "Middle Earth's oldest tramping club", is holding a new member's night on Tuesday. <i>(Jan 28, 2004,Canterbury Times,17</i>)

>  But, aside from gratuitous mentions of hobbits and grand Middle Earth scenery, exactly what is it that will set New Zealand pinot noir apart from pinot noirs from California, Oregon.. and Patagonia? <i>(Jan 31, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  Now everybody sighs when I say that I, too, lived in the land of Middle Earth, where sheep outnumber people and the entire population equals that of Manhattan's Upper West Side. <i>(Feb 7, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

### Middle Island
 
 <i>n.</i> early name for the South Island

>  The telegraph vote.. was sneeringly characterised by a Canterbury member as "a sop thrown to the Middle Island." <i>(Feb 12, 1864,Otago Witness,2</i>)

>  Although the west coast of the Middle Island abounded in minerals, timber and splendid harbours, and also contained no inconsiderable amount of land available for settlement, it was for, the most part, a terra incognita. <i>(1873,NZ Parliamentary Debates,14/646</i>)

>  The Southern Alps in the Middle Island, form such a prominent feature in the landscape, that even the uncsientific traveller, when he passes along the west coast, cannot but admire their wild and fantastic forms, clad in a garment of snow, and standing boldly against the deep blue sky of New Zealand. <i>(Nov 6, 1861,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,4</i>)

>  A correspondent of a Dunedin paper, writing on the distress prevalent on the Middle Island says : "Let me refer back eight or ten years ago." <i>(Feb 9, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> By 1865 public men were referring to the South as a distinct economic and social unit - 'the civilization of the Middle Island'. <i>(1980,Southern Gentry,68</i>)

>  They are found especially in the northern half of the Middle Island ... <i>(1907,TrNZI,39/288</i>)

>  <b>IRVINE & ALPERS</b> Colonel Wakefield.. had in 1844 commissioned Frederick Tuckett, a surveyor and engineer.. to examine the eastern coast of the Middle Island with a view to selecting a site for the "New Edinburgh" settlement. <i>(1902,Progress of NZ in the 20th Century,167</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [Middle Island](mar-moa.md#middle-island))

That of the Middle Isle is, Te Wai Pounamu - the water of the green stone. <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,193</i>)

### miha
 
 <i>n.</i> a symbol based on the young curled fern frond

>  Remember the miha? A simple and effective logo commissioned by the Export Institute 1984 for promoting and exporting New Zealand products under a common banner, the symbol was based on the young curled fern frond. <i>(Dec 3, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  The symbolic fern frond which created controversy when selected as a new national and `emotional' symbol for New Zealand's exports is officially a failure. Launched less than a year ago, the miha, which is a young ponga frond, is to be let to wither and die. For three years the Export Institute wrestled with the concept of a new national emblem which would uniquely identify New Zealand goods and services. <i>(Apr 6, 1986,NZ Times,1</i>)

>  ... miha logo promoted by the New Zealand Export Institute. ...The miha symbol, a stylised curling fern frond, had come in for criticism. ... <i>(Jun 1, 1985,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  By all means let us have the miha as our national symbol; that is, if we want to be perceived as a snail in its shell - or curled up in the foetal position. Barbara Goodman (Auckland) <i>(Jun 1, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

### mihi
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori welcome [ORIGIN: Maori, first cit. below is of another mihi noun; two citations are of mihi verb] (See [mihi whakatau](../M#mihi-whakatau))

>  Why not pay our mihi to our kaumatua of show business, and get the past out of the way? <i>(Jul 29, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

>  The purpose of the mihi is to advise hosts of your identity, of where you come from - biologically and geographically. <i>(2005,AA Directions,Spring, p22</i>)

>  I've been reading Taiohi since it came out, and I'd like to say a big mihi out to everyone who helps Taiohi stay rolling. <i>(2003,Te Mana  Taiohi,7,Winter 2003 ed.</i>)

>  He's also uneasy about being exposed to too much mihi, or Maori speech-making... <i>(Apr 27, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  The mihi was written in the language of the tangata whenua in the first instance to acknowledge and address the dignity and pride of the people in Aotearoa today. <i>(Jan 30, 2000,NZ Catholic,17</i>)

>  In nearly all the aquaria prepared with mud from Eyretown <i>Daphnia similis</i>, (mihi), has been successfully hatched ... <i>(1891,NZ Journal of Science,1/130</i>)

>  [Speeches] consisted of greetings and references to Rata [the deceased whose tangi it was] (what the Maori calls a mihi), then welcomes to the visitors ... <i>(1945,Journal of the Polynesian Society,54/106</i>) [NOTES: (Beaglehole)]

>  <b>MASON</b> WERIHE. ... Now we just send him our mihi, our aroha, and our warmth. He will need them. <i>(1969,Awatea,95,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MITCALFE</b> It might be a good idea to have the parents along on the first Club afternoon, fine way of getting them to feel at home here in the school, you could mihi them in. [fn] mihi: greet, welcome. <i>(1985,Hey Hey Hey,159</i>)

>  He [Buck] had the equally chastening experience of being unable to reply in Maori to the formal mihi on the Rangitukia marae near Ruatoria. <i>(1986,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/16</i>) [NOTES: Sorrenson]

>  This doesn't apply to Maori, though - they will be giving a passionate mihi to the mountains. <i>(Jul 7, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),59</i>)

>  <b>Hui-a-iwi</b> *Karakia/Mihi ... * Confirm Minuites of the Hui-a-iwi held at Te Kuri-a-Tuatai Marae on 28 May 2006 <i>(Jul 1, 2006,Dominion Post,C16</i>)

>  'A good sign,' said Taranaki iwi who had travelled for over four hours to mihi with deep emotion their tipuna. <i>(Nov 16, 2000,City Voice,Parihaka Supplement : [2]</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to greet [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  My family are old-fashioned, but none of us mihi to flax bushes and say prayers before we cut our fingernails. <i>(Nov 9, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)
