### Mihinare
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori Protestant

>  Despite this, Maori stayed with the church and kept their minihare identity. <i>(Jan 24, 2006,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  In fact, the first name given to the Maori church by Maori was "Te Hahi Mihinare" or The Church of Missionaries. This is still used to describe the Maori Anglican Church in Aotearoa today, albeit unofficially. In the Maori world, Anglicans are called Mihinare. <i>(Jan 24, 2006,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> Altogether, there were ... not less than 150 natives, who, according to their custom, being <i>mihanere</i> or Christianised, assembled twice a day for religious service. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,56</i>)

### mihinare
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori Christian convert

>  Portraying missionaries as a curse on Maori does not make for comfortable reading for a Maori mihinare (missionary) like myself. <i>(Jan 24, 2006,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  The Ringatus, a strong factor on this side, are prepared to arrive at a modus vivendi with the mihinare. [ed fn] Missionary <i>(Dec 17, 1928,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/160,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Those natives who are still heathen are generally styled `devils' by the Christianized people; and they themselves adhere to the [153] appellation. Hongi Hongi, ... in answer to my inquiry as to whether he was a `mihorari' or a `pikopo'(Catholic), confessed with evident delight that he was a `devil'. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/152</i>)

>  <i>mihanere n.</i> convert to Christianity [Maori, pidgin variant of English word missionary]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),620</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Mihanere, n</i>. a convert to Christianity; a Maori variant of the English word <i>Missionary.</> <i>(1898,Austral English,292</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> <i>Mihanere, n</i>. a convert to Christianity; a Maori variant of the English word <i>Missionary.</> <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,2/11</i>)

>  <i>mihinare n</i>. missionary; once convert to Christianity, now member of Anglican Church. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

### mike
 
 <i>n.</i> ? scrounge; bludge; ?loaf; a scrounge; a nap; hence to have a mickey (mostly Army) 1948 [[? Les Cleveland's or P note:] `I haven't found anybody who has heard this usage in N.Z. after this 1939 occurrence.' L.C. notes: <i>Micky's Broken Doll</i>, a 1939 N.Z. adaptation of <i>Aussies Broken Doll,</i> a WWI song probably from A.I.F. micky = Mick Savage [? <i>mike</i> a play on Michael Savage] Part: <i>Dict of Force Slang </i>1948 cites <i>Mike</i>. A scrounge, a nap; hence to have a mike [mostly army] <i>Miker. </i>A work-shy L.C. notes: <i>Micky's Broken Doll</i>, a 1939 N.Z. adaptation of <i>Aussies Broken Doll,</i> a WWI song probably from A.I.F. micky = Mick Savage [? <i>mike</i> a play on Michael Savage] Part: <i>Dict of Force Slang </i>1948 cites <i>Mike</i>. A scrounge, a nap; hence to have a mike [mostly army] <i>Miker. </i>A work-shy]

>  <b>CLEVELAND</b> You think that I've been miking all this while;/ Why I took a chance,/ Left one ball in France,/ And now I'm Micky's broken doll. <i>(1939,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: 1939 Trentham Camp BF Shepherd to L. Cleveland 12/4/76]

### miker
 
 <i>n.</i> a work-shy person [[?Les Cleveland's or P's note] I haven't found anybody who has heard this usage in N.Z. after this 1939 occurrence.]

### miki
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Coprosma propinqua</i>, a shrub

>  <i>miki [=] coprosma </i>[s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,49</i>)

### miki-miki
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) mingimingi (<i>Coprosma propinqua</i>), the branches of which are used for mustering sticks. [SI variant of Maori mingimingi]

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> Mustering sticks are usually nearly shoulder high .. manuka is the commonest wood, but Miki-miki and Hazel are sometimes used. <i>(1951,NZ High Country,24</i>)

>  mickymick.- a native shrub from which sticks are cut. <i>(Nov 4, 1933,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> micky-mick: corruption of the Maori name mingimingi (Coprosma propinqua) a native shrub which will yield excellent mustering sticks, which in some quarters are preferred even to manuka. [micky-mick; mickamick; micky; miki] <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,53</i>)

>  <b>Relph</b> We slept in a tent under beech trees, on mattresses of 'mikimiki' - springy coprosma branches. <i>(2007,From Tussocks to Tourists,67</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> mingimingi; usually <i>Cyathodes acerosa (C. juniperina, </i>Williams) or <i>Coprosma </i>spp., esp. <i>C. propinqua, C. linarifolia</i>, a thorny shrub. [|Cyathodes <i>have prickles</i>; Coprosmas <i>not: ?separate out on that characteristic: whether Cyathodes or Coprosma: also note that Leucopogon now - Cyathodes] </i> [[?SI mainly Cyathodes]]

>  Mingi (mikimiki) slab. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,589</i>)

>  I know the starlings feed on the native miki-miki berries while they are in season. <i>(Oct 30, 1890,Otago Witness,20</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Thus spear grass, and scrub called tomataguru and michi michi grow on hard ground. <i>(1857,Southern Districts of Otago,323</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... a clump of miki-mik <i>(1949,High Country Days,98</i>) [NOTES: (Marlborough or Canterbury)]

>  <b>HARPER</b> There is also the rata tree, the prince of firewoods, ... miki-miki, kamahi, or so-called red-birch, white and black birches (which are really beeches), the mountain broom, ribbon wood, broad leaf, totara, and many others, ... and always available in the Ranges [sc. Alps]. <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,35</i>)

>  <i>... C[oprosma] linariifolia</i> (the mikimiki, or yellow-wood), which, although rare in Wellington, is commoner in the South Island, where it atains a good size and is remarkable for the yellow colour of the wood. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/265</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> a SI (Kai-tahu) form of MINGIMINGI: BLUE/ YELLOW MIKIMIKI The Maori boys would eat the [13] blue mikimiki [?Cathodes] but not the yellow mikimiki [prob. Coprosma], which they said was poisonous. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,12</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The under-scrub was very dense, Matipo, Ini-Ini, Konini, Pokaka, Mick-a-Mick, Ohau, Ribbonwood, Ngaio, Lemonwood and others, ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,242,1954 ed.</i>)

>  Micky-mick A corrupt form of mingi-mingi, the Maori name of a class of shrubs, one of which makes a good walking-stick. (Pronounce<i> mick|-i-mick|</i>). <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),258</i>)

>  <b>McNEISH</b> The smoke rose in spirals, carrying with it the aspic odours of their supper and the odours of the bush, leaf-mould, sap and pollen, mikimiki and liverwort and the woody fragrance of manuka. <i>(1970,Mackenzie,14</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> It already has rats and shags and the mikkimik. <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,167</i>)

>  We had endless hours to roam the hillsides in search of mikimiki and snowberries. <i>(Nov 9, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,61</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Coprosma </i> [various spellings]

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> [<i>Coprosma linariifolia,</i> Hook f.,] The `Mikimik' or `Yellow Wood' of the Otago settler. A tall shrub, ... <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,72</i>)

>  Maple trees, totara, miki miki, broadleaf, lancewood, all entangled by a dense undergrowth of ferns and parasitic creepers. <i>(Jun 6, 1885,Adelaide Observer,41</i>) [NOTES: 1885 June 6 The Adelaide Observer 41 in 1987 Hill Richard Henry of Resolution Island 88]

>  <i>mikimiki [=] coprosma</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,49</i>)

>  A patch of mic-a-mic gave out onto open ground [perhaps a misprint for <i>mick-a-mick</i>] <i>(1900,NZ Illustrated Magazine,3/254</i>) [NOTES: (Canterbury)]

>  <i>Mickymick.</i>- A native shrub from which sticks are cut. [Generally Coprosma linarifolia.--A.W.] <i>(Nov 4, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>MARTIN</b> A majority of small bushy shrubs with twiggy branches and small opposite leaves are Coprosmas, colloquially known as `wiggy bushes' or `miki-miks', this name being a corruption of the Southern Maori miki-miki. <i>Ibid.</i> 236 <i>Coprosma spp.</i> Miki-miki <i>(1961,Flora NZ,215</i>)

>  <b>PEARSON</b> mickeymick: A compact twiggy scrub with small leaves of the genus <i>Coprosma.</i> (From Maori<i> mingimingi</i>) <i>(1963,Coal Flat,421</i>) [NOTES: (West Coast)]

### miko
 
 <i>n.</i> edible heart of the leaf sheath, or young shoot, of the NIKAU PALM <i>Rhopalostylis sapida.</i>

>  Breakfasted off sea amemone and <i>miko. </i> <i>(1846,Notes of an Expedition to Kawatiri and Araura</i>) [NOTES: 1846 Heaphy Exped. to Kawatiri in 1959 Taylor Early Travs. 220]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Miko. <i>Areca sapida.</i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

### mikoikoi
 
 <i>n.</i> a plant

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Libertia ixioides (The Ixia-like Libertia) </i>... Maori name, <i>Mikoikoi. </i> <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,113</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>LIBERTIA ... ixioides</i> ... New Zealand Iris. MIKOIKOI. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,21</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Libertia ixioides</i> MIKOIKOI ... Native iris ... Tuukaauki <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,296</i>)

### Mikotahi Rock
 
 <i>n.</i> Geol. definition

>  Douch, C.J. The Quarternary Geology of Mikotahi Rock and the Coastal Area Soutwest of New Plymouth. <i>(Jun 1974,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,37/19</i>)

### mild
 
 <i>adj.</i> (of cheddar cheese)

>  Cheddar is our national cheese, so familiar that sometimes it's not even called cheddar, but simply cheese, and described only in terms of depth of flavour - mild or tasty. <i>(Dec 14, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

### mild-mosaic
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a viral disease of commercial potato crops.

>  Mild-mosaic, rugose-mosaic, aucuba-mosaic, leaf-drop, stipple-streak, spindle-tuber, curly-dwarf, leaf-roll, streak and wildings are all present in the New-Zealand-grown commercial lines. <i>(Jan 21, 1929,NZ Journal of Agriculture,6</i>)

### mile-a-minute
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Clematis vitalba</i>, old man's beard, traveller's joy

>  The fast-growing introduced vine Old Man's Beard is smothering native bush in many parts of the city and surrounding hills, ... Also known as Clematis vitalba, Traveller's Joy and Mile-a-Minute, the climber spreads rapidly. <i>(May 8, 1984,Karori and Western Suburbs News,8</i>)

### military defaulter
 
 <i>n.</i> a conscientious objector

>  This modest paperback recalls one of the murkier areas of our social and legal history - the treatment, in the 1940s, of "military defaulters" (as the law then defined objectors to military service on grounds of conscience). <i>(May 12, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>) [NOTES: "Decision: behind the Wire in New Zealand", by W J Foote.]

### military john
 
 <i>n.</i> a military policeman (cf JOHN n)

>  <b>BRUNO</b> A well lit chaplain's batman lick[s] three military johns. <i>(1948,Maleesh George,5</i>)

### military road
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MATHEW</b> With a view to open up the country in this direction, a Military Road is in progress up the valley of the Hutt. <i>(1845,Present State of NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1845 F.Mathew Present State ... of NZ in 1940 Founding of NZ 239]

### military settlement
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The <i>Inflexible</i> has returned from a cruise, undetaken for the purpose of finding site, which should be at once distant from Auckland, and suitable for the location of the newly-arrived pensioners. Why the original intention of establishing this Military settlement near the capital, as recommended in Lord Grey's dispatch, should have been abandoned - if, in truth, it has been abandoned - is easy enough to understand, although the want of absolute and certai[n] knowledge disincline one to risk asserting as much in express terms. <i>(Sep 18, 1847,New Zealander, The,2</i>)

>  The only other thing spoken of, we believe, was a scheme which the Government has in contemplation, which may considerably affect the fortunes of the province if carried out, and that is the introduction of a large number of immigrants from Europe, and their location upon the unoccupied bush lands (of which there are still about 36,000 acres) as a half military settlement - the cost of their bringing out, and of their land, or a part of it, to be repaid by a certain term of military service and by work on the roads. <i>(Nov 22, 1862,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  Where is Queen's Town, the native military settlement, - the supposed auxiliary to the Pensioner force? <i>(Dec 24, 1852,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  I hope that no very long time will elapse before the first of the men who are to compose this force may be sent; and if it should be found that there is really the facility I expect in enrolling and sending to New Zealand military settlers on the plan I have described, the measure may hereafter be extended, and military settlements will probably be formed not only in the northern part of New Zealand, but also, in concert with the New Zealand Company, in the neighbourhood of Cook's Straits - I am, &c. <i>(Jul 10, 1847,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  His Excellency was pleased with the picturesque appearance of this military settlement, and at the elements of prosperity visible on all sides. <i>(Mar 9, 1870,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  Deceased came to New Zealand in 1860, and was stationed with her husband at the military settlement at Puketapu, and afterwards at Clive. <i>(Sep 23, 1912,Poverty Bay Herald,5</i>)

>  These military settlement blocks must necessarily be in remote districts. <i>(Sep 15, 1891,Wanganui Chronicle,2</i>)

>  <b>NORRIS</b> These military settlements were formed with the object of stopping the Maoris, who had retreated to what is now called the King Country ... <i>(1964,Settlers in Depression,1</i>)

>  The Government are setting aside a block of land, about 5000 acres of the settlement lease of Mount Egmont, as a semi-military settlement ... <i>(Mar 26, 1885,West Coast Times,2</i>)

### military settler
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Notwithstanding that is the feeling of the representative of the Local Government here, he, as every man so circumstanced would most readily and anxiously do, called to his aid the Local Magistrates, and in accordance with their wishes, has authorized the organization of the inhabitants into a volunteer corp, and has entrusted to an efficient military settler the arrangement of a battery for he protection of the town, only requiring, as we think he is entitled to do, that no steps shall be taken by any <i>irresponsible</i> individuals in issuing any orders to the inhabitants unless they shall first have been submitted either to himself or some of the Magistrates of the district, and sanctioned by their approval. <i>(Jun 28, 1843,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

>  SEC. VII. - MILITARY SETTLERS .. Military and Naval Officers will be allowed in all parts of New Zealand where there are Waste Lands of the Crown, the privileges accorded to them by the Regulations of May 1851 ... <i>(Apr 5, 1853,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  We (Military Settlers) are now quite separate from the old Militia, and in many ways under much stricter discipline ... [sic] [make sure addition not by ed.] <i>(Aug 7, 1864,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/119,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Pitcairn, from Omata Stockade)]

>  <b>S. PERCY SMITH</b> [British troops] are every bit as good [as bushmen] as the Military Settlers who formed part of the column which took Opotiki ... <i>(Feb 26, 1867,Taranaki Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1867 Feb 26 S. Percy Smith Taranaki Journal in 1922 Cowan NZ Wars 2/534]

>  <b>GUDGEON</b> The military settlers had been enrolled [?] only a few weeks. <i>(1879,Reminiscences of the War in NZ,27</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> He became captain in 1863, and served in the Military Settlers, and later in the Armed Constabulary as Sub-Inspector. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/139 A message was sent to Wairo for Major Fraser and his company of Military Settlers ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/177,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The Armed Constabulary were armed with carbines; only the Military Settlers carried the Enfield. <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,137</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> The other redoubts were garrisoned by the Hawkes Bay Miltary Settlers, and those from Taranaki. <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,142</i>)

>  <b>NORRIS</b> It was hoped to tempt some of the goldminers in the South Island and Australia to enlist as military settlers. <i>(1963,Armed Settlers,16</i>)

>  <b>NORRIS</b> As a contribution to the [Maori War] war effort, the New Zealand Government had raised four regiments of military settlers <i>(1964,Settlers in Depression,1</i>)

>  <b>BELICH</b> The colonial forces fell into three categories: two small permanent corps, the Colonial Defence Force (cavalry) and the Forest Rangers; the Waikato Militia or `Military Settlers'; and the Auckland Militia and Volunteers. <i>(1986,NZ Wars, The,125</i>)

>  <b>SORRENSON</b> In north Taranaki the Maori situation was even more hopeless since the confiscated land had already been occupied by military settlers. <i>(1986,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/13</i>)

>  <b>IHIMAERA</b> Seventy Hawke's Bay [81] Military Settlers. Fifty Hawke's Bay Volunteers. <i>(1986,Matriarch, The,80</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> <i>Military Settlers. </i> <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),337</i>)

>  <b>NORRIS</b> ... I am directed to authorize you to proceed to New South Wales for the purpose of enrolling a company of Military Settlers for service in the Northern Island of New Zealand, under the conditions dated 5th August 1863, published in the New Zealand Gazette of the 12th September <i>(1963,Armed Settlers,21</i>) [NOTES: 1863 Dec 31 Colonial Defence Office in 1963 Norris Armed Settlers 21]

>  MILITARY SETTLERS Conditions upon which land in the North Island will be granted to settlers willing to perform the after-mentioned military services:- <i>(1863,NZ Gazette</i>) [NOTES: c1863-64 NZ Gazette in 1879 Featon Waikato War 51]

>  The cost to the colony (GBP1092 5s) of the Naval and Military Settlers' Land Claims Commission was made up as follows... <i>(Sep 26, 1898,Nelson Evening Mail,3</i>)

>  Mr Bridger Flint, an old military settler of the Wairoa Gorge, was found dead on the Marine Parade to-day. <i>(Jun 21, 1901,Colonist (Nelson),3</i>)

>  Replying to the Hon. C. M. Luke, in the Legislative Council yesterday afternoon, the Hon. H. D. Bell, leader of the Council, said that in accordance with its promise in the Financial Statement, the Government was giving full consideration to the report of the Public Petitions Committee on the petitions from military settlers. <i>(Oct 5, 1912,Poverty Bay Herald,3</i>)

### militia
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Before his resignation in 1861, Governor Gore-Brown had set up arrangements for a Militia in Auckland to supplement existing government forces. <i>(Feb 2002,NZ Memories,No. 34/6</i>)

### milk
 
 <i>v.</i> to steal, take surreptitiously

>  <i>(Feb 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,163/, 164/4</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> And this bloke gives us a hand to milk his boss's car [sc. illicitly, of petrol] <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,18</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (of land) to support cows in milk; (of farmers) to run cows [[There is a similar usage with SHEAR. The run shears 10,000 sheep]]

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> [This property] will milk 20 cows. <i>(1936,Follow the Call,11</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> They milked between 50 and 60 cows. <i>(1930,Early Canterbury Runs,41</i>) [NOTES: 1946 edition]

>  <b>ACLAND</b> The largest farmer milks at least 1000 cows <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,30</i>) [NOTES: 1951 [?1930] Acland ECR 30 [check discards]]

### Milk Authority
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical)

>  <b>163/1</b> <i>(Feb 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture</i>)

>  "Board" or "Milk Board" means a Metropolitan Milk Board constituted under this Act. <i>Ibid.</i> 320 "Milk Authority".. means the Metropolitan Milk Board or.. the Borough Council prescribed by Order in Council under this Act as the Milk Authority for the district. <i>Ibid.</i> 324 For every milk district there shall be a Milk Authority. <i>Ibid.</i> 340 The principal function of each Milk Authority under the Act shall be to ensure for the inhabitants of the milk district an adequate supply of milk of a standard not less than the standard prescribed under the Sales of Food and Drugs Act, 1908. <i>(1944,NZ Statutes,30/319</i>)

>  A committee of supply.. arranges with the local milk authority to supply all the milk for that district. <i>(1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,163</i>)

### milk bar
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) a shop selling soft drinks, milk shakes, confectionery and also light snacks, etc.; a dairy [C. Ransom notes: Too sweeping: they exist in US under various names]

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> Outside Barry suggested a milkshake, so you went into a milk bar and you sat in a booth sucking raspberry shakes through straws. ... [Carole] was with a High School joker ..., and she stood at the bar with him eating sundaes. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,123,1976 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Milk bars, mostly rather pale imitations of their American counterparts, appeared in the 1950s, and these were soon replaced by coffee houses. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,33</i>) [NOTES: Not true milkbars were around in 1940 to my factual knowledge as Ballantyne shows; see also Winks re drug stores]

>  milkbar (forerunner of dairy with more limited stock) <i>Ibid.</i>6 <i>(1988,University Entrance English,3</i>) [NOTES: Once when she couldn't have me Dad let me go to the flicks by myself. He gave me some extra money, so I went to the milkbar and bought a gobstopper and some other lollies. I stuffed my face, and felt crook all the way home. He bought new footy boots for my eldest brother too, and an Art Union ticket for Mum. He must have had a beaut time up the RSA that day, I reckon.]

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> ... the manager of the Majestic Theatre who had been seen embracing the girl from the <i>Age</i> office in a milk bar in town ... <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,22,1976 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WINKS</b> Milk bars do not exist in America - ice cream, `pop' (never called `fizz drink'. thought to be a childish term), ... are obtained at ... a drug store. <i>(1954,These New Zealanders,62,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CROWTHER</b> During the early months of this year [1956] much publicity was given in the Christchurch newspapers to the fact that large numbers of young people were to be seen, especially at the weekends, in the central areas of the city. These young people congregated in the streets and milk-bars. Many of them had motor-cycles ... At various times these young people were referred to as `Teddy Boys', `Milk Bar Cowboys' and `Bodgies and Widgies'. <i>(1956,Street Society in Christchurch,1</i>)

>  <b>GOLDBLATT</b> The milk bars, the rendezvous of the `Land of the Long White Cloud', are interspersed and barnacled to the Picture House. <i>(1957,Democracy at Ease,12</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> She ... leaned her bicycle against the verandah-post outside the milk-bar. <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,84,1986 ed.</i>)

>  <b>KEINZLY</b> The milk bar sold sausages, it sold vegetables, it sold groceries but it didn't sell disinfectant. <i>(1960,Tangahano,43</i>)

>  <b>DUCKWORTH</b> I enjoyed my job at the milk-bar ... Each time I fastened my apron at the back of the shop I felt domestic and cosy, ... <i>(1963,Barbarous Tongue, A,44</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> I remember I was jiving with a girl ... who worked in a milkbar in the township. <i>(1964,Walk on the Beach,161</i>) [NOTES: (Not for a Seagull)]



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a calfeteria or feeding system for young calves.

>  Mobile milk bars carry and station anywhere, inside or out, on trailer or tray. <i>(Jun 1994,NZ Farmer,49</i>)

>  Equipment for feeding calves: a milk bar. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### milk billy
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a metal can used for collecting milk from the cowshed [or for storing milk in safe or fridge.]

>  <b>HARRISON</b> 'Here Sue, here Tip,' she called, putting the milk billy down in the centre of the drive. <i>(1986,She's a Fair Cow,14</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> She trailed over to us with a basket in her hand, the milk billy in the other. <i>(1912,Stories,113</i>) [NOTES: 1984 Alpers]

>  <b>CASEY</b> He was clutching the handle of a battered milk billy, which he was waving wildly about to help explain to Rex. <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,232</i>)

>  Fresh milk was delivered daily and the good old milk billy was a very important commodity in each homestead. <i>(Jun 2002,NZ Memories,No. 36/18</i>)

### milk biscuit
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a compressed milk powder forming a biscuit, produced for aid to overseas countries.

>  Why on earth doesn't New Zealand market her milk biscuits in Western countries? Milk biscuits are the ideal form of distributing milk to children. Mr Miller, 49, is on the distributing end of the milk biscuit chain to India. <i>(Aug 1972,NZ Dairy Exporter,32</i>)

### Milk Board
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical)

>  <i>(1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,305/3</i>) [NOTES: [OEDS]]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> <i>(1959,Descriptive Atlas,76</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Bird droppings coated the milk-lines and milk-board. <i>(1978,Elver, The,36</i>)

>  The Milk Act by sponsoring the formation of milk boards or milk authorities increased the opportunity for a more local interest in the operation of these control measures. <i>(Oct 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,303</i>)

>  At the time a government-funded quango, the New Zealand Milk Board, used to do everything. <i>(May 22, 2004,Dominion Post,C8</i>)

### milk boy
 
 <i>n.</i> a milk delivery boy

>  Milkboy injured [hdg] A milkboy was taken to hospital with bruises to his head and elbow and a groin injury after he fell off the back of a milk truck at Porirua yesterday. <i>(Aug 21, 1984,Evening Post,3</i>)

### milk can
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TURNER</b> If the farmer supplies a cheese factory, there will be a <i>milkstand</i>, where the <i>milk cans</i> (usually holding twenty or twenty-five gallons) are loaded for the factory; ... <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,149</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The milk-cans ought to be washed immediately when he gets home from the factory. <i>(1895,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,III/89</i>)

>  On each cart two or three milk-cans gleam in the morning sunlight. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,48</i>)

### Milk Cart Derby
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Between the shafts, strong, shaggy, horses stand quietly, and on each cart two or three milk-cans gleam in the morning sunlight [at Ruatoki]. It's the end of the `Milk Cart Derby'. For the last hour or so the carts have been rattling in along the rough roadways leading in from the 120 farms that comprise the settlement. [49] ... The `Milk Cart Derby,' as it is known to many who have seen it, is the key to Ruatoki. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,48</i>)

### milk cheque
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a payment made to a farmer-supplier to a dairy factory. (See [payout](p-per.md#payout))

>  .. dairy farmers are doing very well, and have no cause to complain of their milk cheques, which constitute a record for the Auckland District. <i>(Jan 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,71</i>)

>  Cengel Started with nothing and in fact when I got my first milk cheque I owed the Dairy Company. <i>(Dec 10, 2000,Fencepost.com</i>)

>  For Sale: 60 acres. Three acres of lucerne. Extra good butter-fat returns and milk cheques open for inspection. <i>(Jul 7, 1923,Hawera Star,1</i>)

>  <b>TINSLEY</b> One morning, Abo was called into the factory to pick up his milk cheque. <i>(1985,Cheese Punchers' Ball, The,126</i>)

>  About 3400 of New Zealand's 5200 sharemilkers are on 50:50 arrangements in which they provide the cows, the farm owner provides the land, and the milk cheque is split down the middle. <i>(Dec 21, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,15</i>)

>  <b>WARR</b> There was now [by 1900] a regular monthly milk cheque from the local dairy factory ... <i>(1988,From Bush-Burn to Butter,56</i>)

### milk harvester
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dairy farm worker employed only to milk cows.

 <i>n.</i>

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a ewe producing milk for her lamb/s.

### milk off
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [land] to carry dairy cows.

>  Mixed farming at its best: 830ac - 500 ac good grass, 63 paddocks. Milks off river flats (140 ac) could milk up to 200. <i>(Feb 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,78</i>)

>  Having a fertile paddock is important if you want the paddock ready to milk off in the early spring. <i>(Jun 1970,NZ Dairy Exporter,7</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to separate, of a break of 4 (rather than the needed 3) sheep in a group at a dog trial

>  If he could milk one off at the back he'd be right. <i>(May 25, 1986,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: Commentator Gordon on `A Dog's Show']

### milk on
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to use [an area of land] for grazing and milking cows.

>  A property all set to convert, currently 142 hectares being milked on and balance used for intensive dairy grazing and wintering. <i>(Feb 2, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,38</i>)

### milk out
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to drain a cow's milksacs completely.

>  After calving leave the calves with their mothers for four days, bringing in the cows each morning to be milked out. Any animals prone to milk fever should not be milked out too much for the first few days. <i>(Mar 1967,NZ Journal of Agriculture,37</i>)

>  An average cow giving 10 litres milks out in 5 minutes. <i>(Oct 1999,NZ Dairy Exporter,26</i>)

>  <i>(Jun 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,568/1</i>) [NOTES: [OEDS]]

 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the production history of a dairy cow or herd.

### milk replacer
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a calf feed substitute for mother's milk.

### milk room
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a cowshed area in which a milk vat is located.

>  He has also retained the original milk room and there are plans to add another 900 litre unit next month. <i>(2002,Opunake and Coastal News,14</i>)

### milk round
 
 <i>n.</i> the daily milk run, also called milk run; a) the route covered by a milk deliverer; b) any routine delivery tour with frequent stops.

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> nickname of the programme of employers' interviews on campus(Victoria University)

A wide range of public and private sector employers make themselves available for informal interviews with students of <i>all</i> disciplines; <i>(Apr 27, 1981,Salient,18</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [milk round](mar-moa.md#milk-round))

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,149</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a substance, stockfood or contaminant that causes milk to have an unpalatable flavour or smell.

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>phr.</i> steal money from the till (See [cf Baxter Ballad of Barney Flanagan](../C#cf-baxter-ballad-of-barney-flanagan))

### milk thistle
 
 <i>n.</i> the edible herbaceous wild plant <i>Sonchus oleraceus</i>, and <i>S. asper</i>, also called Maori cabbage, sow thistle, puha, rauriki

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> SOUR THISTLE [SOWTHISTLE] (<i>Sonchus oleraceus</i>) is frequently called milk thistle, from its milky juice, and in the north is quite commonly given its Maori name of Rauriki. It is exceedingly common and well known in all gardens and cultivated fields. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,209,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> We also found [at Queen Charlotte Sound] a species of sow-thistle (<i>sonchus oleraceus</i>,) and a kind of plant which our people called lamb's quarters (<i>tetragonia cornuta</i>,) which we frequently used as sallads; ... <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/200</i>)

>  <i>Sonchus </i> <i>(1846,Calendar of St John's College</i>) [NOTES: (And.)]

>  <i>sow thistle</i> [s]<i> Sonchus oleraceus</i> L. <i>(1966,Standard Common Names for Weeds,72</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [not necessaarily NZ exclusive]

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> The natives had a large copper mowrie to cook the pork they had brought with them, and for the first time I saw them eat the common milk-[52]thistle. They cook it as constantly as the leaves of the wild turnip and cabbage, and seem to relish it just as much. It is so precisely like the English milk-thistle that I am almost inclined to think it must have come with the potatoes. <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,51,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> ... the prevailing species of trees are remo [rimu], totara, and tall manuka, all indicative of inferior soil.... The bush-like woods on the east side are of white birch, likewise no recommendation of the soil, but the chief objection to the district is, that the prairie soil affords scarcely any food for cattle ... it grows only the wiry tree grass [tussock] ... and <i>Phormium tenax</i> [flax], no aniseed, perennial groundsel, or milk thistle, and very little couch grass. <i>(Aug 16, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Aug 16 Tuckett's Diary in 1898 Hocken contributions 223-224]

>  The Puwha, or [31] Milk-thistle (<i>Sonchus oleraceus</i>), the large-leaved variety was common, though not (it is reasonably suspected) too plentiful; and this was abandoned for the smaller-leaved European kind (after its introduction) as being less bitter and more palatable. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/30-31</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> ... the prevailing species of trees are remo [rimu], totara, and tall manuka, all indicative of inferior soil.... The bush-like woods on the east side are of white birch, likewise no recommendation of the soil, but the chief objection to the district is, that the prairie soil affords scarcely any food for cattle ... it grows only the wiry tree grass [tussock] ... and <i>Phormium tenax</i> [flax], no aniseed, perennial groundsel, or milk thistle, and very little couch grass. <i>(Aug 16, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Aug 16 Tuckett's Diary in 1898 Hocken contributions 223-224]

>  The Puwha, or [31] Milk-thistle (<i>Sonchus oleraceus</i>), the large-leaved variety was common, though not (it is reasonably suspected) too plentiful; and this was abandoned for the smaller-leaved European kind (after its introduction) as being less bitter and more palatable. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/30</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> SOUR THISTLE [SOWTHISTLE] (<i>Sonchus oleraceus</i>) is frequently called milk thistle, from its milky juice, and in the north is quite commonly given its Maori name of Rauriki. It is exceedingly common and well known in all gardens and cultivated fields. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,209,1967 ed.</i>)

>  MILK THISTLE (<i>Silybum marianum</i>), sometimes called St. Mary's thistle, variegated or spotted thistle, and, near Akaroa, French thistle ... . It is abundant in scattered localities in both islands, sometimes forming dense masses. It is an annual or biennial, grows up to 6 ft. in height, and has large leaves without wings. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,78</i>)

>  MILK THISTLE (<i>Silybum marianum</i>), sometimes called St. Mary's thistle, variegated or spotted thistle, and, near Akaroa, French thistle ... . It is abundant in scattered localities in both islands, sometimes forming dense masses. It is an annual or biennial, grows up to 6 ft. in height, and has large leaves without wings. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,78</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> I had one [wart] Norm,.. finger left hand, rubbed it with sow thistle milk, gone in a flash, not a mark. Beauty boy. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,222</i>)

### milk tree
 
 <i>n.</i> also called milkwood, turepo a native forest tree with milky sap. <i>Paratrophis microphylla<i> (formerly <i>P. heterophylla</i> (Laing and Blackwell)), and <i>Epicarpus microphyllus </i>(Morris)

>  The "milk trees" (Streblus) of New Zealand belong to the same family (Moraceae) as the fig tree and its many relatives. <i>(Nov 5, 2005,Dominion Post,Indulgence 23</i>)

>  <i>Paratrophis microphylla</i> milk tree <i>(1916,Dunedin Field Club Plant Catalogues</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>P[aratrophis] heterophylla, Bl.Mus</i>. ... NORTH ISLAND: ... <i>Turepo; Milk-tree.</i> ... Abounding in milky sap, which is said to be palatable. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,632</i>)

>  The coast milk-tree (<i>Paratrophis Banksi</i>)[sic] is one of those species which is now found mainly on islands ... <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,7/379</i>) [NOTES: (Oliver Poor Knight Island)]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> [Tree, sometimes 30-40 ft. in height. If a slit is cut in the bark of this tree, a thick, sweet, milky juice will flow. ... Both islands.] <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,141</i>)

>  milk-tree A small New Zealand tree whose bark gives out a milky sap. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),258</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> THE MILK-TREE (TUREPO) The turepo (<i>Paratrophis microphylla</i> ) trunk will slowly exude a creamy sap if it is cut or bruised. The sap was used as a substitute for milk by early colonists. <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,84</i>)

>  Those pioneers who were without dairy cattle made a kind of `milk' from the creamy sap of the turepo or milk tree (<i>Paratrophis microphylla</i> ). <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,20</i>)

>  TUREPO (Milk Tree)/ <i>Paratrophis microphylla</i> / Tree reaching 12m. Bark whitish grey with noticeable breathing pores. <i>(1982,Field Guide to Common NZ Trees & Shrubs,14</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> The `Milk-tree' of the Nelson and Wellington colonists is described by Mr. Bidwill and Dr. Sinclair as producing a milk which is used with tea, and is equally suitable for the purpose with that of the cow; it is also drunk as it flows from the tree, and is good and sweet, but has a vegetable after-taste. - A large tree, 60 feet high, ... often extremely like <i>Carpodetus Serratus</i>. ... Nat. name `Towai', <i>Raoul. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 224</i>)

>  Milk tree, or Tawaapou (<i>Epicarpurus microphyllus</i>). The Milk tree of the settlers.... exuding a vegetable milk when wounded. <i>Ibid.</i> 48 Milk tree. Epicarpus microphyllus, Raoul. <i>(1868,TrNZI,1/37</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan, Botany Otago.) [<i>Williams</i> gives <i>Tawapou</i> (p. 406) as <i>Planchonella . (sideroxylon) Novozelandica</i>, a tree. Perhaps. Buchanan confused this with Maori <i>Tawau</i> `milky juice (of plants)']]

>  <i>Paratrophis microphylla</i> milk-tree <i>(1880,TrNZI,12/</i>)

>  <i>Paratrophis microphylla</i> milk-tree <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Milk-tree, n</i>. a New Zealand tree, <i>Epicarpurus microphyllus</i>, Raoul. <i>(1898,Austral English,292</i>)

>  `Milk-tree ... a tall slender tree exuding a milky sap: wood white and very brittle.' <i>(1873,Catalogue of Vienna Exhibition</i>)

>  [Native name] Turepo .. [Settlers' name] Milk-tree .. [Family] Articeae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,122</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 122]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Paratrophis microphylla</i> milk-tree <i>(1906,Plants of NZ</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

### milkbar
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> The millk bar man was a Communist and liked to talk to me about how we needed a revolution. <i>(1995,Mum,72</i>)

>  In Motueka where I was born / The shopping street's a mile long, / All the shops have a single floor / Except the milkbar with the room above. <i>(Jan 10, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),74</i>)

>  They drove to the milkbar: there they drank milkshakes. <i>(Dec 1956,Landfall,10(4) No. 40 : 288</i>)

>  You realise that what you really want doesn't come from any Milk Bar but from the "Celtic" across the road. <i>(Jul 12, 1943,NZEF Times,III/107 : 5</i>)

>  <b>BOOTH</b> Rejected by her when she discovered he was already married, he stalked Sharon to the milkbar and killed her with one blast from a shotgun. <i>(1997,Deadline : My Story,104</i>)

>  <b>GEE</b> He came down that day into a town of hoardings, shops, traffic lights, where Sunday motor-bike gangs clustered outside milkbars and girls in jeans and leather mini-skirts stood on the corners with jaws moving on cuds of gum. <i>(1972,In My Father's Den,166</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  I would offer up a prayer of thanks to God, whose stern countenance in my imagination was a composite face of my father's.. and the inexorable milk-bar keeper's. <i>(Apr 6, 1966,Weekly News,41</i>)

>  [Hdg] Girl who 'Did the Milk Bar Round.' <i>(May 5, 1954,NZ Truth,11</i>)

>  Older people in fact don't remember the Teds doing very much except hanging around the streets and scaring milkbar customers. <i>(Nov 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  Older people in fact don't remember the Teds doing very much except hanging around the streets and scaring milkbar customers. <i>(Nov 26, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

### milkbar cowboy
 
 <i>n.</i> motor-cyclist youth who with others congregrated at town milkbars (cf CUBA STREET YANK

>  <b>HOFFMAN</b> Here I could sit and drink a milk shake.. observing the slick-backed, greasy-haired milkbar cowboys in their leather jackets who congregated on the footpath on Friday nights [in the 1950s]. <i>(1998,Tales of Anna Hoffman,44</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> There's no real cowboys around here any more. Only milkbar cowboys now. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,45</i>)

>  <b>DUCKWORTH</b> The Rosemarie [milk-bar]? That ghastly place. You'll get all the cowboys and widgies on Saturday. <i>(1963,Barbarous Tongue, A,43</i>)

>  Milkbar cowboys were here! They loitered near milkbars and drove motorbikes. <i>(Nov 27, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),52</i>)

>  "Milkbar cowboys were working-class apprentices, who donned clothing inspired by fighter pilots," says Yska. <i>(Jul 13, 2000,City Voice,3</i>)

>  Hysterical squirts and zig-zags of milkbar cowboy material, namely artificial strawberry, chocolate and lemon syrups, painted the plate like a drunken Picasso. <i>(May 11, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,4</i>)

>  Hamilton Beach's deco brackets, their pods poised above gleaming mixer arms that de;ivered milkshakes in cool, tall aluminium beakers, were the heart of the milk bar at a time when milk was the cool drink. So cool that heavy dudes, Harley riders, even, were known as milk bar cowboys and jukebox music was eroding the moral fabric of the nation. Great milk bars were places of style and social consequence when pubs were slop houses and restaurants served no wine; serious places that demanded serious drinking, and there was nothing more serious than a milkshake. <i>(Apr 1, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),52</i>)

>  New Zealand was plagued with the noisy, untidy nuisance of milkbar cowboys - motorbike gangs often bent on trouble. <i>(1951,NZ Truth,22/3</i>)

>  I knew I was watching real [motor-bike] riders - not just milkbar cowboys. ... Milkbar cowboys, those throttle - happy fellows, could do well to watch TT riders. <i>(Mar 23, 1952,2ZB</i>) [NOTES: 2ZB Sports Session: Wallie Ingram.]

>  [Auckland] `Milk bar cowboys' (also described as youthful motor-cyclists with a smattering of square-dance fever who nightly mob juke boxes) were warned that they would have to behave themselves. <i>(May 14, 1952,NZ Truth,5</i>)

>  [Motor-cyclists allege] that as a body they face unfair criticism because of the antics of a few `milkbar cowboys'. <i>(Sep 2, 1952,Evening Post,[local news page]</i>)

>  A Hastings traffic officer who referred to a speeding motor-cyclist as a `milkbar cowboy' in the Magistrates' Court was reprimanded.... The magistrate said that the traffic officer might earn himself a defam ation of character in the loose use of the term.' ... it appears that the so-called `milkbar cowboy' has very little to do with milk, cows or even boys,' he said. <i>(Aug 4, 1953,Dominion,local news page</i>)

>  <b>CROWTHER</b> During the early months of this year [1956] much publicity was given in the Christchurch newspapers to the fact that large numbers of young people were to be seen, especially at the weekends, in the central areas of the city. These young people congregated in the streets and milk-bars. Many of them had motor-cycles ... At various times these young people were referred to as `Teddy Boys', `Milk Bar Cowboys' and `Bodgies and Widgies'. <i>Ibid.</i> 2 Milk Bar Cowboys show more individual variation in their dress, and do not display the cleanness and neatness of the Teddy Boy. Leather jackets and trousers tucked into the tops of high wool-lined boots are common. Their interests centre round motor-cycles and cars; many wear one or more of the badges issued by motor-cycle manufacturers. Youths of this type are common in the Christchurch streets at the week-end. It is worth noting that whereas the name of Teddy Boy is accepted proudly, that of Milk Bar Cowboy is not. Boys who appear to conform to this type will repudiate the label. <i>(1956,Street Society in Christchurch,1</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> ... and the brazen milk-bar with the new class, the milk-bar cowboy, the teddy-boys, hanging around the door and putting money in the nickelodeon... . <i>(1957,Owls Do Cry,76,1958 ed.</i>)

>  An alcoholic grave-robbing friend said to me the other day, as we sat and watched the milkbar cowboys come and go - <i>(1973,Islands,3/19</i>) [NOTES: 1967 Baxter The Man on the Horse. (`Notes on the Education of a New Zealand Poet') [in 1973 Islands 3/19]]

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> <i>larrikin</i>: Young people of whom you happen not to approve. Note that all the terms of disapprobation for rumbustious youth left the rest of the world when youth took it over and crawled off to die in New Zealand where the old have such a strong hold. Expressions still used are `milk-bar cowboy', `flagon wagon gang' and even occasionally `Teddy boy.' <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,183</i>)

>  <b>SHERWOOD</b> `But princess,' he drawled, `us sex-starved bushwhackers buried out here in the sticks have to make do with what we can get.' `Queen Street bushwhackers from a commune in Auckland, more likely. I'm sure you don't talk like a milk-bar cowboy at the commune.' <i>(1985,Botanist at Bay,45</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>milkbar cowboy</i> a post-war bikie, though probably not anymore. NZA. cp P: <i>Drug-store cowboy</i> Any lout that hangs about a corner drug-store, talks tough, gives the girls `the glad eye': Canad. coll: adopted ca. 1935 ex U.S. (Leechman) cf <i>Nafty Romeo; </i>cf OEDS <i>cowboy</i> 3b drugstore-cowboy, etc but milkbar-cowboy n.f. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,73</i>)

>  <b>JACKSON</b> ... we usually ended up at the Centreway, ordering sundaes or sodas and watching the milkbar cowboys revving their motorbikes ... while their girlfriends sneered at the reactions of the passers-by. <i>(1988,Rainshadow,113</i>)

>  <b>GEE</b> 'You look like a milkbar cowboy,' she said. <i>(2001,Ellie and the Shadow Man,17</i>)

### milker
 
 <i>n.</i> a cow in milk; a milch cow

>  We had several old milkers and nearly all the others pail fed, only seven wilde ones. <i>(Sep 22, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,59,1950 ed.</i>)

>  ...got two milkers from the wild mob. <i>(1864,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: [1864 Puketoi Dairy] 1947 Beattie E R. 82[This usage is comparable to freezer, killer q.v. The action is felt from the point of view of the agent or subject - the person doing the milking - rather than from that of the instrument - the cow yielding milk.]]

>  Judging which calf will make the best milker. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,35</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dairy cow in milk.

>  .. Mr Steve Algie, a town supply dairyman on the low-lying Taieri Plain, has provided a partly-covered loafing yard to keep his milkers off the pastures during the winter months ... <i>(Sep 28, 1961,NZ Farmer,46</i>)

>  This place is producing enough feed to keep 30 milkers. <i>(Sep 26, 1925,NZ Dairy Exporter,21</i>)

>  Now why does Dave Watt set-stock his milkers rather than graze them rotationally? <i>(Jan 13, 1977,NZ Farmer,4</i>)

>  No supplements were fed until late September, says Mr Picard, when 150 tonnes of carrots and potatoes were introduced into the milkers' diet, as well as a malt product high in protein. <i>(May 10, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,13</i>)

>  This way the cows will at least eat most of the hay while you build up grass for the milkers. <i>(Jun 6, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,16</i>)

### milkfat
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [butterfat](bri-byo.md#butterfat))

>  If.. we can lift production to 300 lb far per cow, we increase gross returns from milkfat by just on $2,000. <i>(Sep 1971,NZ Dairy Exporter,121</i>)

>  This milk [sc. town supply] is paid for by volume, rather than by its milkfat content (as is the case with farms that supply manufacturing diary factories). <i>(1974,NZ Agriculture,95</i>)

### milkie
 
 <i>n.</i> ?=miggles, an opaque playing marble [-->1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 21 MILKIES [=marbles or game: =miggles]]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Marbles had a language of its own. .. Then there were the terms referring to particular kinds of marbles: for example, agates, aggies, aggotties, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cat's eyes, chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, molly-bars, mulley-bars [mullibars: large marbles], peelies, peewees, piss-wees, pretties, Scotties, Scottish alleys, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers, white alleys, and in more recent years ball-bearings, chippies, plastics, and woodies. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

>  <b>KEITH & MAIN</b> Marbles were the currency of childhood and there were an astonishing number of names for them, some referring to the material or colour, others to value or function in the game. There were agates, aggies, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cats-eye, [sic] chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, mollies, peelies, peewees, pisswees, pretties, Scotties, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers and whities. <i>(1984,NZ Yesterdays,288</i>)

### milking
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) milk harvesting in a cowshed.

>  Full-time night milkings from December to end of season. Relief milkings for partner if desired. <i>(Mar 9, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,25</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> the industry or profession of milking; dairying [(cp. with the usual usage = `the process of milking' 1935 it 100 [she] took her quests down to see the `milking', -->[? omit]]

### milking bail
 
 <i>n.</i> bail

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> A stockyard and milking-bail had also been erected. <i>(1890,History of Oamaru & North Otago,54</i>) [NOTES: nf OED]

### milking cow
 
 <i>n.</i> (mining)

>  <b>McARA</b> <i>Caving method</i>: Also known as the `milking cow'; it applied to an area of several acres where the Mine Lake is now. The method started with the collapse of the large arches on the Martha reef about No. 1 shaft, and was later systematically developed by a series of sub-levels on Nos 7, 8 and 9 levels which were used to weaken this section of country and cause it to run so that it could be drawn off through chutes. It operated for a quarter-century and is conservatively estimated to have yielded over a million tons of good run-of-mine grade ore. <i>Ibid.</i> 327 <i>Milking Cow or Caving Block</i>: (See also caving method). A block of country between Nos 1 and 6 shafts and Junction Road, where the Mine Lake is now, which originally contained a network of large reefs and which collapsed after the removal of undergound support pillars and arches and became a caving area. It is estimated that over 1,000,000 tons of ore of good grade was obtained on Nos 7 and 8 levels by caving methods during a period of 25 years. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),314</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a mechanical means of milking an animal

### milking platform
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a productive grazing part of a dairy farm.

>  Farm production average 80,000 kgMS from a 90 hectare milking platform and a 30 hectare run off. <i>(Dec 29, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,26</i>)

>  As well as being a finishing farm for 40,000 lambs, it has room for 900 finishing deer, a pine plantation and a 720-cow milking platform. <i>(Aug 30, 2002,Dominion Post,C5</i>)

>  It [the runoff] is being used for the first time to support the milking platform with winter grazing and baleage. <i>(Oct 1998,NZ Dairy Exporter,58</i>)

>  164,000kg MS off a 160ha milking platform of Rangitikei's finest river silt soil types. <i>(Nov 25, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,38</i>)

>  This year production on the milking platform is on target to reach, autumn permitting, 156,000 kg/MS ... <i>(Apr 12, 2001,NZ Farmer,36</i>)

>  Dairy Farm - Apiti. 63 hectares (161 acres) with an effective milking platform of 60 hectares .. Production achieved off 285 hectares of contiguous flat milking platform. <i>(Feb 16, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,40</i>)

>  As well as being a finishing farm for 40,000 lambs, it has room for 900 finishing deer, a pine plantation and a 720-cow milking platform. <i>(Aug 30, 2002,Dominion,C5</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an area in a cowshed where cows are milked.

>  Once milking is completed, the milking platform is activated once to allow the automatic wash system to steam clean all the surrounding area. The speed of the milking platform can be adjusted to anywhere between seven and twelve minutes to allow for morning and afternoon milking or seasonal milk flows. <i>(Dec 12, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,15</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### milking shed
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  MILKING SHEDS AT MATAPUNA FARM DAIRY, WAIAREKA. <i>(Jul 20, 1878,Otago Witness,3</i>) [NOTES: Illustration caption]

>  The milking sheds were inspected, and the party was conducted over the grounds by the manager of the farm (Mr Drysdale). <i>(May 31, 1907,Taranaki Herald,5</i>)

>  The improvements consist of a substantial Wooden Cottage containing five rooms, Kitchen with Stone chimney, and Oven, paved Dairy with Well of excellent water attached; Barn, Root and Milking Sheds, Outhouse, Piggery, securely fenced Stock and Rick Yards; a well stocked Orchard[,] Flower and Kitchen Garden, 30 acres of Grass-land and 30 acres of Fallow and Cleared Land fenced by 100 chains of newley [sic] erected substantial post and rail Fencing. <i>(Jun 8, 1853,Taranaki Herald,1</i>) [NOTES: Advert]

>  Goodwill of lease of farm, containing 157 acres, being sections 24 and 25, Block 2, Waimate, Rama Road, good house, milking sheds, yards, etc. <i>(May 26, 1896,Hawera & Normanby Star,3</i>) [NOTES: Advert]

>  The Land is mostly cleared, and there is a good House and Milking Shed on the property. <i>(Nov 10, 1860,Otago Witness,10</i>) [NOTES: Advert]

>  <b>McMEEKAN</b> There are three commonly used types of milking-shed in New Zealand - the walk-through, the tandem and the herringbone. <i>(1960,Grass to Milk,148</i>)

>  Farm buildings include a 50-bail turnstile milking shed, a yard that holds 450 cows, two calf sheds and several other sheds, workshops and barns. <i>(Nov 5, 2002,Dominion Post,C3</i>)

>  <b>CURNOW</b> She's all of eighty said the cowman/ Down at the milking-shed. <i>(1940,Island and Time,20</i>) [NOTES: (House and land)]

>  I object to working in a milking-shed! <i>(Jun 1, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,95</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> He walked past another store to the open country where houses alternated with fields of black-and-white cattle with glossy flanks, full udders and swishing tails, congregated by the gates to the milking sheds. <i>(1988,Carpathians, The,194</i>)

 <i>v.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

It grows up to 18 inches high, and is distinguished by the golden green of its spreading flower-head, ... <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,119,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i> giant milkweed [=] sun spurge</i> [s] <i>Ibid.</i> 49<i> milkweed, giant [=] sun spurge</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,29</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Euphorbia <i>spp; ? Brit </i>cow-parsley; <i>(SUN-)SPURGE </i> [1867 HHN 1915 CKB (Anderson, 13 entries)]

>  The most notable exception in the above list is the Milk-weed <i>(Euphorbia Peplus</i>, L.) ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/91</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> SPURGE (<i>Euphorbia peplus</i>), often called milkweed or petty spurge, is a very common garden weed all over both islands. It is an annual growing about 6 to 8 inches high, upright, branched, with green tender leaves... SUN SPURGE (<i>Euphorbia peplus</i>), also called giant spurge, milkweed, and giant milkweed, is not uncommon as far south as Canterbury. It grows up to 18 inches high, and is distinguished by the golden green of its spreading flower-head, ... <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,119,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <b>JEFFREY</b> She stopped raking the leaves that were shrouding the hellebores, and began frantically pulling up handfuls of milk-weed that grew about the trunk of the whau tree. <i>(1964,Mairangi,30</i>)

>  49<i> milkweed</i> [s] <i>Euphorbia peplus</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> MILKWEED (<i>Euphorbia peplus</i>) ... Native to Eurasia. Common throughout New Zealand in gardens, driveways and waste ground,. . . <i>(1980,Weeds of Crops,65</i>)

### milkwood
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Paratrophis microphylla</i> [=earlier <i>Epicarpurus microphyllus</i>] occasionally (and locally) used for MILK-TREE [[1919 Jul 29] Ver. (WB).= Vernacular names used by settlers supplied by W. Best, Otaki (And) <i>Paratrophis microphylla</i> milkwood So written in communication from WB. [a1927] Ver (Stony Bay) = Vernacular names heard by J.C. Andersen at Stony Bay, Banks Peninsula) <i>Paratrophis microphylla</i> milk wood]

>  Milkwood (waiuatua) .. Epicarpurus microphyllus .. A very light white wood; ... [waiu[u]-atua gods' milk] <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,12</i>)

>  In <i>Paratrophis Banksii</i> there is an example of a large-leaf form of the turepo, ot `milkwood' of the bushman. ... Growing in close proximity ... is <i>Sideroxylon costatum</i>, known to the Bay of Plenty Natives as `orewa', but given theless euphonious name of `tawaapu' by writers on the New Zealand flora. <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,7/186</i>) [NOTES: (Sladden)]

>  <i>Paratrophis microphylla</i> milkwood <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,7/</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

 <i>n.</i>
