### milky
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> One feature was noticed regarding the muscle meat of Barracouta and that was when this fish was kept for more than twenty-four hours the meat soon became soft and known to fishery workers as `milky', a condition caused by an infection and rapid development of spores after death. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,312</i>)

### milkydog
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. milky adj](mar-moa.md#milky))

>  [species name] spiny dogfish .. [scientific name] <i>Squalus acanthias</i> .. [other name(s)] spiky dogfish, spurdog, milkydogs <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

### mill
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CARR</b> Old fashioned `Breast' benches (where flitches from the breaking-down saw are cut into commercial sizes) are still used in many mills. <i>(1913,Country Work,40</i>)

### mill feeder
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... he stood on the stack and looked down upon the mill feeder who, knowing the Shiner held the fork would have for him a measure of contempt. <i>(1984,Some Other Country,32</i>) [NOTES: 1936? Lee in 1984 Some Other Country 32]

### mill owner
 
 <i>n.</i> an owner of a threshing mill

>  <b>DUFF</b> Where chaffies came from in those bad old days I don't think I ever knew, but the mill-owners knew, and I suppose the hotel-keepers. <i>(May 17, 1951,Shepherd's Calendar,31,1931 ed.</i>)

### mill road
 
 <i>n.</i> the track from a sawmill to its bush workings, or from the mill to a formed road

>  <b>HOOPER</b> `She's a bit rough along here, but we'll take it easy along the old mill road.' <i>(1981,Goat Paddock,20</i>)

### mill skid
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MALFROY</b> The best conditions ... are an easy falling grade from bush to mill-skids ... n pl <i>(1923,Small Sawmills,7</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> From this contract resulted another to supply logs for delivery at the mill skids. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,35,1963 ed.</i>)

### mill timber
 
 <i>n.</i> millable timber

>  <b>HARPER</b> Open up immense bodies of mill timber. <i>(1934,Windy Island,193</i>)

### mill time
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TURNER</b> Some [saw]mill terms have died out. One is <i>mill time.</i> This was a setting of the clocks an hour early so that the working day, eight to five by mill time, was really seven to four. It was a kind of localized summer time to allow the saw sharpeners to work in daylight after working hours. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,153</i>)

### mill whare
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a portable hut used by threshing-mill or ploughing teams.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Every [harvest] camp has its own cook, with a travelling galley and the men lived in the big iron-wheeled mill whares so common in the days of the old combines. <i>(1973,Big Country of the South Island,241</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> They [scrubcutters]'d have one of the old iron-wheeled mill whares - bunkhouse and galley combined. <i>(1973,Big Country of the South Island,163</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> stinkie: a threshing-mill whare. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,78</i>)

### millable
 
 <i>adj.</i> of a tree, fit to be milled for timber. cf mill timber

>  Most of the timber was down and the few standing trees being taken were non-millable. <i>(1950,Evening Post</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWE;LL</b> Isn't it more likely that they listened to Torlesse saying `the country's easy and roadable, there are 3,500 acres of millable bush, ... <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,142</i>)

### Millard Stand
 
 <i>n.</i> the large grandstand at Athletic Park which replaced the old Western Bank

>  Sitting on the Millard Stand (for the young ones, it was once one of Wellington's most famous structures - now it's probably a Big Fresh car park. <i>(Jun 14, 2001,Evening Post,32</i>)

>  The Millard Stand has rusted worryingly. <i>(Jun 26, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),59</i>)

### milled butter
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Stamps</i>. - The following stamps are prescribed by the regulations:- ... In the case of a packing-house - ... NEW ZEALAND/ Produce. Pure/ MILLED BUTTER./ No. 0 Regd. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,151</i>)

### Millers Point
 
 <i>n.</i> a marijuana cigarette, joint. [ORIGIN: rhyming slang]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> MILLERS POINT (n) marijuana cigarette Rhyming slang from joint. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,251</i>)

### millhand
 
 <i>n.</i> in NZ usually a sawmill worker, occas. a worker in a flax-mill

>  <b>RUST</b> ... hundreds of bushmen, loggers, jackers, bullock-drivers, ... were employed besides stackers, saw doctors, benchmen and mill-hands in the different sawmills [at Maungakaramea]. <i>(1936,Early Whangarei,163</i>) [NOTES: [c.1860s]]

>  <b>MARSH</b> ... `Too Bloody Big' ... was what the mill-hands said repeatedly of the giant we had seen felled. <i>(1981,Black Beech & Honeydew,103</i>)

### milling bush
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It stands to reason that the more timber is used in what is called a `milling bush,' the more economical to the State is the use of that portion of its forests. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,111</i>)

### millionaire's salad
 
 <i>n.</i> the central leaf bud of the nikau; a nikau heart

>  On special occasions, or <i>in extremis</i>, Maori would prepare a meal from the central leaf bud - the succulent heart of the nikau. Early settlers called this dish "millionaire's salad", because removal of the heart cost the palm its life. <i>(Sep 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 65/79</i>)

>  A favourite food of the settlers was the pith of the nikau palm which was edible raw as a green vegetable. It was nicknamed `millionaire's salad' because if the pith was taken the palm died. <i>(Jan 4, 1985,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Succulent and sweet to the taste, nikau heart later became known as `millionaire's salad' because the tree dies when the heart is removed. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,7</i>)

### Milton Hilton
 
 <i>n.</i> (humorous) an Otago Regional Correction facility

>  [Caption] <b>What's on the menu at 'Milton Hilton'</b> Dining at the 'Milton Hilton' is unlikely to be an extravagant affair. The 335 inmates of the Otago Regional Correction facility will receive three 'good, basic' meals a day, seven days a week... <i>(Jul 1, 2006,Otago Daily Times,17</i>)

### mimi
 
 <i>n.</i> urine, the passing of urine [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> Urine Mimme <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/336</i>) [NOTES: (English-NZ Vocabulary)]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> M|imi, ...<i> s.</i> Urine. ... <i>v.n</i>. Making water; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,180</i>)

>  urine - mi|m|mi [mimi] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,111,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: also 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 283 (|= macron precedes) urine, mimmi [mimi]]

>  I found a little mimi of boards and sacks and felt and found only one man in it, a beach worker, I presumed. <i>(May 12, 1915,Great Adventure,47,1988 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Malone)]

>  <b>HULME</b> Have a mimi, grab a couple of half g's, and walk back. <i>Ibid.</i> 467 186 Mimi = piss <i>(1983,Bone People, The,260</i>)

>  <b>Bernard Gadd</b> I just couldn't take any more of that. I hoped she'd think I was going out for a mimi. <i>(1981,Where to Go?,15</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> urinate

>  <b>COLLINS</b> E-ma-mi, <i>To go to make water. </i> <i>(1804,English Colony in NSW,559</i>)

>  Lillybing was allowed to "mimi on the couch", says Namana, but the child "was wetting everywhere". <i>(Jun 16, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> When we reached the place where the rocks were we'd always put our kits down on the sand and mimi on them so the shellfish would be plentiful. <i>(1986,Countless Signs,40</i>) [NOTES: From Patricia Grace (1975) : Waiariki]

### mimi hill
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>mimi hill </i>comfort stop; from Maori `mimi', to urinate; eg `Coming up to mimi hill, chief?' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,73</i>)

### mimi pot
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LATHAM</b> ... and we made our own hats. Believe me, there were some beauts! One Maori girl's remark summed it up in a lot of cases - `Call that plurry thing a potae (hat)? Looks more like a mimi pot!' <i>(1986,WAAC Story, The,3</i>)

### mimiha
 
 <i>n.</i> a fish, possibly a young whale

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>mimiha</i>: a fish, possibly the young whale. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,193</i>)

### mimosa
 
 <i>n.</i> KOWHAI [AND Acacia 1959] (an occasional and mistaken name - occas. NATIVE MIMOSA from the yellow colour of both Austral, wattle and kowhai blooms)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Everywhere are to be seen ... the beautiful mimosa, called Kohai, (<i>Edwardsia microphylla</i>,) covered with clusters of yellow flowers. [but cp op. cit. 152 `some mimosas from N.S.W. [have been introduced]'] <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,144</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> <i>S. tetraptera</i>, Aiton, var. <i>microphylla,</i> Jacq. ... The `Kowhai' of the North Island Maori; the `Kowai' of settlers, - or as the word is variously corrupted and spelt by them, - `Goai' or `Ghoai,' `Goa' or `Ghoa.' According to Hector, the type is known to Otago Maoris as `Houma;' while the term `Kowhai' is applied to var. <i>grandiflora</i> in common with its other varieties. Var. <i>microphylla </i>is also sometimes designated by the colonists the Native `Laburnum' or `Mimosa,' from the similarity of its foliage, in the arboreous condition, to that of these familiar ... European trees. <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,73</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> occas. name in NZ (? now obsolete) for Australian wattle (from the former scientific name)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> occas. name in NZ (? now obsolete) for Australian wattle (from the former scientific name)

>  <b>pol</b> ... mimosa barks ... for dyes <i>(1830,Manners & Customs of the NZers</i>)

>  Many species of the <i>laurel</i> (laurus) exist, especially the<i> philanthus,</i> or sea-side laurel, and a species of the retiring <i>mimosa</i>, or sensitive plant, sheltered by the drooping leaf-branches of the <i>chamerops</i>, or dwarf palm. <i>(1838,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/294</i>)

>  Mrs Felton Mathew exploring the Waitemata as early as 1840 ... was impressed by `the bright yellow balls or tufts of the Acacias or Mimosa growing in the interstices of the rocks, which was very beautiful and of which I got a quantity of seed'. <i>(1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: [?1840 Mathew Journal in] 1974 Bartlett The Emigrants 51]

>  <b>SARAH MATHEW</b> We found a species of Acacia or Mimosa growing in the interstices of the rocks, which was very beautiful, and of which I got a quantity of seed. [check cit] <i>(May 8, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 May 8 Sarah Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 138]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... some mimosas from N.S.W. [have been introduced]. <i>(1848,Hamilton Courier,152</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [a deserted Mission house garden near Matamata] On entering the garden the strawberries had spread themselves; ... the N.S.W. mimosa was of very large size and just in bud; here was a bed of wild asparagus, there one of flowers. <i>(Apr 21, 1849,Journal,6/73</i>)

>  Thatched with mimosa bark which grows about 1 and a half inches thick on the large mimosa tree. <i>(1849,Otago Journal,4/62</i>)

>  Roses in full flower [in Auckland], and mimosa with a delicate golden flower, and various other shrubs ... in full bloom. <i>(Jul 9, 1855,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1855 July 9 J.C. Patteson in 1874 Yonge Life of John Coleridge Patteson 2/207]

>  <b>PASH</b> The wattle or mimosa, which supplies bark to the New Zealand tanneries ... <i>(1883,Report on NZ</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> It was here [in Christchurch] my eyes were gladdened ... by wattles [fn mimosa] in full bloom, the first I had ever seen, ... <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,36</i>) [NOTES: 1889 Sep in 1894 Wilson Land of the Tui 36]

>  <b>TURNER</b> Even the scientific names of these trees [wattles] has a story, as <i>mimosa</i> was the first name given to the trees. The genus <i>Acacia</i>, once included in <i>Mimosa</i>, is now a separate genus. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,61</i>)

### minah
 
 <i>n.</i> the Indian starling, naturalised in New Zealand.

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Minah</i>: The Indian starling, naturalised in New Zealand. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,40</i>)

>  <i>minah, mina, mynah n.</i> a bird allied to the starling, introduced from India. [Indian name] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,8</i>)

### mince
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  And the Napier girls talked about `mincing'. That means when they hop from one ship in port to another. <i>(Jan 19, 1982,NZ Truth,7</i>)

### mince about
 
 <i>v.</i> to hang about

>  <b>BEATON</b> SANDY:... I've been up to the office eh? Runnin' messages for cowface, eh? MA: You'll mince about once too often, lady. <i>Ibid.</i> 110 Mince to hang about where one shouldn't be. <i>(1984,Outside In,25</i>)

### mince pie
 
 <i>n.</i> a savoury meat pie

>  Across the green and white bridge, past the pink rhododendron tree and on to the Opotiki Coillege playground where the boys eat mince pies and the shield flicks sunlight back into their faces. <i>(Aug 22, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

### mind
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [sheepdogs] to watch sheep, confining them in a particular area.

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> They [experienced stockhandlers] were firm believers in having their young dogs do what they called "mind sheep." <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,31</i>)

### mind one's own pigeon
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>to mind one's own pigeon</i>, to mind one's business (an interesting extension of the English slang <i>some one's pigeon</i>, someone's business or private affair) <i>(1941,NZ Slang,53</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Mind one's own pigeon</i>. To mind one's own business: New Zealand: C. 20. B., 1941. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1109</i>)

### minder
 
 <i>n.</i> A policeman (usually senior sergeant) who directs an undercover operation, though not undercover himself; rather like a spy's `control'. Every undercover policemen has a `minder' - with whom he is in contact every day.

### minding dog
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheepdog that will quietly keep a loose mob intact.

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> .. ewes that have gone away with only one lamb will be observed making their bleating way back to recover the other, especially if the mob is quietly and loosely held with your silent 'minding dog.' <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,99</i>)

### mine
 
 <i>n.</i> a crevasse

>  <b>PASCOE</b> Our party took over the lead, and we plunged across the slopes alive with new snow. Tom in front went `mining' down crevasses twice, but luckily the `mines' were little ones and the rope was conveniently taut. <i>(1939,Unclimbed NZ,143</i>)

>  ...he was carefully inched over the side. About half the rope was out when it suddenly slackened. Had he encountered a `mine', or one of the many little lakes to be found on the glacier? <i>(Aug 1950,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,5/19/78</i>)

### miner
 
 <i>n.</i> a paid mine-worker; a permanent worker

>  ... no permanent mining industry of any extent has been established in New Zealand. ... The alluvial diggings ... in Otago and West Coast can hardly with any propriety be termed mines, or those who work them, miners. For the sake of convenience, I shall, in the following essay, observe a distinction between the alluvial digger, by whom I understand one who gathers gold from the surface ;and from later deposits only, and the alluvial miner, ... that is ..., one who works the deeper deposits or deep placer mines as they are called. <i>(1869,AJHR,D-6</i>) [NOTES: (R.H. Eyton Essay on Gold-mining Population)]

>  <b>WASHBOURNE</b> At a later date [in the 1857-9 rush] came miners (who are often confused with diggers) who would only work for wages, a thing the old digger would only do when hard up. <i>(1933,Reminiscences of Early Days,10</i>)

### miner's cage
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>miner's cage, chair ,</i> or <i>cradle</i>. Any of these uses may be applied to the box on an endless rope used for crossing rivers. Trampers on the whole seem to prefer <i>cage</i>. The Australians use<i> flying fox. </i> <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,31</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail]

### miner's chair
 
 <i>n.</i> a device for crossing rivers etc on a wire; a miner's cage [ca 1946, (R Mason) `Heard among trampers. Perhaps an old NZ outdoor usage']

>  <b>HISLOP</b> [caption] Miner's chair Over Cromwell Gorge. [The picture shows a platform on an iron frame hanging by pulleys on a wire across a gorge.] <i>(1943,Pure Gold,49</i>)

>  <b>R. MASON</b> Miner's chair: I first heard this c1946 I think, but that merely indicates my aquaintance with it. It must be years older. <i>(Jun 7, 1952,Letter</i>)

>  <i>miner's cage, chair ,</i> or <i>cradle</i>. Any of these uses may be applied to the box on an endless rope used for crossing rivers. Trampers on the whole seem to prefer <i>cage</i>. The Australians use<i> flying fox. </i> <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,31</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

### miner's cradle
 
 <i>n.</i> = miner's cage

>  <i>Miner's cage, chair, or cradle. </i>Any of these uses may be applied to the box on an endless rope used for crossing rivers. Trampers on the whole seem to prefer<i> cage.</i> The Australians use <i>flying fox. </i> <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,31</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

### miner's cress
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>miner's cress</i> [s] <i>Lepidium densiflorum</i> Schrad. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,49</i>)

### miner's lantern
 
 <i>n.</i> a bottle lantern

>  <b>HARPER</b> My host had given me a miners' lantern. ... It is made of a clear glass bottle, the bottom of which is cut off by means of a worsted thread, soaked in paraffin oil, tied around it, and set alight. In the neck of the bottle, inside, a piece of candle is lit, the bottle carried by its neck upright, and, provided there is no rain, no lantern is more effective, amd, if needs be, it can be stuck upright in the ground. <i>(Nov 10, 1872,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,172,1914 ed.</i>)

### miner's lettuce
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Montia perfoliata</i> (Willd.) Howell

>  There's a semi-shaded patch for salad greens, including unusual types such as miner's lettuce. This early spring green, native to western North America, prevented scurvy among Californian goldminers. <i>(Nov 30, 2004,Dominion Post,E13</i>)

>  miner's lettuce [s] <i>Montia perfoliata</i> (Willd.) Howell <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,69</i>)

>  <i> lettuce, miner's [s] Montia perfoliata </i> (Willd.) Howell <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,44</i>)

### miner's right
 
 <i>n.</i> a licence to dig for gold, etc. [AND 1855] (cf. Prospectors' Right (1971)) [also miners right]

>  It shall be lawful for the Governor to cause documents to be issued, each of which shall be called `The Miner's Right' and shall be granted to any person applying for the same upon payment of the sum of One Pound <i>(1858,NZ Statutes,21 and 22 Vict. No.74 442-446</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Gold Fields Act in N.Z. Statutes, 1858 21 and 22 Vict. No.74 442-446 in 1971 Speeches and Documents 41 III.]

>  No miner can work without a miner's right, which costs, as in Victoria, [pound]1 a year, ... <i>(Oct 4, 1861,NZ Goldfields,39,1976 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Oct 4 Dunedin in NZ Goldfields 1861 (1976) 39]

>  <b>WALKER</b> Two Germans made 40lbs weight of gold out of one hole, but somebody found out they were working without a `Miners' Right' and so turned them out and went in themselves and are making their pile fast. <i>(Jan 15, 1862,Journal and Letters (Walker)</i>) [NOTES: (Central Otago)]

>  <b>WALKER</b> ...working without a `Miners' Right' <i>(Jan 1, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker),1</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,47</i>) [NOTES: 1867 Apr 26 facsimile of one issued by Province of Canterbury in 1914 Pfaff The Diggers' Story 47]

>  <b>PYKE</b> The only qualification [for gold-digging] is the possession of a `Miners' Right'. <i>(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The,41</i>)

>  I do not suppose that ... one fourth have Miners Rights ... <i>(May 20, 1869,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1869 May 20 letter in 1962 Evans Waikaka Saga 17]

>  <b>McLEAN</b> ...Miner's Right... <i>(1869,Papers,31/189</i>)

>  See, here, Mr. Commissioner, jest you make them galoots show their Miners Rights. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,Bk I ch v 22,1889 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WOOD</b> No sin-born cloud its lustre dim, no vice or folly blight,/ But Heaven's blessing ever rest upon our `Miner's Right'. <i>(1875,Waiting for the Mail,26</i>)

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> The Thames goldfield belongs to them [sc Maoris]. Each miner pays a `miner's right', and most of this goes to the Maories ... <i>(1876,Colonial Travel,240</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> .. everyone rushes at the clerk's table, and ... dumps down his note ([pound]1) for the `Miner's Right', which is his license and authority to dig for gold within the limits of the field. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/289</i>)

>  <b>SWANTON</b> ... befire [a man] can exercise the exclusive right to dig in any piece of land ... and take legal possession of the gold ... he must procure a `miner's right'. <i>(1892,Notes on NZ,148</i>)

>  <i>Paratrophis microphylla</i> milk-tree <i>(1872,Catalogue of the Colonial and Vienna Exhibition</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Miner's Right, n</i>. the licence to dig for gold. See quotation. [1890. Rolf Boldrewood] <i>(1898,Austral English,294</i>)

>  <i>miner's right n.</i> licence to dig for gold. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),620</i>)

>  <i>miner's right n.</i> official licence to prospect for gold or other minerals. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

>  The Miner's Right, used on the goldfields from the earliest days until replaced by the Prospectors' Right in 1971. The Nelson goldfields were in the West Coast Mining District. <i>(1976,Historic Gold Trails of Nelson & Marlborough,12</i>)

>  <i>miner's right NZ</i> & <i>Austral</i>. licence to dig for gold etc. on public or private land. [F (MINE 2)] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,477</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 718; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 311] 1984 Still extant [quote Act]]

### mineral belt
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ..where the Miner River marks a boundary between the mineral belt and the Maitai Group sediments... <i>(1990,Tramping in South Island Forest Parks,18</i>)

>  ... but a highly distinctive feature is introduced by the occurrence of the `Mineral Belt' - a zone of boulder-strewn country, often dun-coloured owing to the rusty weathering of the rocks, almost totally devoid of trees, and covered only by a sparse growth of stunted shrubs and smaller plants. <i>(1911,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.12 (NS) 2</i>) [NOTES: Bell, de Courcy, and Marshall `The Geology of Dun Mountain Subdivision, Nelson]

### mineral block
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a proprietary lick containing specific minerals. (See [lick](lay-liv.md#lick); [salt lick](sa-sho.md#salt-lick))

>  Mineral blocks do much to promote a fully rounded diet, supplementing natural pastures and winter crops .. The use of mineral blocks will eliminate that nagging feeling that mineral, known to be present, may be locked in the soil .. Stock readily accustoms itself to using mineral blocks. <i>(Sep 1973,NZ Journal of Agriculture,110</i>)

### mineral head
 
 <i>n.</i> see cit. [mining]

>  In the upper levels of the mines the ore - shoots are said to have been intimately associated with what are termed `mineral heads' or `bars'. These are apparently layers of sedimentary rock, which originally proved more permeable to the circulating ground-waters than the general mass of rock, and supplied aqueous precipitants of gold and silver to the vein channels. <i>Ibid.</i> 124 A much more extensive shoot of ore was mined in former years from the intersection of a rather large mineralised stratum (`mineral head') with one of the veins <i>(1907,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No. 4 (NS.):122</i>) [NOTES: Fraser & Adams, Coromandel]

### mineral-belt scrub
 
 <i>n.</i> (Geology)

>  It passed through varied vegetation, including an area of mineral-belt scrub, before descending to Totara Saddle. <i>(2003,Tramping in South Island Forest Parks,43</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CHURCHMAN & HURST</b> The 6.35 am from Greymouth was the "Miners' Train", while the return working, the 7.35 am from Rewanui was the "Fannie Train", as the women from Dunollie and Rewanui used it to go to work in Greymouth. <i>(1990,Railways of NZ,196</i>)

### mingi
 
 <i>n.</i> a scrub-like native tree of the Cyathodes family; mingimingi [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  There are [sic] a number of native trees in blossom at the same time as pukapuka, including puriri, tea-tree, 'bush lawyer', clematis, cabbage-tree, mingi and ohoho. <i>(Jul 1911,NZ Farmer,765</i>)

>  It was in native scrub and boxthorn. The native was mainly a local weed called mingi, which is similar to tawhinu. <i>(Jul 14, 1983,NZ Farmer,9</i>)

>  589 Mingi (mikimiki) slab. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,28</i>)

>  Cyathodes acerosa Mingi <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/123</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

 <i>n.</i> a fish

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> The Europeans in Westland call the adult [whitebait] `cents' or `scents' and the Maori of Taranaki called them `mingies' (probably a corruption on `minnows') <i>(1942,Maori Place Names,442</i>)

### mingi-mingi
 
 <i>n.</i> also called mickymick, any of several small-leaved shrubs. [Maori]

### mingiming
 
 <i>n.</i> [noun & attr.]

### mingimingi
 
 <i>n.</i> Any of several native shrubs with small and often sharp-tipped leaves belonging to the families Epacridaceae and Rubiaceae. See also miki-miki. [ORIGIN: Maori, p.c. John Dawson 28.10.88 C. juniperina--> now = C. acerosa-->: earlier <i>C. acerosa</i> (=prickly) the SI species: now generally popularly divided into PRICKLY MINGIMINGI and SOFT MINGIMINGI: generally found e.g. on beech-forest floors: mingimingi with twisted grain probably]

>  The best is mingimingi, a vine that settlers amd gold-diggers would pull from thet trees to stuff their mattresses with or make hinaki for catching eels. <i>(Dec 18, 2005,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),40</i>)

>  6 Hand specimen, turuhuhu (mingimingi). [turuhuhu nf Williams] <i>Ibid.</i>16 9 Head piece, mingimingi. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,163</i>)

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> At the edge of the bush, slim trunks of kamahi formed a background for prickly bushes of mingi-mingi, with their slightly flattened white drupes reminiscent of old-fashioned linen buttons, stitched in the middle. <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,73</i>)

>  The mingi-mingi in her berried dress. <i>(1936,Manuka Blossoms,9</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Coprosma spp., esp C. propinqua [noun & attr.]

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> [<i>Coprosma parviflora</i>, Hook f.] The `Mingi-mingi' [fn] of the Otago settler. The commonest form of the plant on exposed uplands is a smallish, stout, erect shrub ... [fn] A term also applied, according to Colenso, to <i>Leucopogon fasciculatus</i>, A.Rich. [72 applies mikimik to Coprosma] <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,72</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>C[oprosma] Cunninghamii, Hook.f. ... Mingimingi. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,249</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Coprosma propinqua (The [Similar] Coprosma).</i> A thickly-branched shrub, 6 ft.- 20 ft. in height ... Maori name <i>Mingimingi. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,398,1940 p.,425 ed.</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Coprosma ... propinqua </i>... Common Coprosma. MINGI-MINGI. ... <i>C. Cunninghamii </i>... MINGI-MINGI (twisted in grain). <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,60</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Coprosma propinqua</i> MINGIMINGI ...Bushy dark shrub about 1-5 m tall. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,60</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> The whole [Waikaka] valley and the ... ridges were covered [in 1876] with luxurious white tussocks (Poa caespitosa) diversified with a few snow grass tussocks (Danthonia raoulii), trees and shrubs comprising Maukoko, or native broom (Carmichaelia australia[?lis]), Koromiko (Veronica salicifo), daisy tree (Aleria rirgata [?virgata]), cottonwood (Cassinia fulvida), ... native thorns or matagouri and mingimingi (Coprosma propinqua). [check] <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,239</i>)

>  <i>Coprosma Cunninghamii</i> Hook. f. .. Mingimingi, yellow-wood. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1.143</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Cyathodes (or Leucopogon) spp. [[has prickles]]

>  Mingimingi. Leucopogon fasciculatus, A. Rich. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/46</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  These include two that are indiscriminately termed mingimingi (<i>Cyathodes acerosa</i> and <i>Leucopogon fasciculatus)</i> ... <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/200</i>)

>  <i>mingimingi</i>, or<i> mingi,</i> a shrub (Cyathodes acerosa). <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/154</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Mingimingi, <i>Col[enso]. Leucopogon fascisculatus. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> [<i>Coprosma parviflora</i>, Hook f.] The `Mingi-mingi' [fn] of the Otago settler. The commonest form of the plant on exposed uplands is a smallish, stout, erect shrub ... [fn] A term also applied, according to Colenso, to <i>Leucopogon fasciculatus</i>, A.Rich. [72 applies mikimik to Coprosma] <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,72</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> ... the large bushes of the mingimingi (<i>Styphelia acerosa</i>), some with abundance of white and others with pink drupes. <i>(1910,NZ Plants,51</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>C[yathodes] acerosa, R.Br.</i> ... An erect or rarely decumbent branching shrub 4-15 ft. high ... Mingimingi. <i>Ibid.</i> 414 <i>L[eucopogon] fasciculatus, A.Rich</i>. ... A branching shrub or small tree 5-15 ft. high ... [415] Mingi <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,411</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Cyathodes acerosa (The Prickly Cyathodes)</i>. A shrub or small tree, with blackish branches ... Maori name, <i>Mingi-mingi.</i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,329,1940 p. 349 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Cyathodes juniperina </i>(J.R. & G. Forst.) Druce Maori names: <i>Taumingi, tumingi, mingimingi ... ### mining tenure
 
 <i>n.</i> one of various forms of land holding in <i>mining districts</i> (a term not specifically recorded in DNZE) under successive Mining Acts - <i>residence site licence, business site licence</i> and <i>special licence</i> - granted by a <i>Warden's Court</i>(DNZE). [Jim Cameron writes: Largely historical. These tenures have been brought under the land transfer system by the Mining Tenures Registration Act 1962. (I do not know if they still exist as such).]

### Minister of Wine and Cheese
 
 <i>n.</i> (humorous) Jonathan Hunt

>  His nickname, Minister of Wine and Cheese, goes way back to when wine was hardly a mainstream Kiwi pursuit - but already the boy from Palmerston north was onto it. <i>(Mar 25, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

### Ministerialist
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The House would not act. more inconsistently.. than it did in carrying Mr Macandrew's amendment after the vigorous protest of the Ministerialists that it was too late in the session to bring forward a question involving the fate of the Government. <i>(Aug 31, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

### Ministry
 
 <i>n.</i> In New Zealand until quite recently (Ministry of Works, 19 ), only the former of the two main `governmental' senses of Ministry was commonly used: OED 5 from 1710, the body of ministers forming the government' eg. the Seddon ministry, etc. As the name of a Stare department (OED 6, from 1877) it was introduced when the name of the `Public Works Department (see P.W.D.) was for various reasons changed to Ministry of Works. Since then the reorganisation and extension of responsibilities of the Transport Department has given a new Ministry of Transport. -->`Ministry' is felt to be a more inclusive (perhaps grander) name than `department' in the NZ State Services system. (Based partly on a note by R. Mason) -->cf EWD 5.

### Ministry of Jerks
 
 <i>n.</i> a play on Ministry of Works

### Ministry of Women's Affairs
 
 <i>n.</i>

Ann has also assured us all that the MWA is not the precursor of "some grey androgynous society". <i>(May 31, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

### Ministry of Works
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical)

>  The Ministry of Works was composed of engineers and experts, who sat frequently to discuss the various propects [sic]. <i>(Jun 30, 1944,Hawera Star,3</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Driving along the Wairoa-Napier road, I was brought to a halt by the Ministry of Works... I myself had worked for the Public Works (to use its old name) on the Rotorua-Tirau road. <i>(1971,Our Open Country,133</i>)

>  <i>The Man from the Ministry</i>, by W Frank Ponder.., covers some of the career of the designer and preserver of Scott Base - and closes with a lament for the Ministry of Works. <i>(Oct 26, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),49</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> I just know where the fish are to be found [in rivers]. It's getting them out in an orthodox manner that bothers me.' `Ministry of Works minnows,' chuckles Kerewin, but he affects shock. <i>(1983,Bone People, The,86</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> Years of living in a land man has made ugly will show the advantages of a country where even the Ministry of Works and Comalco have only managed to scratch the surface. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,177</i>)

>  <b>MAHY</b> Angela's home consisted of two Ministry-of-Works cottages, placed side by side. <i>(1985,Catalogue of the Universe,39</i>)

>  <b>NOONAN</b> Under date 26 May 1948, Cabinet approved of the Public Works Department being merged in the Ministry of Works for all administrative purposes under the Commissioner of Works as permanent head. <i>(1975,By Design,196</i>)

>  The Ministry of Works, therefore, is primarily concerned with the planning of works, the allocation of priorities, the supply of materials and labour, and the inauguration of projects in which the Government has a major interest. <i>(1946,AJHR,D-1/2</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Minnow, n</i>. name sometimes given to a very small fish of New Zealand, <i>Galaxias attenuatus</i>, Jenyns, family <i>Galaxidae</i>; called also<i> whitebait</i> (q.v.). The Maori name is <i>Inanga</i> (q.v.). <i>(1989,Austral English,294</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The fish known as the minnow (<i>Galaxias attenuatus</i>), is, as its name implies, a small fish. <i>(1922,Naturalisation of Animals and Plants,187</i>)

>  <i>Galaxias attenuatus</i>(Jenyns). Minnow, whitebait. <i>Hiwi, inanga</i>. ...The only marine record is that of a number of specimens 3 1/4 ins. ... caught in a garfish bunt in the Otago Harbour in February, 1931. <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/402</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> MINNOW or WHITEBAIT (INANGA) <i>Austrocobitis attenuatus</i> (Jenyns)... For a long time it has been known to the Maori and to Europeans that our New Zealand Whitebait is the young of a native freshwater fish commonly known as Minnow, or Inanga or Hiwi to the Maori, and erroneously called a Trout. ... The early settlers called it Maori Trout, Native Trout, Rock Trout and Minnow. But it is not a Trout. Nor is it a Minnow, through it resembles the English freshwater Minnow. While the name Trout is not correct, it is and will always be known as such by farmers and naturalists. In the Bay of Plenty, Whitebait is known to the Maori as Porohoe. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,118</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> INANGA (WHITEBAIT) ... <i>Other names: Galaxias attenuatus</i>; minnow; jolly tail (Australia). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,195</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>I[i]nanga, inanga</i>, n. <i>1. Whitebait</i>, the adult of the minnow, <i>Galaxias attenuatus</i>(also the fry), and the fry of the smelt, <i>Retropinna retropinna.</i> ... Maoris distinguish between <i>inanga papa</i>, a variety of smelt, <i>Retropinna</i>, and <i>inanga tu[u]tuna,</i> a spotted variety of minnow, <i>Galaxias. </i> <i>(1971,Dictionary of the Maori Language,77</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> MINNOW Another name for the `Inanga'. Given by early settlers because of its resemblance to the English minnow, - of which it is no relative. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,288</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Galaxias attenuatus</i> [whitebait], or the New Zealand Minnow

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> THE NEW ZEALAND MINNOW. NOTES ON THE LIFE-HISTORY OF THE NEW ZEALAND MINNOW (THE ADULT WHITEBAIT), <i>GALAXIAS ATTENUATUS. </i> <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,7/117</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> NEW ZEALAND MINNOW (<i>Galaxias attenuatus</i>). <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/291</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> NEW ZEALAND MINNOW <i>Galaxias attenuatus</i> (Jenyns) ... This is the inanga of the Maori; but the term is not used in the same sense by all tribes. Some call whitebait inanga and use the term in a general sense, others apply it to the adult and others strictly to the young of the minnow which form the main constituent of whitebait, Galaxias attenuatus. Hiwi is a name used in the Bay of Plenty for the parent fish, and numerous other names have been applied to this important fish by Maoris in all parts of the country. <i>(1940,Fishes NZ,25</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> [T]he complete life history of <i>Galaxias attenuatus</i> [whitebait], or the New Zealand Minnow, as it is often termed, was made known. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,63</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> As adjective, often used as part of the name of a species

>  As adjective, often used as part of the name of a species found in the back country, mostly smaller than their down-country relatives, e.g. <i>m</i>. pine, totara, minnow, duck (blue duck), daisy, lily (Mount Cook lily). (C.) [<i>M.</i> musk, used for flavouring tobacco, is Celmisia novae-zelandiae A.W.] <i>(Nov 4, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

### Minties moment
 
 <i>n.</i> an embarrassing (often comical) mistake in a sporting situation etc. (as shown in Minties television ads) (See [alluding to the slogan (for Minties the mint-flavoured sweets)](../A#alluding-to-the-slogan-(for-minties-the-mint-flavoured-sweets)),['It's moments like these you need Minties'](../'#'it's-moments-like-these-you-need-minties'))

>  [Hdg] Headgear scores in Minties moment... The Hawke's Bay referee's "Minties" moment came during Nelson Bay's 8-7 win against Manawatu.. on Saturday. <i>(Oct 7, 2002,Dominion Post,D2</i>)

>  Wrote McLean: "It's moments like these Minties don't help very much". <i>(Jul 18, 2004,Sunday Star Times,B11</i>)

>  ...there were plenty of other Minties moments, including a clearing kick that went virtually backward into his own dead hand. <i>(Jul 19, 2004,Dominion Post,D2</i>)

>  I was wondering if the term "Minties moment" has found its way into the NZDC database. I was watching the Taranaki-Wellington NPC game on Sky last weekend and one of the commentators used it to describe the embarrassing situation one of the players found himself in close to his own goal line after the ball bounced awkwardly for him... <i>(Sep 26, 2003,Personal Communication</i>)

### miro
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Podocarpus ferrugineus</i> black pine (Otago) and its wood; a tall native forest tree with hard timber, aromatic foliage and red fruit. [Maori] [<i>Prumnopitys ferruginea</i>, previously <i>Podocarpus ferrugineus</i> aka bastard black pine]

>  He sank gratefully to the ground down the bole of a large miro. <i>(May 4, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

>  <b>Harper</b> For external use, we have the gum of the miro pine (Podocarpus ferruginea), the finest healing ointment for an open wound that I have ever used, and a sure cure for warts. <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,34</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Miro, n.</i> ... (2) Maori name for a New Zealand tree, <i>Podocarpus ferruginea</i>, Don., N.O.<i> Conifera</i>; the Black-pine of Otago. <i>(1898,Austral English,295</i>)

>  <b>LASLETT</b> <i>(1875,Timber and Trees,308</i>)

>  The timbers of commercial value are totara, rimu, miro, matai, rata, and kamahi, in mixed bushes; but <i>Fagus fusca</i> and other beeches predominate on the high lands between the lakes and the fiords on the west coast. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,30</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>P[odocarpus] ferrugineus, D.Don.</i> ... bark greyish-brown or almost black ... <i>Miro; Toromiro; Black-pine. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,650</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Podocarpus ferrugineus (The Rusty Podocarpus or Miro). </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,68,1940 p. 66 ed.</i>)

>  The chief indigenous trees of commercial value are: Kauri (<i>Agathis Australis</i>),.. Miro (<i>Podocarpus ferrugineus</i>), .. northern and southern rata (<i>Metrosideros robusta</i> and <i>Metrosideros lucida</i>). <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,578</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Rimu is the red pine, and for the miro I never heard an English name. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,13</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> In the northern and eastern portions of the Island, the predominant trees are Rimu ... accompanied by Miro (<i>Podocarpus Ferrugineus</i>), thin-barked Totara (<i>Podocarpus Hallii</i>) and southern Rata (<i>Metrosideros Lucida</i>). <i>(1940,Rakiura,xviii</i>)

>  <i>miro:</i> large tree with berries. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  The Maori name for a valuable New Zealand timber tree, a pine <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),260</i>)

>  <i>miro ... n.</i> N.Z. forest tree. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

>  <b>479</b> <i>miro 1 n.</i> NZ timber tree. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>LASLETT</b> The miro-tree (Podocarpus ferruginea) ... is found in many of the forests in New Zealand. <i>(1875,Timber and Trees,308</i>)

>  408 Miro (bastard black pine) slab. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,24</i>)

>  Miro. Podocarpus ferruginea, Don. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/48</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  In an economic point of view, the chief trees of the Middle Island, are the Red and White Pine, respectively called by the Maoris: the former Mai or Matai <i>(Podicarpus spicata</i>),[fn] the latter the Kahikatea (Podocarpus dacrydioides).<i> [ed. fn] Black Rue of Otago, Hook., f.; Miro, Black Rue or Black Pine in Otago, Hector; Miro, often confounded with Black Pine, Balfour; Matai, Colenso, - Mai or Matai (Taylor). </i> <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/12</i>) [NOTES: (Monro)]

>  Podocarpus ferruginea Mairo <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/122</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  MIRO, also called BLACK PINE in Otago.- (<i>Podocarpus ferrugineus</i>.) <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,11</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> The forest [near Rotorua] was mostly tawai (Leiospermum racemosum), miro, and hinau; the Dracophyllum, which often grows to the size of a small tree, and the reddish leaves of which render it a very remarkable and beautiful shrub, was ... to be met with. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/399</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>miro</i>: tree 50 ft. to 80 ft. high; has reddish-purple berries 3/4 in. long; favourite food of native pigeon. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,193</i>)

>  Successional planting of such species as totara and miro is so labour intensive. <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,1</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> M|iro,<i>s</i>. A fruit-tree so called; also the fruit of the <i>M|iro. </i> <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,181</i>)

>  <b>McCRAE</b> Next day went to Wy Whero a fine tract of cultivated land - Peaches - Taroo Flax, Meero - return to house, - <i>(1820,Journal,26,1928 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> Miro (<i>Podocarpus ferruginea</i>) - This plant grows to the height of from forty to sixty feet, ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),45</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the many valuable trees ... the following are best known. The kouri, totarra [a1-acute], puriri, rata [a's-acute], pohutokawa, rohito, kahikatea [a's, e-acute], kaikatoa [a2-acute], towai, rimu, maidi, torairi, tanikaha [a2,a3-acute], kowai, rewarewa, maioi, tipou, touwa, kawaka [a's-acute], miro, koikoi, hinou, tiaki, akki pirou, angi angi, patu [a-acute], koihiu, &c., and various akkas' [a2-acute], or liands. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/286</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The Miro is a beautiful tree of a small light green leaf, bears a bright red berry, and this also a favorite food of the wild pigeon; ... <i>(1844,Journal,105,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> We passed a large number of Matai trees and the ground was literally covered with their berries ... and several miro trees whose beautiful red berries have a rich flavor not unlike that of mace <i>(Apr 1847,Journal,5/36</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> 8. Miro, (<i>Podocarpus ferruginea</i>.) This tree grows to a considerable size in the central and southern parts of New Zealand. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,142</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> The `miro' Podocarpus ferruginea is also excellent timber, but too scarce for general use. <i>(Feb 14, 1853,Traveller under Concern,78,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Podocarpus <i>ferruginea</i>, Don. ... Nat. name, `Miro', <i>Cunn. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 232</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> Amongst the trees ... are the matai (Podocarpus spicata), ... miro (Podocarpus ferruginea), and others. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,148</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Miro, <i>Col[enso]. Podocarpus ferruginea. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Miro, `Black Pine' (<i>Podocarpus ferruginea</i>), is, like the matai, a large-sized tree. <i>(1882,Bright Fine Gold,2/195</i>)

>  [Native name] Miro .. [Settlers' name] Miro .. [Family] Coniferae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  <b>KNOX</b> Then there is the Matai, which is much like the English yew, and is used for making furniture; the Miro, which has a beautiful red fruit on which pigeons grow fat; and the Kawaka, which has a remarkable leaf and a durable wood. <i>(1888,Boy Travellers,201</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> The Miro is a valuable tree, common in all parts of the colony .... It is usually distinguished by its ordinary native name <i>(1889,Forest Flora,163</i>)

>  Miro (bastard black pine) .. Podocarpus ferruginea .. Wood hard, elastic, strong, and grain prettily figured; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,12</i>)

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> Next in abundance is the Miro, [fn Podocarpus ferrugineus] a tree exactly like the English yew, but bearing sweet berries about the size of horse-beans, with an internal seed. <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,36 and fn,1952 ed.</i>)

>  The most valuable for sawn timber are Black Pine, or Miro (Podocarpus ferruginea), having red, hard, durable wood. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/31</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]



 
 <i>n.</i> the white-breasted North Island tit.

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> This bird lives on the fleshy ... fruits of the hinau (Etacocarpus [sic =?Elaeo-] hinau), of the tawai (Leiospermum racemosum), of the miro (Podocarpus ferruginea), and of the mai (Dacrydium mai). <i>Ibid.</i> 1/151 The boldest [bird] was a flycatcher [fn 1 Miro longpipes, Less.] of an ashy colour, which hopped continually over the rotten trees searching for insects. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/55</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Miro</i> (1) Maori name for a <i>Robin</i> (q.v.). and adopted as the scientific name of a genus of New Zealand Robins. The word is shortened form of <i>Miro-miro. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,295</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,403</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> =Black Robin <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,503</i>)

>  <i>(1875,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/399,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Miro Traversi </i>... Found now only on Little Mangere Island ... Black Wood Robin. TOU-TOU-WAI, MIRO. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,71</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.] -]

>  <i>miro 2 n. NZ</i> (also <i>miromiro</i>) white-breasted or pied tit. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,479</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ELDER</b> They then offered me their little parcels of millo (<i>miro</i>) or thread which they had made with their own hands, ... <i>(Jul 25, 1934,Marsden's Lieutenants,68</i>) [NOTES: 1814 July 25 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 68]

### miro/miro berry
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  This piripiri had also been used to rub my main course chicken breast, succulently roasted, garnished with avocado salsa, and filled with a "miro" farce - more poetic licence, since Kai in the Bay doesn't yet have a supply of wild miro berries and seeks to replicate the citric flavour with orange and cranberry puree. <i>(Sep 18, 2003,Dominion Post,D5</i>)

>  <b>HAWES</b> Eats miro berries. Loves them. Trouble is they're made of turpentine. Pigeons is dying out from sclerosis of the liver. <i>(2000,Dream of Nikau Jam, The,21</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The <i>korai, koutu utu, miro</i>, and <i>putuhutu</i>, are wild forest fruits, prized only by the elder natives. The <i>tu pakihi</i> (Coriaria sarmentosa), or elder-berry, is a very pleasing fruit. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/288</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> <i>(1905,Where the People Sing</i>)

### miromiro
 
 <i>n.</i> a bird <i>Petroica toitoi</i>, white-breasted North Island tit ?a NI name cf also miro; katoitoi; (North Island tit; tomtit; wren )

>  27. <i>Petroeca toi-toi</i>, Less. Pied Tit, Miro-miro. <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,286</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> He visited the wood-fairies ... in the form of a tiny bird, the <i>miromiro</i>, ... [fn] The New Zealand Tom-tit (Myiomoira toitoi). <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,436</i>)

>  <i>miromiro</i>, a bird (Petroeca toitoi), North Island tomtit; white-breasted tit it differs from the South Island form (P. macrocephala) yellow-breasted tit in the colouration of the breast. Whilst miromiro was the name of the North Island bird, both of them bore the name ngirungiru, so that has been adopted for the South Island form, though the South Island Maori would probably give the `ng' sound scurvy treatment and call the bird kirukiru, as he calls ngaio kaio. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/154</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>miro-miro</i>: white breasted tit. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>HINTZ</b> The little miromiro, for instance, the pied tit that abounds in the upper river valley, his sooty plumage relieved by the dazzling white of his breast. <i>(1964,Trout at Taupo,124</i>)

>  <i>~ n.</i> New Zealand robin: valuable forest tree [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),620</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> TOMTIT <i>Petroica macrocephala Other names</i>: Miromiro (North Island), Ngirungiru (South Island), Wheedler, Butcher-bird (Taranaki). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,200</i>)

>  <b>MEAD</b> In writing up his diary [for 9 December 1846] he recorded that on the trip through the forest one of his boys whistled to a little bird, the miromiro, which flew down to his cupped hands. <i>(1966,Richard Taylor,106</i>)

>  <i>miromiro ... n</i>. N.Z. robin. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> She lived on the edge of a volcano, but, like the <i>miromiro</i>, learned how to flutter above eruptions, shielding her children ... <i>(1969,Awatea,111,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <i>257.a. Petroica macrocephala toitoi</i> (Lesson, 1828) <i>Pied Tit (Miro-miro) </i>... North Island ... <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,68,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> [cf <i>Ngirungiru</i> ? here = Pirangirangi,<i> Petroica toitoi</i>, tit] 25.<i> Miro longpipes. Less.</i> ... [191] Gha toitoi of the natives of Dusky Bay. <i>Forst.</i> Miro miro of the natives. <i>Garnot.</i> [possibly<i> Miro australis</i>, the robin (= katoitoi), rather than <i>miromiro</i>, NI Tit <i>Petroica</i>] 26. <i>Miro Forsterorum</i>. - Turdus minutus. <i>Forst</i>. ... Deep-shining black, with breast and abdomen pale yellow; ... Mirro mirro of the natives of Queen Charlotte's Sound. <i>Forst</i>. Pirangirangi of the natives of Queen Charlotte's Sound. <i>Dieffenbach.</i> .. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/191</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/191]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> As we came through the woods one of my boys whistled to a little bird the miromiro or wren the least bird of this country and it actually flew into his clasped hands ... it is less then the English wren but much resembles it ... <i>(Dec 6, 1846,Journal,4/148</i>)

>  Petroica toi-toi. Miro-miro. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> MYIOMOIRA TOITOI. (PIED TIT \{NORTH ISLAND TOMTIT\}.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Miromiro, Komiromiro, \{Pimiromiro,\} Ngirungiru\{, Pingirungiru, and Pipitori\}. ... [40] Its note in the early morning is like the Maori syllables <i>ngi-i-ru, ngiru-ngiru</i>, from which it derives its name, the first syllable being rather prolonged. <i>Ibid.</i> 126 [1/42] MYIOMOIRA MACROCEPHALA. (YELLOW-BREASTED TIT \{SOUTH-ISLAND TOMTIT\}.) <i>Native names</i>. Miromiro, Komiromiro, \{Pimiromiro,\} Ngirungiru\{, Pingirungiru, and Pipitori\}. ... <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,124,{1888 2nd edn 1/39 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 30. PETROICA TOI-TOI. Less. Pied-tit. Miro-miro. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,12</i>)

>  I have got a great many birds since you have been away, which I shot with your gun or a catapult (excuse spelling), and which I stuff and hang up by the beak. ... One of them is a New Zealand bittern ... I have also the mako-mako, silver-eye, riro-riro, miro-miro, fanny etc. ... <i>(Aug 16, 1875,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/399,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (?Bill Atkinson, Nelson, 15 years)]

### miroroi
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [(cf mairoero) ?=moriori](../(#(cf-mairoero)-?=moriori))

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> This is the locality [Mataura] for wonderful stories of wonderful beings; the Maories declare that there are hairy giants, who inhabit a mountain near these valleys; they call them Miroroi, and have a great dread of them; we were very anxious to procure a specimen, and quite willing to hazard the capture, thinking it would be a fine spec., other resources failing, to return to England with. <i>(May 13, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1844 May 13 Tuckett Diary in 1898 Hocken Contributions 220]

### mirror dory
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ZENOPSIS NEBULOSUS. <i>Mirror Dory</i>. During April the trawler `Nora Niven' secured small numbers of mirror dory in about 40 fathoms in Cook Strait. ... They were sold in Wellington as `silver dory.' <i>(May 1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,V/95</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps and Hodgkinson Further Notes on Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Zenopsis nebulosas</i> (Schegel). MIRROR-DORY. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,25,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Mirror Dory <i>Zenopsis nebulosas</i> (Temminck and Schlegel) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,404</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 404]

>  <b>PARROTT</b> The Mirror Dory is found, as far as is known in northern waters, and, as far as I know, has not been recorded off the coast of the South Island. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,52</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> MIRROR DORY see <i>John Dory </i> <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,162</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> MIRROR DORY <i>Zenopsis nebulosas</i> ... Mirror dories are uniformly silver in colour, so bright as to be almost mirror-like. ... They are commoner around northern New Zealand ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,181,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] dory, mirror 1035-44 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### mirror-belly
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> MIRROR-BELLY Opisthoproctus grimaldi ... The mirror-belly is one of the more unusual of the mid-water fishes even tough it only reaches a maximum length of 6cm ... The [ventral] sole itself is backed by a reflective silvery lining. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,108,1984 ed.</i>)

### miru-miru
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Acanthisitta chloris. Miru-miru. At Harataonga. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

### miserable
 
 <i>adj.</i> mean, stingy

### miserable as a bandicoot
 
 <i>phr.</i> in a very miserable or pitiful physical state

>  <b>CHAMIER</b> [An Irish station hand says] ... as miserable as a bandicoot. <i>(1891,Philosopher Dick,246</i>)

>  <b>COTTLE</b> ... miserable as a bandicoot. <i>(1891,Frank Melton's Luck,111</i>)

>  What have you been doing to Norman? He's as miserable as a bandicoot. I'll have to go in and sweeten him up as soon as I finish this. <i>(1984,Some Other Country,91</i>) [NOTES: 1950 Gaskell in 1984 Some Other Country 91]

### miserable as a wet hen
 
 <i>phr.</i> [ca1940 H.S. Gajadhar, Wellington] (See [miserable](mar-moa.md#miserable))

>  I found that even when the clubhouse and links were well peopled I was as lonely and miserable as a wet hen. <i>(Oct 7, 1922,NZ Truth,8</i>)

### mish
 
 <i>n.</i> mission

>  It was " a mish" (= mission = difficult task) but he's very pleased with the results. <i>(Nov 8, 2000,Capital Times,9</i>)

### mismother
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to unsuccessfully re-unite ewe and lamb after a docking muster etc.

>  <b>OVERTON</b> ...great care should be taken not to mismother the lambs when approaching the yards. <i>(1949,Fifty Years Sheep Farming,97</i>)

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> Ewes with lambs will not 'follow on' the same as dry sheep will. Instinctively they know that once in a mob they will mis-mother. Under no circumstances should ewes and lambs be bunched to the extent that they mis-mother. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,85</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The manager of an adjoining station once told me that he saw about 5,000 Merino ewes and lambs yarded on one occasion by an inexperienced man, and about 200 were smothered before the men started tailing. Then they let the ewes and lambs out on to a big block, <i>yard by yard as they finished,</i> the result being that fully 500 lambs were mis-mothered, some of the ewes being miles away before the mob was through. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,114,1954 ed.</i>)

### mismothered
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of mother and offspring pairs] unsuccessful in re-pairing following a muster.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> As the sheep get bunched together the lambs begin to get mismothered ... <i>(1951,NZ High Country,25</i>)

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> As they are allowed to string through the gate and away, a proportion of mis-mothered lambs .. will appear to have been abandoned. [mis-mothered] <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,98</i>)

### mismothering
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the unsuccessful re-pairing of ewes and lambs following a muster etc.

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> Maybe at times some mis-mothering will take place at the gate as the flow of the mob thickens. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,86</i>)

>  <b>MACINTOSH</b> We preferred to bring our ewes and lambs in paddock by paddock to avoid mismothering. <i>(1974,Never a Dull Moment,111</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> The mobs were too large, the mismothering was very bad and shifting lambs to other paddocks at a very early age was causing further mismothering. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,60</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The whole aim in mustering ewes and lambs is to avoid mis-mothering. <i>(1996,Boss's Story, The,140</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> ... any undue disturbance of the [Merino] ewes at this time will result in much mis-mothering of lambs, many of which will subsequently die or survive as `poddies' and `weeds'; ... <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,94</i>)

>  <b>COOP</b> On fat lamb farms ... the shearing muster cannot be considered a muster at all and there should be no check on mismothering at the December shearing. <i>(1950,Shearing Ewes before Lambing,20</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> If sight were the only method by which lambs can identify their mothers, or the chief method, mis-mothering after shearing would be a far more serious problem than it is now. <i>(Jan 2, 1951,Shepherd's Calendar,9,1961 ed.</i>)

### Miss Adams
 
 <i>n.</i> Fanny Adams

>  `Six hearts - no bid. Who'll back the Dinky Di, come on, all you lucky players ... Well, up they come, and it's Charlie pays Jim and the old man gets Miss Adams.' <i>(1945,Sixes & Sevens,16</i>)

### miss-hole
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Witness believed that shot-firers should be appointed, whose duties would be to fence off the scene of miss-holes, thus minimising the risk of accident. <i>(Sep 15, 1911,Maoriland Worker,28/6/2</i>)

### Mission House
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MATHEW</b> The River is here about a mile wide, and the [Wesleyan] Mission House and Chapel are built on elevated ground ... back from the Shore. <i>(Feb 16, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Feb 16 F.Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 50]

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> Two quaint bridges connect the town; and the neat little whares of the natives, with the mission-house, church, parsonage, and schools, contribute to render the place a little paradise. <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,27</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> The whaling establishment [at Waikouaiti] was of course separate and distinct, and employed perhaps thirty men. No vestige whatever of this remains save the broken-down fence and overgrown graves of the little cemetery, which was close by Mr. Watkin's mission house. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,107</i>)

### mission station
 
 <i>n.</i> [2 cits below belong with missionary station] (See [station](spa-sys.md#station))

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Formerly the mission-station was on this island [Mokoia], and many of the shrubs which were planted in the garden of the station are still remaining. <i>Ibid.</i> Near this tree, they [Rotorua Maori] said, their forefathers killed the last moa. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/393</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> Waianiwaniwa.. Is a fine fall of the waters of the Kerikeri, about two miles from the Mission Station. [Church Mission Station is often abbreviated to C.M.S., which at this time is usually the acronym for Church Missionary Society] <i>(1835,Account of NZ, An,11</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> [<i>station</i> always means `mission station' <i>(1840,Present State of NZ,18</i>)

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> I therefore went on to Tawranga, which is at present the last mission-station to the southward, and from thence eventually started on my voyage of discovery into the unknown regions of New Zealand. <i>(1941,Rambles in NZ,29,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> A small commercial community had thus grown up around the missionary station, dependent entirely upon trading, ... <i>(1842,New Zealand (various authors/titles),6</i>)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> ..., we went on over very swampy land to Otawao - a Mission-station near the Waipa river. <i>(Dec 26, 1842,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Dec 26 Selwyn Journal Letter in 1847 NZ Part I (The Church in the Colonies) 92]

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> At the Rev. Mr. Schnackenberg's mission station (Te Mahoe, inside Mokau Heads). <i>(Apr 30, 1845,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1845 Apr 30 Maclean Diary in 1940 Cowan Sir Donald Maclean 26]

>  We succeeded in hiring a small canoe ... to take us up the rivers Waikato and Waipa as far as ... [sic] Mr Buttle's missionary station. <i>(Feb 23, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/84</i>) [NOTES: (H.R. Richmond)]

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> ... it is a rare occurrence to travel for a whole day without coming to a native settlement. Now and then, too, a solitary European squatter may be met with; and, more frequently still, a Mission station. <i>(1853,Auckland,126</i>)

>  <b>BUDDLE</b> Was the note of alarm that sounded from the mission station at Waipa a false alarm? <i>(1860,Maori King Movement,26</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> We ... had to sleep in a hut at the old mission-station of Wairoa ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,11</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> As we approached the Mission station, we rejoiced to see fenced fields with cattle grazing, white houses embowered in trees, and beyond a church. <i>(1884,Our Maoris,30</i>)

>  It seems to be fairly well established that the low lands ... extending up towards the old mission-station of Kaitaia, were at no very distant period covered with salt water ... <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/566</i>) [NOTES: (Maxwell)]

>  <b>ECCLES & REED</b> It would be safe to say that no shore-whaler had ever before thought of such a crazy idea, and the thought of the masterful man, so free with his fists, setting up a mission station, must have caused many tongues to wag. <i>(1949,John Jones of Otago,59</i>)

### mission-boy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> The mission-boys were very attentive to their prayers and hymns every evening and morning, and commenced them always without my reminding them, which I had been desired to do by their masters in case of their omission. <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,43,1952 ed.</i>)

### mission/missionary farm
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an early European New Zealand farm, run by missionary farmers.

>  <b>ALLEY & HALL</b> The missionary farms at Kerikeri and Waimate were the first real European farms. <i>(1941,Farmer in New Zealand, The,5</i>)

>  <b>WASWO</b> Cattle were the most important livestock on early mission farms. Mission farming was, however, never completely successful. <i>(1996,Farming Progress in NZ 1814-1995,2</i>)

>  <b>WASWO</b> The initial success of this pioneer mission farm was mainly [due] to the energy, enterprise and expertise of the missionary farmer. <i>(1996,Farming Progress in NZ 1814-1995,4</i>)

### missionary
 
 <i>adj.</i> (Protestant) Christian

>  <b>STEPHENS</b> The Missionary natives, as they are called <i>(1943,Letters,145</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> In some of the missionary Pas there is a little more pretension [among the unmarried girls] to prudery and reserve, but it goes little beyond ... the demand for a little more `utu' (payment) as a salve to their consciences. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,63</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The people at this settlement being `missionary' natives, a bell was struck. <i>(1850,Savage Life,1/242</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> implying pro-Maori?

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> At his [Fitzroy's] first visit to Cook's Strait, he took advantage of his position to make insulting public attacks on gentlemen who had opposed the Missionary views, and the preceding acts of Local Government. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,75</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a (usually Protestant) Christian Maori (contrast PIKOPO, a Catholic Christian; and DEVIL) spg also mihorari--> (?a mistranscription or misprin a re-borrowing of Maori <i>mihinare</i> `missionary' (in modern Maori also `Anglican': Williams, appendix) originally a maoricisation of <i>missionary.</i> a Maori Christian convert spg mihinare--> mitanere-->, mitonare

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> I certainly have never been able to discover any great difference in the conduct of the converted and the unconverted - if any difference really exists, it seems by general consent to be given in favor of the latter - indeed, the word Missionaries is used by the Europeans here only to distinguish those rogues, for whose superior rascallity we have no other sufficiently comprehensive term in our language. The fact is, they are all whether converted or unconverted, radically bad - and it is my own opinion as well as that of every one else whom I have conversed with on the subject, that no permanent good can be done among them until they are placed under a power, which shall be at once their terror, their protection, and their model. <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal,35,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... others [sc. Maoris] again said that they had turned `Mitanari', ... and that the white Mitanaris said the `Pukapuka' would be strong against the heathen. <i>(Feb 20, 1841,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Feb 20 E.J.Wakefield Journal in 1842 Heaphy NZ 58]

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> It was to the effect, that [54] should he turn `mitonere' his father would return and see him. <i>(1842,New Zealand (various authors/titles),53</i>)

>  [The natives] had all evidently became `Missionaries' since I last saw them. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/23/292 [The natives] call themselves missionaries, having embraced Christianity <i>(1840,NZ Journal,1/16/199</i>)

>  <b>Markham</b> ... the Native Settlements become thicker. They are all Missionaries as they call the Christians ... Ibid. 54 I heard they were Missionaries and went up to them; ... Thirty Tattooed earnest Faces, reading and singing by the Fire with their Muskets across their Breasts, .... <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),41,1963 ed.</i>)

>  Report says that he was a great warrior but he has now become Missionary <i>(Aug 1840,Journal of Ensign Best,233,1966 ed.</i>)

>  I asked [Raupara] if he had given up fighting intrigues and become a missionary (the common expression). <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/32/87</i>)

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> [Christian Maoris refer to themselves as `missionaries', heathens are called `devils'] <i>Ibid.</i> (1952) 44 It sounds rather curious to hear a native, in answer to a question as to whether he is a missionary or not, reply quite coolly, `No, I'm a devil!' <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,20 et passim</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> [The Maoris] refused us food, saying they had become missionaries of late. <i>(1843,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/326</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The natives here have all embraced Christianity, and style themselves `mihorari', or missionary people. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/242 At sunset, the people of this settlement being `missionary' natives, a bell was struck, and they then went through their evening devotions, ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/241</i>)

>  <b>MALONE</b> Mihaneri (missionaries, the universal name, in New Zealand, for Protestant Christians.) <i>(1854,Three Years Cruise,22</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the sweet briar [get quot Kentall or Marsden]

>  <b>LARKWORTHY</b> sweet briar, which [in Tarawera] goes by the name of `Missionary' blocking the roads and vacant spaces. <i>(1881,New Zealand Revisited,30</i>)

>  <b>REEVES</b> Sweet briar .... covers whole hillsides to the ruin of pasture. Introduced, innocently enough by the missionaries, it goes by their name in some districts [eg north North Island] <i>(1898,Long White Cloud, The,17</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> `Missionary', in the North Island is frequently an alternative spelling for `sweet-brier', which is a pest. As a matter of more justice, therefore, I am glad to take this opportunity of pointing out that it can also spell `peaches', which are not. <i>(1912,Brown Bread,48</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Sweet-briar (<i>Rosa rubiginosa</i>), `Missionary' as it is still called, has been spread abroad by the horse. ... As its local name - `Missionary' - implies, sweet-briar too is a child of the Church of England. <i>(1926,Tutira,274</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> steady, careful

>  <b>CAMERON</b> A broken-down crock that will go along at a missionary pace. <i>(1899,In Fair New Zealand,26</i>)

### missionary farmer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farmer working land on an early mission property.

>  <b>WASWO</b> The initial success of this pioneer mission farm was mainly [due] to the energy, enterprise and expertise of the missionary farmer. <i>(1996,Farming Progress in NZ 1814-1995,4</i>)

### missionary settler
 
 <i>n.</i> a settler doing missionary work among Maori

>  .. Sheperd James, Wangaroa, missionary settler, landed estate ... <i>(Mar 6, 1857,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  There were differences amongst Marsden's missionary settlers, which Mr. Leigh assisted in settling by mutual explanation and prayer. <i>(Jun 5, 1915,NZ Truth,5</i>)

>  <b>KENDALL</b> He had asserted before that if Governor Macquarie would not give him free pardon he would by his influence with the natives drive every missionary settler back to Port Jackson. <i>(Nov 12, 1816,Marsden's Lieutenants,132,1934 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1816 Nov 12 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 132]

>  <b>KENDALL</b> I am certain you are aware of the extreme difficulty of withstanding their incessant importunities, having yourself witnessed them at the doors of the missionary settlers; ... <i>(Sep 1821,Marsden's Lieutenants,174</i>) [NOTES: 1821 Sep 27 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 174]

>  <b>KENDALL</b> I am certain you are aware of the extreme difficulty of withstanding their incessant importunities, having yourself witnessed them at the doors of the missionary settlers; ... <i>(Sep 27, 1821,Marsden's Lieutenants,174</i>) [NOTES: 1821 Sep 27 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 174]

### missionary station
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. mission station](mar-moa.md#mission-station))

>  <b>MACKIE</b> On shore we discern the smoke from a fire of the natives off Otaki, a missionary station. <i>(Jan 28, 1853,Traveller under Concern,71,1973 ed.</i>)

### missionary tree
 
 <i>n.</i> [fide M.M.Small p.c. R. Mason (1955) A shrub growing about Hokianga Harbour. Identity uncertain.]

### missionary weed
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Verbena officinalis</i>; vervain [19? R. Mason quoting Hilgendorf]

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> VERVAIN (<i>Verbena officinalis</i>), also called blue top, griffin weed, and missionary weed, is a slender tough-stemmed perenial 1 to 2 ft. high, with a few opposite rough, cleft leaves, and small lilac flowers, ... It is becoming very common in the country from Wairoa to Gisborne [and is also called recorded from Christchurch]. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,146,1967 ed.</i>)

### Missus
 
 <i>n.</i> constr often THE MISSUS, as a frequent contemporary male familiarity (cf THE WIFE, THE OLD WOMAN) as an often now un-contempuous reference used as much (or more) by New Zealanders as by other English speakers. In earlier days used by employees, especially on stations or farms, as a form of polite reference or address to the `boss's' wife.

>  <b>JACKSON</b> If only my Mrs. could see me now. <i>(1943,Passage to Tobruk,19</i>)

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> If it's not the pakeha coot from up the road, it's the wife's brother ... or the missus. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,1(et passim)</i>)

>  Now I'm a shade on the bashful side myself, as men go, so you'll have to build me up ahead to the Mrs and any stray women folk there may be the station, a man always wants a `knock down' to hop off with when he's breaking a bit of fresh ground with the women, it ain't like city life ... <i>(May 6, 1911,NZ Truth,306/4/6</i>)

>  <b>CHERRILL</b> `The Missis' <i>(1950,Story of a NZ Sheepfarm,166 et pass</i>)

### mist
 
 <i>n.</i> the film or `bloom' on apples, plums etc.

### mist flower
 
 <i>n.</i> =Mexican devil

>  <b>HILGENDORF & CALDER</b> MIST FLOWER (<i>Eupatorium adenophorum</i>). This is one of the few Central American weeds which have become established in New Zealnd. It is widely distributed in the Auckland district. <i>(1967,Weeds of New Zealand,225</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1926]]

>  <i>mist flower [=] Mexican devil</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,49</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> MIST FLOWER (<i>Eupatorium riparium</i>) Perennial, obviously related to Mexican devil ... is locally common in North Auckland. <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,97</i>)

### Mister
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McKENZIE</b> <i>Mister </i>form of address between officers <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,97</i>)

### misties
 
 <i>n.</i> morphine sulphate tablets

>  [Homebake] was replaced in the 1990s by "misties", morphine sulphate tablets, heavy duty painkillers bought or stolen from cancer patients and traded on the black market... While the measured quantities of opiates in misties gave the users an idea of how much was too much, pure heroin is dangerous even for seasoned users. <i>(May 5, 2001,Evening Post,2</i>)

### mistletoe
 
 <i>n.</i> Any of several genera of parasitic shrubs belonging to the plant families Loranthaceae and Viscaceae; esp. Ileostylus micranthus (formerly Loranthus micranthus) with greenish-yellow flowers, Peraxilla spp. (formerly Elytranthe) with scarlet flowers, parasitic shrubs of southern beeches (Nothofagus spp.), and Tupeia antarctica (see also pirita 2) (all fam. Loranthaceae).

>  While <i>Ileostylus micranthus</i> can be locally abundant it, too, is declining in some parts of New Zealand. <i>(Jul 2005,Open Space,No 64</i>)

>  Types of mistletoes: Beech; <i>Alepis falvida</i> Yellow mistletoe <i>Peraxilla colensoi</i> Scarlet mistletoe <i>Peraxilla tetrapetala</i> Red mistletoe Extinct; <i>Trilepetala adamsii</i> Adam's mistletoe Green; <i>Ileostylus micranthus</i> Small flowered mistletoe <i>Tupeia antartica,</i> White mistletoe Dwarf; <i>Korthalsella clavata</i> Leafless mistletoe <i>Korthalsella lindsayi</i> Leafless mistletoe <i>Korthalsella salicornioides</i> Leafless mistletoe. All are endemic to New Zealand except for <i>Ileostylus micranthus</i>, which also occurs on Norfolk Island. <i>(Jul 2005,Open Space,No 64</i>)

>  Auckland has lost 70 per cent of its mistletoe population. <i>(Jan 11, 2007,NZ Herald,A7</i>)

>  <b>JENNINGS</b> The misteltoe (scarlet) was lovely - on the sides of the road & just within reach in full flower. <i>(1880,Diary of an Expedition to the Table Land,4</i>)

>  New Zealand mistletoes are an endangered species. The nine native species, semi-parasitic plants which grow on native and exotic trees, have been declining in numbers since early last century, due mainly to possum damage and land clearance. <i>(Nov 29, 2001,Contact (Wgtn),17</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> At a certain season ... the beech trees are brilliant with bunches of flaming Mistletoe, and then might be mistaken for the crimson Rata of certain regions. [prob. Elytrante] <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,212</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>NOTHOFAGUS MENZIESII, silver beech. </i>A hardy tree, growing from sea level to timber line and often forming pure forests, especially in Nelson and Southland. ...Some stems become malformed through infestations of mistletoe, <i>Elytranthe colensoi,</i> and it is the only known host for our three species of <i>Cyttaria</i>, strawberry fungi which cause large galls. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,80</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> More brilliant splashes of colour ... may be provided by certain mistletoes which commonly parasite the beeches. <i>Peraxilla colensoi</i>, usually found on silver beech, and <i>P. tetrapetala</i> have bright red flowers. <i>Alepis flavida</i> with orange-yellow to yellow flowers used to be common, but has now been depleted by opossums. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,123</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> On one tree saw a fine bunch of a species of Mistletoe with white berries. [prob. Tupaeia] <i>(May 4, 1853,Traveller under Concern,105,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kohuorangi, ... 3. Tupeia antarctica mistletoe</i>. [so 1957/126] <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,148</i>)

>  <i>Tupeia antarctica</i> <i>(1907,AJHR,C-8</i>) [NOTES: Cockayne Report on Botanical Survey of Kapiti Island]
