>  By using a small `m' for `moahunters' Dr Cumberland skilfully avoids entanglement in the current controversy among New Zealand prehistorians over the respective claims of [97] `Moa-hunter Maori' or `Archaic East Polynesian' to designate the early phase. <i>(Apr 1963,NZ Geographer,96</i>) [NOTES: (Duff)]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> ... the evidence seems to indicate that the bow and arrow may have been known to our early Moa-hunter people and later rejected by the Fleet immigrants. <i>(1966,Maori Life and Custom,105</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> THE [AUTHOR'S] USE OF THE TERM MOA-HUNTER [heading] [XII] ... Because this hunting economy and imported artifact styles coincided with the then unknown period of the extermination of the Moa in particular, Skinner, Duff and Buck revived the term Moa-hunter Maori to distinguish the first phase of ecological adaptation from the climax Classic Maori phase of the eighteenth century, with such new elements as agriculture, the fortified village, warfare, and artifact styles evolved within New Zealand. [XII] The term Moa-hunter follows the practice in culture-phase designation of coining a label of contrast. Although Moa-hunter Maoris gathered shell-fish and hunted all available birds, fish, seals and small whales, the cultural and economic phase is conveniently designated from the Moa as the largest and most spectacular game animal available during man's first adaptation to the environment and the one no longer available in the Classic Maori era. The term Moa-hunter is preferred here to the longer `Archaic Phase of New Zealand East Polynesian', too easily corrupted to `Archaic Maori', with `Archaic' meaning only `early'. <i>(1977,Moa-Hunters of NZ,xi-xii</i>)

>  <b>BEGG</b> The first of these [invasions] brought the Katimamoe across the Strait to push the nomadic Moahunters to the south. <i>(1979,World of John Boultbee, The,173</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> ... the remarkable Moahunter encampment at the mouth of the Rakaia [was] investigated. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,168</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> [MOA HUNTER CULTURE] <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,168</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> The MOA-HUNTER PERIOD OF MAORI CULTURE [title] <i>(1977,Moa-Hunters of NZ</i>)

>  Sir Julius von Haast distinguished above the marine sands two series of beds. The lower he called moa-hunter beds. <i>(Oct 28, 1924,Otago Witness,6</i>)

### moa hunting
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Anderson].. notes that most of the moa-hunting activities occurred south of Cook Strait... He concludes that, although some coastal sites are immense and must represent the slaughter of many thousands of moa, "moa-hunting was generally a temporary, perhaps seasonal, activity" which seldom provided more than a quarter to a half of the moa-hunters' total sustenance... His broad conclusion is that moa-hunting occurred between the 11th and 17th centuries, but peaked early in the 123th century in most areas. <i>(Jan 21, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),80</i>)

>  <b>WHITE</b> Moa-hunting might afford good sport, and their joints give variety to the butchers' shops; ... <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,109</i>)

>  ... a push by conquering tribes in the North or the call of adventure - greenstone and moa-hunting - would force or attract from the North to the South. <i>(Mar 22, 1933,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/73</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  The importance of this essay is the convincing case presented for the wholesale devastation of forest as early as the thirteenth century, ... when the pivot of the early fishing and fowling economy appears to have been moa-hunting and, in the reviewer's opinion, justifying its designation as a Moa-hunter phase of Maori culture. <i>(Apr 1963,NZ Geographer,95</i>) [NOTES: (Duff)]

### moa skeleton
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I went into those caves in the hope of meeting with a rich harvest of Moa skeletons, but I was sadly disappointed. <i>(Jul 8, 1859,Auckland Provincial Gazette,VIII (14) : 93</i>)

### moa stone
 
 <i>n.</i> (usually pl.) a round stone, one of many found in groups or masses, and reputed to be a moa gizzard or crop stone.

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> Besides .bones and eggs, little heaps of small rounded stones are very frequently found, generally chalcedony, carnelions [sic], opals and achates [sic]..... which are designated by the natives as "Moa stones". <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),183</i>)

>  <b>TANCRED</b> There are found on parts of the plains little heaps of rounded agate or quartz pebbles (far distant from any rocks of that nature) which are popularly called `moa stones', and are supposed to be the contents of the gizzards of those birds which have died at the places where these heaps are found. <i>(1856,Natural History of Canterbury,13</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> I do not remember finding a single sandstone specimen of a [139] moa gizzard stone. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,ix/138</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> The testimony that Moa bones have been found lying loose amongst the grass on the shingle of the [Canterbury] plains, together with small heaps of so-called Moa stones where probably a bird died & decayed, is too strong to be set aside altogether,....<i>Ibid.</i> 72 In the caves of Collingwood, all the moa stones are derived from the quartz ranges close by. <i>(1872,TrNZI,4/71</i>)

>  <b>8/85</b> These little quartz pebbles I take to be `crop-stones', swallowed by the Moa to aid digestion, although they are not as smooth as what I have seen exhibited in our own Museum for `Moa Stones;'... <i>(1876,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: (Thorne)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> With many of these buried skeletons are found little heaps of crop-stones ... I have in my possession a very interesting collection of these `gizzard-stones', consisting of quartz-pebbles, carnelians, and [xxix] pieces of chert, all worn and polished by attrition ... [fn] Mr. Frederick Chapman (to whom I am indebted for these `Moa-stones') writes ... <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/xxviii-xxix</i>)

>  To aid in the digestion of this food, the moa, like many other birds, swallowed little pebbles, which, rounded & polished by the fricton of their stomach, assumed a peculiar form, and are called to this day `moa-stones' by the natives, who are familiar with them. [Hochstetter, 186]. <i>(1893,TrNZI,25/27</i>) [NOTES: (De Guatrefages, trans L. Buller)]

>  [Australian] Settlers called them [sc. australites] `gum-stones,' as we in New Zealand say `moa-stones.' (Hardcastle) <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/65</i>)

>  <b>McNEISH</b> Polson rummaged in a bag and brought out some tiny pebbles. `And these,' he went on, `are moa stones.' As he rolled them on the table, the surface radiated with a chameleon intensity. <i>(1970,Mackenzie,29</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> These bones were not, as one would expect them to be, near the moa stones, but scattered generally over the run. <i>(1985,Coldstream,xiii</i>)

### moa swamp
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DUFF</b> Little further field activity could be recorded other than continued work in the Pyramid Valley moa-swamp and the demonstration that one of the last of a colony of anomalously surviving `bush' moas (<i>Megalapteryx</i>) had been killed in the Fiordland hinterland of Lake Te Anau, by a Maori fowling party of the eighteenth century. <i>(1977,Moa-Hunters of NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1977 Duff Moa-Hunter Period XI]

### moa theory
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DAWSON</b> We come now to the `moa theory' elaborated by Greenwood and Atkinson, which has found favour with some but disbelief from others. ... Essentially, Greenwood and Atkinson propose that the divaricating shrub habit is probably unique to New Zealand and results from an environmental factor that was also unique ..., namely the moas. [Divaricate habit prevented moa browsing.] <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,144</i>)

### Moa's Ark
 
 <i>n.</i> NZ version of Noah's Ark

>  Moa's Ark. Adding a Kiwi kink to a traditional tale isn't new . . . and Peter Millet reprises the idea in style with rhyming storybook <i>Moa's Ark</i> (Reed, pb, $14.99). <i>(Nov 2005,North & South,122</i>)

### moa's egg
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Bouquets of barbed wire to:... Canterbury Museum: For trying to create a replica of one of the few remaining moa's eggs in the world. <i>(Jan 7, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

### moa-grey
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Three moa-grey professors in a row/ Most ably represent the status quo. <i>(1979,Collected Poems,292</i>) [NOTES: 1964 Baxter `A Little Letter to Auckland Students' Collected Poems (1979) 292]

### moa-hunting
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Proceeding now to the traces left by the moa-hunting population ... <i>(1872,TrNZI,4/78</i>) [NOTES: (Haast Moas and Moa Hunters)]

>  <b>HAAST</b> This old dwelling place of the Moa hunting population ... covered an area of more than twenty acres ... <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,419</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The greenstone and the moa-hunting expeditions would halt wherever food was available ... <i>(1948,Down the Years in the Maniototo,5</i>)

>  <b>BUICK</b> Consistent with this spirit of precaution, might it not ... have been considered indiscreet to become communicative regarding their Moa-hunting exploits? <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,231</i>)

### moa-like
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  A cast of the egg of the Moa-like bird of Madagascar (<i>AEpyornis</i>), ... was ... compared with models of the chief Moa eggs that have been found in New Zealand. <i>(1872,TrNZI,4/363</i>)

### Moabone
 
 <i>n.</i> a place name

>  We'll brave the point of Moabone;/ <i>(1866,Canterbury Rhymes,46</i>) [NOTES: 1856 Dec in 1866 Canterbury Rhymes 46]

### Moaland
 
 <i>n.</i> a nonce-formation on <i>Maoriland</i>, New Zealand.

>  In this colloquial set are the predictable <i>Kiwiland</i>.. and the much rarer <i>Moaland</i>, suitable perhaps only for reference to pre-European and even pre-Maori Aotearoa. <i>(Oct 2000,NZWords,4 : 2</i>)

>  On a dirty, windy day Athletic Park is probably the very bleakest spot in Moaland. <i>(Aug 12, 1905,NZ Truth,8/2/7</i>)

>  When the tussle was about to eventuate between the leek-wearers and the men from Moa-land, one of the firm's customers bespoke a column advertisement in which the result of the match was published. <i>(Sep 15, 1906,NZ Truth,65/2/7</i>)

>  <b>STEVENSON</b> Apparently some time during the early part of the 19th century, the continuity of this ancient form of life was finally broken and Moaland became Maoriland. <i>(1947,Maori and Pakeha in North Otago,156</i>)

### moana
 
 <i>n.</i> the sea, a lake

>  <b>FORSTER</b> A Comparative Table of the Various Languages in the Isles of the South-Sea ... [English] <i>The Sea </i>... [New-Zeeland] Moanna [a1-macron grave, a2-macron] <i>(1778,Observations Made During a Voyage,284</i>)

>  sea - mo'na [moana] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,113,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 113]

>  moana The Maori word meaning lake, e.g. Waikaremoana. It is really wrong, therefore, to speak of Lake Waikaremoana, because Waikaremoana already means Waikare lake. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),262</i>)

### Moaning Minnie
 
 <i>n.</i> the light and whistle buoy off Barrett Reef, Wellington Harbour [(p.c. R. Mason)]

>  <i>(Apr 15, 1953,Dominion,7</i>)

### Moari(e)
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [Maori](m-mar.md#maori))

### moata
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a New Zealand-bred cultivar of ryegrass (<i>Lolium multiflorum</i>).

>  Nitrate levels are likely to be higher with vigorous-growing grasses, including short-rotation ryegrasses such as tama, moata and manawa. <i>(Oct 1986,NZ Dairy Exporter,6</i>)

>  He began his renovation programme by undersowing 25% of pastures with a mix of nui and moata ryegrass, wana cocksfoot and pitau white clover. <i>(Jan 4, 1989,NZ Dairy Exporter,11</i>)

>  Annual grasses, such as Tama and Moata, with high metabolisable energy values, are grazed throughout the winter. <i>(Apr 12, 2001,NZ Farmer,31</i>)

>  A trial on the farm has shown Moata and Flanker had superior metabolisable energy (ME) ... <i>(2002,Wool Grower,Autumn 11</i>)

### moatic
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>EVERSLEY</b> (`Address to a Moa in the Dunedin Museum') ... fifty years it took/ To render this a less mo-atic clime. <i>(1899,NZ Voices,11</i>)
